A Theory of Universal Agnostic Learning
\name Steve Hanneke \email steve.hanneke@gmail.com

Abstract
We provide a complete theory of optimal universal rates for binary classification in the agnostic setting. This extends the realizable-case theory of Bousquet, Hanneke, Moran, van Handel, and Yehudayoff (2021) by removing the realizability assumption on the distribution. We identify a fundamental tetrachotomy of optimal rates: for every concept class, the optimal universal rate of convergence of the excess error rate is one of e − n e^{-n} , e − o ⁡ ( n ) e^{-o(n)} , o ( n − 1 / 2 ) o(n^{-1/2}) , or arbitrarily slow. We further identify simple combinatorial structures which determine which of these categories any given concept class falls into.



Keywords:

Statistical Learning Theory, PAC Learning, Agnostic Learning, Sample Complexity, Universal Learning Rates, Learning Curves, Asymptotic Rates

1
Introduction

The past fifty years of research in statistical learning theory has produced a rich and beautifully general theory
of learning, building on the foundational works of

Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
;
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1974
establishing
necessary and sufficient conditions for distribution-free learnability of any given concept class.
In particular, one of the most fundamental
and seminal results in this literature is the
identification of
minimax optimal rates

of convergence of excess risk for binary classification
relative to any given function class.
Specifically, for any set
\mathcal{H}
(called a
concept class
)
of functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,
a learning algorithm is tasked with producing a

predictor

\hat{h}_{n}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

learned from
n
i.i.d. samples from an arbitrary
unknown distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
.
The objective is to obtain a small

error rate

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})=P((x,y):h(x)\neq y)

relative to the best achievable error rate

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
by the class
\mathcal{H}

(known as
agnostic learning
, following terminology introduced by
Kearns et al. 1994
).
A seminal result of
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1974
establishes
that it is possible to design such a learning algorithm
achieving a convergence guarantee
holding
uniformly
in
P

— i.e.,

\sup_{P}\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\to 0
—
if and only if
\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})<\infty
,
where
\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})
is a combinatorial dimension of
\mathcal{H}

known as the
VC dimension

(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
)
.
Moreover, results of
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1974
;
Talagrand 1994
provide a further quantitative guarantee: if
\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})<\infty
,
the
optimal
rate of convergence
of
\sup_{P}\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

is of order
n^{-1/2}
.
This fact identifies a fundamental
dichotomy

of optimal uniform rates:
the optimal rate is
n^{-1/2}
if
\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})<\infty
,
and otherwise it is
\Omega(1)
.

While this result forms a cornerstone of statistical learning theory,
it does not tell the whole story of what makes a learning
algorithm successful.
In reality, any given learning scenario represents just a
single
distribution
P
, and therefore
the
uniform
rate of convergence of

\sup_{P}\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

may represent an overly
pessimistic

bound on the actual rate of convergence of

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

under
P
.
We may therefore be interested in obtaining a
refinement

of the theory of uniform rates,
which better describes the rates of convergence of

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

in the sample size
n
, holding for every
P
,
though without the requirement of uniformity in
P
.

In the present work, we develop such a theory.
Following prior work of
Bousquet et al. 2021
(which studied a more-restrictive setting, discussed below),
we are interested in understanding which rates
R(n)\to 0

are achievable
universally
in
P
: that is,
there is a learning algorithm which guarantees that,
for
every
distribution
P
,

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

converges at an asymptotic rate
R(n)
,
where the asymptotic nature of the guarantee allows for

P
-dependent constant factors (and sometimes other relevant
P
dependences).
For instance, in sharp contrast to the
n^{-1/2}
optimal
uniform
rate,
we will find that some concept classes
admit rates which are
exponentially fast
, i.e.,

\forall P
,
\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq e^{-c_{P}n}
,
for a
P
-dependent constant
P
.
This theory of universal rates notably retains desirable
properties of the classical uniform analysis.
It admits a notion of an
optimal
rate
for any given class
\mathcal{H}
, which can therefore
serve to motivate algorithm design (i.e., the theory can
distinguish between learning algorithms guaranteeing
optimal rates vs suboptimal rates).
As we will show, it also admits simple combinatorial
structures which completely characterize the optimal
universal rates achievable for any given concept class
\mathcal{H}
.

In contrast to the fundamental
dichotomy
of
optimal uniform rates established by the classical theory,
our main contribution in this work is to identify a
fundamental
tetrachotomy
of
optimal
universal
rates.
We show that every concept class
\mathcal{H}
exhibits an optimal
rate that is one of
e^{-n}
,
e^{-o(n)}
,
o(n^{-1/2})
, or arbitrarily slow (precise definitions are given below).
We further provide combinatorial structures which
completely characterize which concept classes
\mathcal{H}

exhibit which of these as its optimal universal rate.
Specifically, we show that the optimal rate is
e^{-n}

iff
|\mathcal{H}|<\infty
,
while the optimal rate is
e^{-o(n)}
iff

|\mathcal{H}|=\infty
and
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite

Littlestone tree
(Definition
4
),
and the optimal rate is
o(n^{-1/2})
iff

\mathcal{H}
shatters an infinite Littlestone tree
but does not shatter an infinite
VCL tree

(Definition
5
),
and otherwise
\mathcal{H}
requires arbitrarily slow rates.

Background:

The study of universal rates in learning theory has a long
history, with numerous works establishing universal rate
upper and lower bounds in specialized settings,
under various names, such as
individual rates
,
strong minimax rates
,
true sample complexity
, etc.

(
Ben-David et al. 1995
;
Castelli and Cover 1996
;
Schuurmans 1997
;
Antos and Lugosi 1998
;
Balcan et al. 2010
;
Hanneke 2012
;
Yang and Hanneke 2013
;
Kpotufe and Orabona 2013
, e.g.,)
,
and of course, notions of universal asymptotic optimality have a
rich history in theoretical statistics,
particularly for certain families of parametric tests and estimators

(
Fisher 1924
;
Wilks 1938
;
Le Cam 1952
, e.g.,)
.

The first
general
theory of optimal universal rates
for binary classification were presented in the
work of
Bousquet et al. 2021
.
The main difference from the present work
is that
Bousquet et al. 2021
study a
restricted setting, known as the
realizable case
,
which restricts to distributions
P
satisfying
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=0
:
i.e., they assume there are near-perfect functions in
\mathcal{H}
.
The present work represents an extension
of their theory to the agnostic setting.
Under the realizability assumption, they identify a
fundamental
trichotomy
of optimal universal rates,
where every concept class
\mathcal{H}
exhibits an optimal rate
that is either
e^{-n}
,
\frac{1}{n}
, or arbitrarily slow.
They further identify simple combinatorial structures which
completely determine which concept classes
\mathcal{H}
fall into
which of these categories.
Concept classes
\mathcal{H}
which do not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree (Definition
4
)
exhibit optimal rate
e^{-n}
,
while classes which shatter an infinite Littlestone tree
but do not shatter an infinite VCL tree (Definition
5
)
exhibit optimal rate
\frac{1}{n}
,
and otherwise
\mathcal{H}
requires arbitrarily slow rates.
They provide numerous examples of concept classes
illustrating these rates, and distinguishing
optimal universal rates from optimal uniform rates
and other notions from the literature,
such as
non-uniform
rates
(
Benedek and Itai 1994
)
and the
universal Glivenko-Cantelli
property
(
Dudley et al. 1991
;
van Handel 2013
)
.

While the results of
Bousquet et al. 2021

have since been extended to numerous
variations on the original binary classification setting

(
Kalavasis et al. 2022
;
Hanneke et al. 2022
;
Hanneke et al. 2023
;
Bousquet et al. 2023
;
Attias et al. 2024
;
Hanneke et al. 2024
;
Hanneke et al. 2025
)

and analysis of specific algorithms
(
Hanneke and Xu 2024
;
Hanneke and Xu 2025
)
,
the extension of the general theory of optimal rates beyond the realizable case has remained open.
Here we completely resolve this issue, providing a complete
extension of the theory of optimal rates to the agnostic setting
which makes no restrictions on the distribution
P
.

Technical contributions and challenges:

The key technical tool introduced in the work of
Bousquet et al. 2021

is an expression of measurable winning strategies
for certain Gale-Stewart games:
sequential games defined based on the class
\mathcal{H}

which are guaranteed to have a winning strategy
for a particular player when
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an
appropriate tree structure (see Definitions
4
,
5
, and Lemmas
9
,
11
).
They use these winning strategies, in combination with
careful statistical techniques for aggregation and parameter tuning, and learning methods for prediction with
certain data-dependent partial concept classes induced by
the winning strategies, to define the optimal learning methods.
In our present work, we again make use of these winning
strategies as the core of our learning methods.
However, the techniques used to produce the
desired learning rates are significantly more-involved,
due to the fact that the data sequence may be non-realizable.
For many of these aspects, we make use of appropriate concentration inequalities
to replace the simpler statistical aggregation and parameter tuning
techniques of
Bousquet et al. 2021
,
together with a data-relabeling technique
(similar to a technique of
Hopkins et al. 2022
).
However, we face a particularly challenging point,
specific to the agnostic setting, in the portion of the
method making use of data-dependent partial concept classes induced by the winning strategies mentioned above.
This requires a novel analysis of universal learning
rates for partial concept classes of finite VC dimension,
critical to obtaining the claimed
o(n^{-1/2})
rates.
This analysis differs significantly from the analogous
problem for total concept classes,
and this contribution should itself be of independent interest
(Section
9.1
).

2
Summary of the Main Results

We adopt the same basic definitions from the original work of
Bousquet et al. 2021
on universal learning in the realizable case. Let
\mathcal{X}
be a non-empty set (called the
instance space
).
A
concept
is a function
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,
and a
concept class

\mathcal{H}
is a non-empty set of concepts. We adopt the same (standard) measure-theoretic requirements for
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{H}

from the original work of

Bousquet et al. 2021
(Definition 3.3 therein).
1
1

1

Specifically, we require the
image admissible Suslin
property
(
Dudley 2014
, Section 5.3)
: namely, we suppose
\mathcal{X}
is a Polish space, and there exists a Polish space
\Theta
and a Borel-measurable map
h:\Theta\times\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
such that
\mathcal{H}=\{h(\theta,\cdot):\theta\in\Theta\}
. This is a standard
condition relied on by much of the literature on empirical process theory, and holds for most concept classes of practical interest.
Henceforth, any concept class
\mathcal{H}
in this article will be assumed to satisfy this condition; for brevity we omit this qualification from the statements of all results.
A
learning algorithm
is a sequence of universally measurable functions
f_{n}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
In the context of learning,
we will consider a distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
,
and independent
P
-distributed samples

(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
,
and when
P
is clear from the context we denote by

\hat{h}_{n}
the corresponding
trained classifier
,
namely,

\hat{h}_{n}(x):=f_{n}(X_{1},Y_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{n},x)
.
Moreover, to simplify notation,
we will simply refer to
\hat{h}_{n}
itself as a
learning algorithm
,
leaving implicit the dependence of
\hat{h}_{n}

on
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
via the
f_{n}
function.
We also adopt the colloquialism of referring to
\hat{h}_{n}

as the classifier
returned
by the learning algorithm,
and we describe learning algorithms
f_{n}
below as procedures which construct such a classifier
\hat{h}_{n}
.
For any measurable
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,
define the
error rate
(or
risk
)

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h):=P((x,y):h(x)\neq y)
.

The work of
Bousquet et al. 2021
focused on the
case of
realizable
distributions
P
:
namely, those satisfying
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=0
,
studying the rate of convergence of the expected error rate

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}\right)\right]

holding universally over all realizable distributions.
In contrast, in the context of
agnostic learning
,
our interest is in characterizing the optimal rates of
convergence of the expected
excess
error rate

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}\right)\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

holding universally over
all
distribution
P
(without realizability restrictions).
We extend the definition of achievable rates from
the work of
Bousquet et al. 2021
, both to apply
in the context of agnostic learning,
and to allow for
asymptotic behaviors not captured by that work
(namely, rates that are
arbitrarily close
to a given rate).
Importantly, in the following definitions,
note that the order of quantifiers allows for the constants
C,c
to be
P
-dependent (which is what distinguishes universal rates from uniform rates).

•

\mathcal{H}
is
agnostically learnable at rate
\bm{R}
if there is a learning
algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
such that for every distribution
P
,
there exist
C,c>0
for which

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq CR(cn)
for all
n
.

•

\mathcal{H}
is
not agnostically learnable faster than
\bm{R}
if there exist
C,c>0
such that,
for every learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
, there exists a
distribution
P
for which

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\geq CR(cn)
for infinitely many
n
.

•

\mathcal{H}
is
agnostically learnable with optimal rate
\bm{R}
if

\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable at rate
R
and

\mathcal{H}
is not agnostically learnable faster than
R
.

•

\mathcal{H}
requires
arbitrarily slow rates
for agnostic learning if there is a learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
such that, for every distribution
P
,
\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq 0
,
but for every
R(n)\to 0
,
\mathcal{H}
is not agnostically learnable faster than
R
.

In addition to the definition of rates above, we will need another type of rate definition, capturing
super-root
, and
near-exponential
, as follows.

•

(
super-root rate
)
\mathcal{H}
is
agnostically learnable with optimal rate exactly

\bm{o(n^{-1/2})}

if there is a learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
such that, for every distribution
P
,

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=o(n^{-1/2})
,
but for every
R(n)=o(n^{-1/2})
,

\mathcal{H}
is not agnostically learnable at rate
R
.

•

(
near-exponential rate
)
\mathcal{H}
is
agnostically learnable with optimal rate exactly

\bm{e^{-o(n)}}

if for every
\psi(n)=o(n)
,

\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable at rate
R(n)=e^{-\psi(n)}
,
and for every learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
,

\exists\psi(n)=o(n)

and a distribution
P

such that

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\geq e^{-\psi(n)}

for infinitely many
n
.

The main result of this work is a fundamental
trichotomy
of possible optimal rates, applicable to any
infinite
concept class (finite classes will be addressed below, by a vastly simpler analysis).

Theorem 1

For every infinite concept class
\mathcal{H}
,
exactly one of the following holds.

•

\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable with optimal rate exactly
e^{-o(n)}
.

•

\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable with optimal rate exactly
o(n^{-1/2})
.

•

\mathcal{H}
requires arbitrarily slow rates for agnostic learning.

The fact that both of the rate guarantees above are
near
rates is an interesting distinction, both from the realizable case (where the corresponding rates are
e^{-n}
and
\frac{1}{n}
exactly) and from uniform analysis (where there is a dichotomy between
n^{-1/2}
rate and non-learnable classes).

Interestingly, finite concept classes do not fit into this trichotomy.
For completeness, we also prove the following (rather obvious) observation: finite classes always have optimal rate
e^{-n}
(proven in Section
4
via Theorems
6
and
7
).
Technically, this means we have in fact established a
tetrachotomy
of possible optimal rates
(excluding the trivial case
|\mathcal{H}|=1
, which clearly has optimal rate
R(n)=0
).

Theorem 2

For any
finite
concept class
\mathcal{H}
with
|\mathcal{H}|\geq 2
,

\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable with optimal rate
e^{-n}
.

For infinite concept classes,
we also completely characterize which classes fall into which categories. We note that these criteria are precisely the same as those establishing the trichotomy of possible rates for the realizable case
(
Bousquet et al. 2021
)
.
Formal definitions of the relevant structures — Littlestone trees and VCL trees — are given in the next section.

Theorem 3

For every infinite concept class
\mathcal{H}
,
the following hold:

•

If
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree,
then
\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable with optimal rate exactly
e^{-o(n)}
.

•

If
\mathcal{H}
shatters an infinite Littlestone tree but does not shatter an infinite
VCL tree, then
\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable with optimal rate exactly
o(n^{-1/2})
.

•

If
\mathcal{H}
shatters an infinite VCL tree, then
\mathcal{H}
requires arbitrarily slow rates for agnostic learning.

Two comparisons are in order.
First, we should compare to the
realizable case

analysis of
Bousquet et al. 2021
.
In the realizable case, classes with no infinite Littlestone
tree have optimal rate
e^{-n}
(compare to
e^{-o(n)}
in Thereom
3
),
while classes with infinite Littlestone tree and no infinite VCL tree have optimal rate
\frac{1}{n}
(compare to
o(n^{-1/2})
in Theorem
3
).
Second, we should compare to the
uniform
rates
of agnostic learning. Results of
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1974
and
Talagrand 1994
reveal the optimal uniform rates
are
n^{-1/2}
for classes of finite
VC dimension
,
and do not converge to
0
for classes of infinite VC dimension.

Bousquet et al. 2021
provide a number of examples of
concept classes with infinite VC dimension, but for which
there is no infinite Littlestone tree, or having an infinite Littlestone tree but no infinite VCL tree.
We repeat some such examples in Section
3

for completeness.

The proof of Theorem
3
is presented in several parts below.
The last item in Theorem
3

follows from known results.
Specifically, the fact that every class
\mathcal{H}

is learnable with (at most) arbitrarily slow rates
follows from the existence of
universally Bayes-consistent learning algorithms
(see Lemma
14
below).
Moreover, the fact that classes with an infinite VCL tree
require arbitrarily slow rates follows immediately
from the fact that this is true even in the realizable case,
as shown by
Bousquet et al. 2021
.
Thus, our proofs below will focus on establishing the
other items in Theorem
3
.
These claims will be established in the sections below by
Theorems
19
and
22

(establishing the first item, concerning
e^{-o(n)}
optimal rates)
and
Theorems
23
and
24

(establishing the second item, concerning
o(n^{-1/2})
optimal rates).

3
Additional Definitions and Notation

For a distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
,
we denote by
P_{X}
the marginal distribution of
P
on
\mathcal{X}
.
For a sequence
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
,
we denote by
x_{\leq n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}

and
x_{<n}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1}\}
.
For another sequence
y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
,
we may write

(x_{\leq n},y_{\leq n})=\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n},y_{n})\}

and
(x_{<n},y_{<n})=\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n-1},y_{n-1})\}
.
For any set
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, denote by
\hat{P}_{x_{\leq n}}(A)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbold{1}[x_{i}\in A]
the empirical measure of
A
.
Also, for functions
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,
we define
h(x_{\leq n})=(h(x_{1}),\ldots,h(x_{n}))
.
In some cases we express a sequence of sequences
(e.g., Definition
5
)

x_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{X}^{k}
for an index
\mathbf{u}
,
in which case we index the elements of
x_{\mathbf{u}}

by superscripts, i.e.,
x_{\mathbf{u}}=\{x_{\mathbf{u}}^{1},\ldots,x_{\mathbf{u}}^{k}\}
.
For
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
define
\log(x)=\ln(\max\{x,e\})
.
Also, for
x=0
, define
x\log(1/x)=0

and
\left(\frac{c}{x}\right)^{x}=1
for any
c>0
.

Many of the lemmas and proofs in this article
concern conditional probabilities and expectations.
In all such contexts, to account for such quantities
being defined only up to measure-zero differences,
any claims concerning these
(e.g., inequalities of the type
\mathbf{P}(E|X)\leq\delta
)
are formally interpreted as stating that
there exists a version of the conditional probability
(or expectation) for which the claim holds.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
S=\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n},y_{n})\}\in(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{n}
, for any
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,
define the
empirical risk

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(h)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbold{1}[h(x_{i})\neq y_{i}].

Any such sequence
S
is said to be

realizable
with respect to
\mathcal{H}
if

\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(h)=0
.
Moreover, an infinite sequence
\{(x_{t},y_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}

is said to be realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
if
every finite
prefix

(x_{\leq n},y_{\leq n})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.
A distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
is
said to be realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
if

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=0
.

The following definitions from
Bousquet et al. 2021

supply the combinatorial
structures critical to characterizing the optimal
rates in Theorem
3
.
The first can also be viewed as an infinite variant
of a structure well known to the literature on adversarial
online learning, introduced in the seminal work of

Littlestone 1988
.

Definition 4
(
Bousquet et al. 2021
)

A
Littlestone tree
shattered by
\mathcal{H}
is a perfect binary tree of depth
d\leq\infty
whose internal nodes are labeled by elements of
\mathcal{X}
, and whose two edges connecting a node to its children are labelled
0
and
1
, such that every finite path emanating from the root is consistent with a concept
h\in\mathcal{H}
.
More precisely, a Littlestone tree of depth
d
shattered by
\mathcal{H}
is a collection

\{x_{\mathbf{u}}:0\leq k<d,\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{k}\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}

such that for every
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
and
n<d
,
there exists

h\in\mathcal{H}
such that
h(x_{\mathbf{y}_{\leq k}})=y_{k+1}
for
all
0\leq k\leq n
.
We say
\mathcal{H}
shatters an
infinite Littlestone tree
if there is
a Littlestone tree of depth
d=\infty
shattered by
\mathcal{H}
.

The second structure can be viewed as a combination
of the notion of a Littlestone tree with the notion of
shattering
from the definition of VC dimension
(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
)

appearing in each node (and is therefore referred to as a VC-Littlestone tree, or VCL tree for short).

Definition 5
(
Bousquet et al. 2021
)

A
VCL tree
shattered by
\mathcal{H}
of depth
d\leq\infty
is a collection

\{x_{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{X}^{k+1}:0\leq k<d,\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{1}\times\{0,1\}^{2}\times\cdots\times\{0,1\}^{k}\}

such that for every
n<d
and

\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{1}\times\cdots\times\{0,1\}^{n+1}
, there exists a
concept
h\in\mathcal{H}
such that
h(x_{\mathbf{y}_{\leq k}}^{i})=y_{k+1}^{i}

for all
0\leq i\leq k
and
0\leq k\leq n
, where we denote

\mathbf{y}_{\leq k}=(y_{1}^{0},(y_{2}^{0},y_{2}^{1}),\ldots,(y_{k}^{0},\ldots,y_{k}^{k-1})),\qquad x_{\mathbf{y}_{\leq k}}=(x_{\mathbf{y}_{\leq k}}^{0},\ldots,x_{\mathbf{y}_{\leq k}}^{k}).

We say that
\mathcal{H}
shatters an
infinite VCL tree
if there is
a VCL tree of depth
d=\infty
shattered by
\mathcal{H}
.

We refer the reader to the original work
of
Bousquet et al. 2021

for numerous examples of concept classes illustrating these definitions, and comparing to previously-studied combinatorial dimensions such as VC dimension.
A simple example which does not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree is
threshold
classifiers
\{\mathbbold{1}_{[a,\infty)}:a\in\mathcal{X}\}
on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{N}
,
whereas threshold classifiers on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}

does
shatter an infinite Littlestone tree (but not an infinite VCL tree).
We can also relate these structures to the familiar
VC dimension

(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
)

by noting that any concept class of finite VC dimension cannot shatter an infinite VCL tree.
On the other hand, any class which VC-shatters some
infinite
set
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
necessarily shatters an infinite VCL tree.
However, there is an intermediate category, namely classes which have infinite VC dimension (witnessed by arbitrarily large, but not infinite, shattered sets),
yet which do not shatter an infinite VCL tree.
Indeed, consider the class
\mathcal{H}
of all (coordinate-wise)
non-decreasing

binary functions
\mathbb{N}^{d}\to\{0,1\}

on
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{N}^{d}
.
While this class
\mathcal{H}
has infinite VC dimension,

Bousquet et al. 2021
show it does not even shatter an infinite
Littlestone
tree.

4
Finite Classes

As a warm-up, we present a very simple proof of Theorem
2
, establishing that finite classes with
|\mathcal{H}|\geq 2
have optimal rate
e^{-n}
.
This will be established by the following two theorems, providing the upper and lower bounds, respectively.
The first was also presented in the work of
Hanneke and Xu 2025
, repeated here for completeness.

Theorem 6

Any finite concept class
\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable at rate
e^{-n}
.
Moreover, this is achieved by
empirical risk minimization
:
that is, any learning algorithm
f_{n}
which, for any
S\in(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{n}
,
has the function
x\mapsto f_{n}(S,x)

equal to an element
h_{S}
of
\mathcal{H}
satisfying
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(h_{S})=\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(h)
.

Proof
Let
P
be any distribution,
let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
be i.i.d.
P
random variables,
and let us abbreviate

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(\cdot)=\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})}(\cdot)
.
Let
f_{n}
be a learning algorithm such that
\hat{h}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}

and
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(\hat{h}_{n})=\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h)
:
that is,
\hat{h}_{n}
is an empirical risk minimizer.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is a finite set,
the value
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

is achieved;
let
h^{\star}
be any element of
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.

Define the set

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}=\left\{h\in\mathcal{H}:\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)>\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right\}.

If
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}=\emptyset
,
then since
\hat{h}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
we trivially have

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=0
for all
n
.
To handle the remaining case,
suppose
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}\neq\emptyset
,
and define

\epsilon=\min_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}).

Since
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=0
when
\hat{h}_{n}\notin\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}
,
and since
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq 1
always,
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right)\mathbbold{1}[\hat{h}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}\right).

Since
\hat{h}_{n}
is an empirical risk minimizer,
we always have that

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star})
.
Therefore, if
\hat{h}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}
,
it must be the case that
either

\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}

or

\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(\hat{h}_{n})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{bad}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\max_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h)-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\right|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h)-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\right|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)\leq 2|\mathcal{H}|e^{-(\epsilon^{2}/2)n},

where the final two inequalities follow from the union bound and Hoeffding’s inequality, respectively.

Theorem 7

Any
concept class
\mathcal{H}
with
|\mathcal{H}|\!\geq\!2
is not agnostically learnable faster than
e^{-n}
.

We remark that this theorem is only needed because our
condition “
|\mathcal{H}|\geq 2
” is slightly weaker than the
condition assumed by
Bousquet et al. 2021

to establish a lower bound
e^{-n}
for the realizable case
(explicitly, the classes
\mathcal{H}
for which this result establishes an
e^{-n}
lower bound for agnostic learning, and which do not admit an
e^{-n}
lower bound in the realizable case, are precisely those classes of the form
\mathcal{H}=\{h,1-h\}
).
The proof of Theorem
7
will rely
on the following basic lower bound on the sample complexity
of determining whether a coin is slightly biased toward heads or tails.
See Lemma 5.1 of
Anthony and Bartlett 1999
(from which this lemma immediately follows).

Lemma 8

Let
\gamma\in\left(0,\frac{1}{5}\right)
,
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{8e}\right]
,
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
satisfy

n<\frac{1}{8\gamma^{2}}\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{8\delta}\right).

Let
p_{0}=\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
and
p_{1}=\frac{1}{2}+\gamma
.
Let
t^{*}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\frac{1}{2})

and, conditioned on
t^{*}
,
let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
be conditionally i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p_{t^{*}})
.
Fix any function
\hat{t}_{n}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}

(possibly randomized, independent of
t^{*}
).
Then

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{t}_{n}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\neq t^{*}\right)>\delta.

We are now ready for the proof of Theorem
7
.

Proof of Theorem
7

Let
h_{0},h_{1}
be any two distinct elements of
\mathcal{H}
,
and fix any
x\in\mathcal{X}
with
h_{0}(x)\neq h_{1}(x)

(and without loss of generality, suppose
h_{0}(x)=0=1-h_{1}(x)
).
Define two distributions,
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
,
such that
P_{0}(\{(x,0)\})=P_{1}(\{(x,1)\})=\frac{2}{3}

and
P_{0}(\{(x,1)\})=P_{1}(\{(x,0)\})=\frac{1}{3}
:
that is,
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
both have marginal distribution
on
\mathcal{X}
with support exactly
\{x\}
(i.e., a single point-mass),
and they differ in the conditional distribution of
Y
given
X
,
in that each
t,t^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
have

P_{t}(Y=t^{\prime}|X=x)=\begin{cases}\frac{2}{3},&\text{ if }t^{\prime}=t\\
\frac{1}{3},&\text{ if }t^{\prime}\neq t\end{cases}.

In particular, note that for either
t\in\{0,1\}
,
we have that

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P_{t}}(h)=\mathrm{er}_{P_{t}}(h_{t})=\frac{1}{3}
,
and any
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

with
h(x)\neq t=h_{t}(x)

has
\mathrm{er}_{P_{t}}(h)-\inf_{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P_{t}}(h^{\prime})=\mathrm{er}_{P_{t}}(h)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{t}}(h_{t})=\frac{2}{3}-\frac{1}{3}=\frac{1}{3}
.

Fix any learning algorithm
f_{n}
.
Define a sequence
X_{i}=x
, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
Y_{1}^{(0)},Y_{2}^{(0)},\ldots
be independent
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\frac{1}{3})
random variables,
and
Y_{1}^{(1)},Y_{2}^{(1)},\ldots
be independent
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\frac{2}{3})
random variables.
Let
t^{*}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(\{0,1\})

be independent of all
Y_{i}^{(t)}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in\{0,1\}
.
Note that for
t\in\{0,1\}
,

(X_{1},Y_{1}^{(t)}),(X_{2},Y_{2}^{(t)}),\ldots

are independent
P_{t}
-distributed random variables,
and since
t^{*}
is independent of these,
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

(Y_{1}^{(t^{*})},\ldots,Y_{n}^{(t^{*})})

is conditionally
P_{t^{*}}^{n}
-distributed
given
t^{*}
.

We now apply Lemma
8

with
\gamma=\frac{1}{6}
.
Note that defining

(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}):=(Y_{1}^{(t^{*})},\ldots,Y_{n}^{(t^{*})})

satisfies the definition of the
B_{i}
variables
in Lemma
8
.
Also note that the
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
variables
(being all equal
x
) are invariant to
t^{*}
.
In particular,
defining
\hat{t}_{n}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}):=f_{n}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(t^{*})},x)
in the context of Lemma
8
,
the facts about
P_{t^{*}}
argued above yield that

\displaystyle\hat{t}_{n}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\!\neq\!t^{*}\Leftrightarrow f_{n}\!\left(X_{\leq n},Y^{(t^{*})}_{\leq n}\!,x\right)\!\neq\!h_{t^{*}}(x)\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{er}_{P_{t^{*}}}\!\!\left(f_{n}\!\left(X_{\leq n},Y^{(t^{*})}_{\leq n}\right)\right)\!-\!\mathrm{er}_{P_{t^{*}}}\!(h_{t^{*}})=\frac{1}{3}.

Note that any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 5

satisfies
e^{-n}<\frac{1}{8e}

and
\frac{1}{8}e^{n}>e^{n/2}
,
and moreover this latter inequality implies

\frac{1}{8\gamma^{2}}\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{8e^{-n}}\right)>\frac{36n}{16}>n.

Thus, applying Lemma
8

with
\delta=e^{-n}
implies

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P_{t^{*}}}\!\left(f_{n}\!\left(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(t^{*})}\right)\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{t^{*}}}(h_{t^{*}})=\frac{1}{3}\right)=\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{t}_{n}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})\neq t^{*}\right)>e^{-n}.

By Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{t^{*}}}\!\left(f_{n}\!\left(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(t^{*})}\right)\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{t^{*}}}(h_{t^{*}})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{3}\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P_{t^{*}}}\!\left(f_{n}\!\left(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(t^{*})}\right)\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{t^{*}}}(h_{t^{*}})=\frac{1}{3}\right)>\frac{1}{3}\cdot e^{-n}.

Finally, by the law of total expectation,
the above implies that, for any
n\geq 5
,
there exists
t_{n}\in\{0,1\}
such that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{t_{n}}}\!\left(f_{n}\!\left(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(t_{n})}\right)\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{t_{n}}}(h_{t_{n}})\right]>\frac{1}{3}\cdot e^{-n}.

In particular, by the Pigeonhole principle,
there exists
t\in\{0,1\}

with
t_{n}=t
for infinitely many
n
.
For this
t
,
letting
N\subset\mathbb{N}
denote the set of all

n\geq 5
with
t_{n}=t
,
we conclude that when applying the learning algorithm
f_{n}
under the distribution
P_{t}
,
every
n\in N
satisfies

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{t}}\!\left(f_{n}\!\left(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(t)}\right)\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{t}}(h_{t})\right]>\frac{1}{3}\cdot e^{-n}.

Since
|N|=\infty
, this completes the proof.

Theorem
2
now follows immediately from Theorems
6
and
7
.

5
Supporting Lemmas

Turning now to the more-challenging case of infinite classes,
we begin by introducing a few supporting lemmas.
The first is a key result from the original work of

Bousquet et al. 2021
, which extends Littlestone’s

Standard Optimal Algorithm
(SOA)
(
Littlestone 1988
)

to classes which do not shatter an infinite Littlestone
tree, establishing the existence of an
online

learning algorithm which guarantees a
finite

(not necessarily bounded) number of mistakes
on any sequence
(x_{t},y_{t})
realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.
The result stems from an extension of the definition
of the
Littlestone dimension

(
Littlestone 1988
)

to allow for
infinite ordinal
values of the
Littlestone dimension, leading to measurable
solutions to a corresponding
Gale-Stewart game

(see
Bousquet et al. 2021
for the detailed definitions and a formal proof of this lemma).

Lemma 9
(
Bousquet et al. 2021
)

For any concept class
\mathcal{H}
which does not
shatter an infinite Littlestone tree,
there exists a sequence
\mathrm{SOA}_{t}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{t-1}\times\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

of universally measurable functions such that,
there
does not exist
an infinite sequence

\{(x_{t},y_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}

realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
such that

\forall t\in\mathbb{N},\mathrm{SOA}_{t}(x_{<t},y_{<t},x_{t})\neq y_{t}.

In the context of learning from i.i.d. samples,

Bousquet et al. 2021
apply the
\mathrm{SOA}_{t}
predictor as a core component
in a learning algorithm achieving exponential rates
e^{-n}

in the realizable case.
Specifically, the utility of the
\mathrm{SOA}_{t}
predictor
in that context is based on its property (guaranteed by the above lemma) of being an
eventually perfect
predictor.
In particular, by applying the
\mathrm{SOA}_{t}
predictor as a

conservative online learning algorithm
with a sequence of i.i.d. samples from a realizable distribution,
there exists some finite
b^{*}

such that, with a given constant probability, the predictor
achieves error rate
zero
within the first
b^{*}
examples.
Formally, this yields the following result,
established by
Bousquet et al. 2021
.

Lemma 10
(
Bousquet et al. 2021
)

Let

\mathcal{H}
be any concept class which does not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree.
There exists a universally measurable function

\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}:\bigcup_{b\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{b}\times\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
such that,
for any distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}

which is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
,
for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
there exists
b^{*}_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{N}
such that,

\forall b\geq b^{*}_{\gamma}
,
for
S_{b}\sim P^{b}
,

\mathbf{P}\Big(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}(S_{b}))=0\Big)>\gamma.

Bousquet et al. 2021
also extend these same types of results
to classes which do not shatter an infinite
VCL tree
.
This forms a core component in their algorithm achieving rate
\frac{1}{n}
in the realizable case,
and is likewise a core component in our algorithm achieving
rate
o(n^{-1/2})
in the agnostic case.
In this case, the predictor
\mathrm{SOA}_{t}
is replaced by
a
pattern avoidance
function

\mathrm{SOA}^{\mathrm{VCL}}_{t}
, which predicts label patterns which

do not
match the true labels on
given
k
-tuples of
x
’s.
In the learning algorithm below
(used to establish Theorem
24
below),
the role of such pattern avoidance functions is to perform
a kind of sequential data-dependent
model selection
,
constructing a
partial concept class

for which favorable learning rates are achievable.
The existence of this pattern avoidance function is again based on
reasoning about ordinal values of a VCL-type extension
of the Littlestone dimension, leading to measurable
winning strategies for Gale-Stewart games corresponding
to VCL game trees (see
Bousquet et al. 2021
for the
detailed definitions and a formal proof of this lemma).

Lemma 11
(
Bousquet et al. 2021
)

For any concept class
\mathcal{H}
which does not
shatter an infinite VCL tree,
there exists a sequence
\mathrm{SOA}^{\mathrm{VCL}}_{t}:\times_{k=1}^{t-1}(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{k}\times\mathcal{X}^{t}\to\{0,1\}^{t}

of universally measurable functions such that,
there
does not exist
an infinite sequence
\{S_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}

of finite sequences

S_{t}=\{(x^{t}_{1},y^{t}_{1}),\ldots,(x^{t}_{t},y^{t}_{t})\}

\in(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{t}

such that the sequence
S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup\cdots
is
realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
and

\forall t\in\mathbb{N},\mathrm{SOA}^{\mathrm{VCL}}_{t}(S_{<t},(x^{t}_{1},\ldots,x^{t}_{t}))=(y^{t}_{1},\ldots,y^{t}_{t}).

Based on this
\mathrm{SOA}^{\mathrm{VCL}}_{t}
pattern avoidance function,

Bousquet et al. 2021
prove a variant of
Lemma
10
for the case
of classes which do not shatter an infinite VCL tree,
stated formally as follows.

Lemma 12
(
Bousquet et al. 2021
)

Let
\mathcal{H}
be any concept class which does not shatter an infinite VCL tree.
For every
b\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists a universally measurable function

k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{b}\to\{1,\ldots,b\}
,
and for every
k\in\{1,\ldots,b\}
there exists
a universally measurable function

\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{VCL}}^{b,k}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{b}\times\mathcal{X}^{k}\to\{0,1\}^{k}

such that,
for any distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
which is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
,

\forall\gamma\in(0,1)
,
there exists
b^{*}_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{N}
such that,

\forall b\geq b^{*}_{\gamma}
, for
S_{b}\sim P^{b}
,
for
\mathbf{k}=k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(S_{b})
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(P^{\mathbf{k}}\!\left((x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{\mathbf{k}},y_{\mathbf{k}}):\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{VCL}}^{b,\mathbf{k}}(S_{b},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathbf{k}})=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\mathbf{k}})\right)=0\right)>\gamma.

Remark 13
(A Remark on Measurability)

The learning algorithms we construct below to establish the upper bounds
on achievable rates in Theorem
3
(namely, in the proofs of Theorems
22
and
24
)
apply the universally measurable functions

\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}
,
k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}
, and
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{VCL}}^{b,k}

from Lemmas
10
and
12

in a simple manner, to a finite number of
subsets of the data sequence (plus the test point).
As such, it is a straightforward (though rather tedious)
exercise to verify that these learning algorithms
can be described as a sequence of
universally measurable functions
f_{n}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

(i.e., a universally measurable function mapping
a training set
S_{n}
of size
n
plus a test point
x

to a prediction
f_{n}(S_{n},x)\in\{0,1\}
).
For brevity of presentation, we largely omit the detailed
discussion of this fact from the proofs of these theorems.

In addition to the above functions
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}
,
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{VCL}}
,
another key component of our learning algorithms achieving
the claimed
e^{-o(n)}
and
o(n^{-1/2})
rates is
the use of a
universally Bayes-consistent
subroutine.
In the context of the proofs, such an algorithm is used to
address the case where the distribution
P
is not

Bayes-realizable
: that is,
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
, where
h
ranges over all measurable functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
in the latter infimum.
Such cases allow for an excess error rate

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

which may even be
negative
,
and as such may be considered as rather
easy
cases
in the context of universal rates for agnostic learning.
To address these distributions,
we employ a secondary
learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
as a subroutine,
which guarantees, for all distributions
P
,
that
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]\to\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
,
a property known as universal Bayes-consistency
(
Devroye et al. 1996
, see e.g.,)
.
Such learning algorithms are known to exist

(
Hanneke 2021
;
Hanneke et al. 2021
)

under the conditions on
\mathcal{X}
considered in the present work
(from footnote
1
,
which in particular requires that
\mathcal{X}
be a separable
metric space),
as stated formally in the following lemma.

Lemma 14
(
Hanneke et al. 2021
)

There exists a learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
such that,
for all distributions
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}\right)\right]=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h).

In addition to supplying a key component of our learning algorithms below,
Lemma
14

also substantiates the
claim in Theorems
1
and
3

that every concept class
\mathcal{H}
at least admits
arbitrarily slow rates: that is,
existence of a learning algorithm
with
\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq 0

for all distributions
P
.

Next, we present several lemmas which will be useful
in the proofs of the main results below.
These may be of independent interest,
as they concern general properties of concept classes.
The lemmas concern concept classes
\mathcal{H}

satisfying the
universal Glivenko-Cantelli
(UGC) property, defined as follows
(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
;
Dudley et al. 1991
;
van Handel 2013
)
.

Definition 15

A concept class
\mathcal{H}
is said to satisfy the
universal Glivenko-Cantelli (UGC) property if,
for every distribution
P_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
,
for
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
independent
P_{X}
-distributed random variables,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\left|\hat{P}_{X_{\leq n}}(x:h(x)=1)-P_{X}(x:h(x)=1)\right|\right]=0.

(1)

The first lemma is based on an argument of
Bousquet et al. 2021
, and establishes that the UGC property holds for
any concept class
\mathcal{H}
which does not shatter an infinite VCL tree.

Lemma 16

Any concept class
\mathcal{H}
which does not shatter an infinite VCL tree satisfies the UGC property (Definition
15
).

Proof
Since
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite VCL tree, it follows that there does not exist any infinite subset
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
that is
finitely shattered by
\mathcal{H}

(i.e., such that every
t,i_{1},\ldots,i_{t}\in\mathbb{N}

have
\forall y_{1},\ldots,y_{t}\in\{0,1\}
,

\{(x_{i_{1}},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{i_{t}},y_{t})\}
is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
),
noting that if such an
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
were to exist then we could construct an infinite VCL tree shattered by
\mathcal{H}
by defining
the nodes

x_{\mathbf{u}}\in(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})^{k+1}
,
0\leq k<\infty
,
\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{1}\times\cdots\times\{0,1\}^{k}
,
so that every
x_{\mathbf{u}}^{i}
is distinct over all
\mathbf{u}
and
i
.
It was shown by
Dudley et al. 1991
that, if no such finitely-shattered infinite subset
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
exists,
then
\mathcal{H}
satisfies the UGC property.

For a distribution
P_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
,
we say a concept class
\mathcal{H}
is
totally bounded
in
the
L_{1}(P_{X})
pseudo-metric if,
for every
\epsilon>0
,
there exists a
finite
set
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{H}

that is an
\epsilon
-cover
of
\mathcal{H}
in
L_{1}(P_{X})
:
namely,

\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\min_{\tilde{h}\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}}P_{X}(x:\tilde{h}(x)\neq h(x))\leq\epsilon.

(2)

The following lemma establishes that classes
\mathcal{H}
satisfying the UGC property are always totally bounded in
L_{1}(P_{X})
(the result is rather immediate from known characterizations of UGC, but we include a simple proof for completeness).

Lemma 17

For any concept class
\mathcal{H}
satisfying the UGC property (Definition
15
),
for any probability measure
P_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
,

\mathcal{H}
is totally bounded in
L_{1}(P_{X})
.

Proof
Fix any distribution
P_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
,
and let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
be independent
P_{X}
-distributed random variables.
We first argue that, for any concept class
\mathcal{H}
,
the Glivenko-Cantelli
property stated in (
1
)
implies the same condition for the class

\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}=\{x\mapsto\mathbbold{1}[h(x)\neq h^{\prime}(x)]:h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}\}
of pairwise differences.
For any concept class
\mathcal{H}
,
denote by

\mathcal{H}(X_{\leq n}):=\{(h(X_{1}),\ldots,h(X_{n})):h\in\mathcal{H}\}
the set of
\mathcal{H}
-realizable classifications of
X_{\leq n}
.
First, we recall a classic result of
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
,
which establishes that a concept class
\mathcal{H}
satisfies
(
1
)
if and only if

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{n}\log_{2}(|\mathcal{H}(X_{\leq n})|)\right]=0
.
Second, since each element of

\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(X_{\leq n})
is specified as a
pointwise difference of two elements of
\mathcal{H}(X_{\leq n})
,
we can conclude that

|\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(X_{\leq n})|\leq|\mathcal{H}(X_{\leq n})|^{2}
.
Therefore,
if a concept class
\mathcal{H}
satisfies (
1
),
then

0\leq\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{n}\log_{2}(|\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}(X_{\leq n})|)\right]\leq\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{2}{n}\log_{2}(|\mathcal{H}(X_{\leq n})|)\right]=0,

so that (
1
) also holds
when we replace
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}
.
It follows that, for any concept class
\mathcal{H}

satisfying the UGC property (Definition
15
),
the class
\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}
of pairwise differences also
satisfies the UGC property.

From this, we can argue that any such
\mathcal{H}
must be totally bounded in
L_{1}(P_{X})
, as follows.
For any
\epsilon>0
, the UGC property for
\mathcal{H}_{\Delta}
implies
\exists m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\sup_{h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}\left|P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\prime}(x))-\hat{P}_{X_{\leq m}}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\prime}(x))\right|\right]\leq\epsilon.

In particular, this implies that there exists
S\in\mathcal{X}^{m}
such that

\sup_{h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}\left|P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\prime}(x))-\hat{P}_{S}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\prime}(x))\right|\leq\epsilon,

which, as a special case, implies that any
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
with
the same classification
h(S)=h^{\prime}(S)
of
S

have
P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\prime}(x))\leq\epsilon
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
be a subset
containing exactly one classifier to witness each classification of
S
realizable by
\mathcal{H}
:
that is,
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}(S)=\mathcal{H}(S)
and
|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}|=|\mathcal{H}(S)|
.
It follows that
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}
is an
\epsilon
-cover of
\mathcal{H}
under the
L_{1}(P_{X})
pseudo-metric:
that is, it satisfies (
2
).
Since
|\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}|=|\mathcal{H}(S)|\leq 2^{m}<\infty
,
and this argument holds for any
\epsilon>0
,
we conclude that
\mathcal{H}
is totally bounded in
L_{1}(P_{X})
.

Next we present a lemma showing that, for totally bounded classes,
there exist functions
achieving
the infimum error rate,
within the
closure
of the class under
L_{1}(P_{X})
.
This generalizes a similar result of
Hanneke 2012
for VC classes.

Lemma 18

For any concept class
\mathcal{H}
and any probability measure
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
,
if
\mathcal{H}
is totally bounded in
L_{1}(P_{X})
,
then here exists a measurable function
h^{\star}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
(not necessarily in
\mathcal{H}
)
such that

\displaystyle\inf_{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}P_{X}(x:h^{\prime}(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=0

\displaystyle\text{and }~~\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\inf_{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\prime}).

In particular, it follows from Lemma
17
that this holds for any concept class
\mathcal{H}
satisfying the universal Glivenko-Cantelli (UGC) property,
and, by Lemma
16
,
this further implies that it holds for any
\mathcal{H}
which does not shatter an infinite VCL tree.

Proof
We prove this following an argument of
Hanneke 2012
, which originally established this property for VC classes;
noting that the argument there only relies on the total boundedness property, we can extend the result to any class that is totally bounded in
L_{1}(P_{X})
. For completeness, we include the complete argument here.

Fix any distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}

and any concept class
\mathcal{H}
that is totally bounded in
L_{1}(P_{X})
.
Let
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{H})
denote
the
closure
of
\mathcal{H}
in the
L_{1}(P_{X})
pseudo-metric:
namely,
the set of all measurable
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
satisfying

\inf_{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\prime}(x))=0
.
Note that any
\epsilon
-cover of
\mathcal{H}
under
L_{1}(P_{X})

is also an
\epsilon
-cover of
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{H})
, so that
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{H})
is also totally bounded.
Moreover, the entire space
\mathbf{L_{1}}(P_{X})
of integrable functions is
complete
(Theorem 5.2.1 of
Dudley 2002
),
and since
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{H})
is a
closed
subset of
\mathbf{L_{1}}(P_{X})
in the
L_{1}(P_{X})
pseudo-metric,

\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{H})
is also complete
(
Munkres 2000
)
.
Thus,
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{H})
is complete and totally bounded, hence compact.
This gives us that, for any sequence
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{H}

with
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{t})\to\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
(which must exist by definition of the infimum),
there exists a subsequence
t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots
such that
\{h_{t_{s}}\}_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L_{1}(P_{X})
,
which, by compactness, must admit a limit point in
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{H})
under
L_{1}(P_{X})
:
that is,
\exists h^{\star}\in\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{H})
with
\lim_{s\to\infty}P_{X}(x:h_{t_{s}}(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=0
.
By the triangle inequality, we have for any
s\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\geq\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{t_{s}})-P_{X}(x:h_{t_{s}}(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))

and taking the limit as
s\to\infty
reveals
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\geq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
Likewise, by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)+P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{t_{s}})+P_{X}(x:h_{t_{s}}(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h).

This completes the proof.

6
Near-Exponential Rate Lower Bound

This section begins our analysis leading to
Theorem
3
,
starting with the near-exponential lower bound.

Theorem 19

For any infinite concept class
\mathcal{H}
,
for any learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
,
there exists
\psi(n)=o(n)
and a distribution
P
such that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\geq e^{-\psi(n)}
for infinitely many
n
.

We will in fact show that this lower bound holds even for
distributions
P
satisfying the
Massart noise

condition
(
Massart 2007
;
Massart and Nédélec 2006
)
.
To prove this theorem, we require a result establishing existence of certain structures within any infinite class,
based on the recent work of
Hanneke 2024
.
Specifically, consider the following definition,
related to a quantity known as the
eluder dimension

introduced by
Russo and Van Roy 2013
;
Foster et al. 2021
in
the literature on reinforcement learning and
adversarial analysis of contextual bandits.

Definition 20

A sequence
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

in
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}

is an infinite eluder sequence for a concept class
\mathcal{H}

if there exist
h_{1},h_{2},\ldots
in
\mathcal{H}

such that,

\forall i\in\mathbb{N}
,

h_{i}(x_{<i})=y_{<i}

and
h_{i}(x_{i})\neq y_{i}
.
We say
\mathcal{H}
has an infinite eluder sequence if such a
sequence
(x_{i},y_{i})
exists.

Essentially, an infinite eluder sequence is a
countable sequence of labeled examples for which,
for every finite prefix,
there is an
h\in\mathcal{H}
correct on that
prefix but incorrect on the next example.
Importantly for our purposes,
recent work of
Hanneke 2024
shows that
any infinite concept class
\mathcal{H}
has an infinite
eluder sequence, as stated by the following lemma.

Lemma 21
(
Hanneke 2024
)

Any infinite concept class
\mathcal{H}
has an infinite eluder sequence.

The reasoning behind this lemma is a rather straightforward inductive construction of an eluder sequence:
for each
i=1,2,\ldots
,
supposing we inductively maintain that the set

\mathcal{H}_{(x_{<i},y_{<i})}:=\{h\in\mathcal{H}:h(x_{<i})=y_{<i}\}

of prefix-consistent functions is
infinite
,
we can extend the eluder sequence
by choosing any
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
for which
the functions
h\in\mathcal{H}_{(x_{<i},y_{<i})}

do not all agree on
h(x_{i})
(to satisfy the eluder property),
and by the pigeonhole principle there is a choice of
y_{i}\in\{0,1\}

for which
\mathcal{H}_{(x_{\leq i},y_{\leq i})}

is still infinite (to maintain the inductive invariant).

We are now ready for the proof of Theorem
19
,
establishing that no algorithm can achieve better than a near-exponential rate for any infinite concept class.

Proof of Theorem
19

By Lemma
21
,

\mathcal{H}
has an infinite eluder sequence

(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}),\ldots
in
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
.
Let
h_{1},h_{2},\ldots
be the sequence in
\mathcal{H}
witnessing this
(as in Definition
20
):
that is,
\forall i\in\mathbb{N}
,
h_{i}(x_{<i})=y_{<i}
and
h_{i}(x_{i})\neq y_{i}
.
Noting that these
x_{i}
’s must all be distinct,
let
h_{0}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
be any measurable function
(not necessarily in
\mathcal{H}
)
with
h_{0}(x_{i})=y_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Fix any
\beta\in(0,1/2)
,
and for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
define
p_{j}=2^{-j}
.
Define a family of distributions
P_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, as follows.
Let
P_{0}
denote a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
such that,
for
(X,Y)\sim P_{0}
,
each
j\in\mathbb{N}
has
\mathbf{P}(X=x_{j})=p_{j}

and
\mathbf{P}(Y=y_{j}|X=x_{j})=1-\beta
.
For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
define
P_{i}
as the probability measure on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
such that,
for
(X,Y)\sim P_{i}
,
each
j\in\mathbb{N}
has
\mathbf{P}(X=x_{j})=p_{j}
,
and

\mathbf{P}(Y=h_{i}(X)|X=x_{j})=\begin{cases}1-\beta&\text{ if }j<i\\
1&\text{ if }j\geq i\end{cases}.

Since
\sum_{j}p_{j}=1
, this completely specifies the probability measures
P_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
Define a sequence of independent
P_{0}
-distributed random variables:

(X_{1},Y_{1}^{(0)}),(X_{2},Y_{2}^{(0)}),\ldots
,
and for each
i,t\in\mathbb{N}
,
define

Y_{t}^{(i)}=\begin{cases}Y_{t}^{(0)},&\text{ if }X_{t}\in\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1}\}\\
h_{i}(X_{t}),&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}.

Note that, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

(X_{1},Y_{1}^{(i)}),(X_{2},Y_{2}^{(i)}),\ldots

is a sequence of independent
P_{i}
-distributed random variables.
Let
f_{n}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) be any learning algorithm,
and denote by
\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}=f_{n}(X_{1},Y^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y^{(i)}_{n})
for each
n,i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
:
that is
\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}
is the classifier returned by
the algorithm trained under
P_{i}
.

Sketch:

Before presenting the formal proof, we first briefly outline the argument.
For
n,i\geq 1
,
denote by
E_{n,i}
the event that

(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(0)})=(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(i)})
.
In particular, on
E_{n,i}
,

\hat{h}_{n}^{(0)}=\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}
.
Moreover,
since
h_{0}(x_{i})=y_{i}\neq h_{i}(x_{i})
,
the event
E_{n,i}
forces the learner to
guess

the appropriate classification of
x_{i}

without any distinguishing information

to tell whether the true distribution is
P_{0}
or
P_{i}
.
We also note that
E_{n,i}
is equivalent to the event that every

t\leq n
with
X_{t}\notin\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1}\}

has
Y^{(0)}_{t}=h_{i}(X_{t})
;
in particular, since this only concerns the variables
Y_{t}^{(0)}
which are
replaced
in the definition of
Y_{t}^{(i)}
,
this implies the event
E_{n,i}
is

conditionally independent
of
(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(i)})

given
X_{\leq n}
.
Therefore,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{(0)}(x_{i})\neq h_{0}(x_{i})\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\geq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\land E_{n,i}\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)=\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(E_{n,i}\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)
.
Denoting by
\hat{N}_{n,i}
the number of

t\leq n
with

X_{t}\notin\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1}\}
,
we have

\mathbf{P}\!\left(E_{n,i}\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\geq\beta^{\hat{N}_{n,i}}
.
A Chernoff bound
reveals that with probability at least
1-e^{-2p_{i}n/3}
,

\hat{N}_{n,i}\leq 4p_{i}n
.
Altogether,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{(0)}\right)\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h_{0})

\displaystyle\geq(1-2\beta)p_{i}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{(0)}(x_{i})\neq h_{0}(x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\geq(1-2\beta)p_{i}\left(1-\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}(x_{i})\neq h_{i}(x_{i})\right)-e^{-2p_{i}n/3}\right)\beta^{4p_{i}n}.

In particular, since w.l.o.g. we have

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})\neq h_{i}(x_{i})\right)\to 0

(otherwise the claimed lower bound is trivially satisfied for
P_{i}
),
this implies there exists a sequence
i_{n}\to\infty

for which

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{(0)}\right)\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h_{0})\geq(1-o(1))(1-2\beta)p_{i_{n}}\beta^{4p_{i_{n}}n}=e^{-o(n)}
.

Formal Proof:

We now proceed with the formal proof, following the above outline.
For each
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by

\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{i}}(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P_{i}}(h).

There are two cases.
First, if
\exists i\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}\neq 0,

(3)

then the claimed lower bound holds for this algorithm trivially, by taking distribution
P=P_{i}

for the
i
satisfying (
3
):
that is, in this case, there is a distribution
for which the excess risk does not merely converge slowly,
but in fact does not even converge to
0
.
Formally, for any such learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
,
taking
P=P_{i}
and

\psi(n)=\log\!\left(2/\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}\right)
(which is bounded, hence
o(n)
)
establishes the claimed lower bound
by definition of the
\mathop{\rm limsup}
.

To address the remaining case,
suppose every
i\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}=0.

(4)

In particular, note that
\mathrm{er}_{P_{i}}(h)
is minimized
(over all measurable
h
) by
h=h_{i}
,
and any
h
with
h(x_{i})\neq h_{i}(x_{i})
satisfies

\mathrm{er}_{P_{i}}(h)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{i}}(h_{i})\geq p_{i}.

Thus, by Markov’s inequality,
\forall n,i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})\neq h_{i}(x_{i})\right)\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P_{i}}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{i}}(h_{i})\geq p_{i}\right)\leq\frac{1}{p_{i}}\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}.

(5)

On the other hand, noting that

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h)=\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h_{0})
,
and any
h
with
h(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})

must have
h(x_{i})\neq h_{0}(x_{i})

(by definition of
h_{i}
),
any such
h
satisfies

\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h)-\inf_{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h^{\prime})=\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h)-\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h_{0})\geq(1-2\beta)p_{i}.

Thus,
\forall n,i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(0)}_{n}\right)\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h)\geq(1-2\beta)p_{i}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(0)}_{n}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\right).

(6)

Next we establish a relation between the
bounds (
5
) and (
6
)
by arguing that
\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}=\hat{h}_{n}^{(0)}

on an event of probability
e^{-o(n)}
.
For any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by

\hat{N}_{n,i}=|\{t\leq n:X_{t}\in\{x_{j}:j\geq i\}\}|

and let
t_{i,1},t_{i,2},\ldots
denote the subsequence of

t\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
satisfying
X_{t}\in\{x_{j}:j\geq i\}

(which may be infinite or finite, though the former occurs almost surely).
Let
E_{n,i}
denote the event that

\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,\hat{N}_{n,i}\},Y^{(0)}_{t_{i,k}}=h_{i}(X_{t_{i,k}}).

Note that on
E_{n,i}
, we have
(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(0)})=(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(i)})

and hence
\hat{h}_{n}^{(0)}=\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}
.
Recalling the definition of
P_{0}
,
we note that by conditional independence of the
Y_{t}^{(0)}
labels
given
X_{\leq n}
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(E_{n,i}\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\geq\beta^{\hat{N}_{n,i}}.

(7)

Since
\hat{h}_{n}^{(0)}=\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}

on the event
E_{n,i}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(0)}_{n}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\left\{\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\right\}\cap E_{n,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(\left\{\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\right\}\cap E_{n,i}\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\right].

(8)

Recalling the definition of
Y^{(i)}_{t}
,
we note that the labels
Y^{(0)}_{t_{i,k}}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,\hat{N}_{n,i}\}
,
are conditionally independent of
(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}^{(i)})

given
X_{\leq n}
.
Since whether or not the event
E_{n,i}
holds
is determined entirely by
X_{\leq n}

and these
Y^{(0)}_{t_{i,k}}
variables,
we have that

\hat{h}_{n}^{(i)}(x_{i})
is conditionally independent of
E_{n,i}

given
X_{\leq n}
.
Thus, the expression in (
8
) is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(E_{n,i}\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\right],

which, by (
7
),
is no smaller than

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\beta^{\hat{N}_{n,i}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{N}_{n,i}\leq 4p_{i}n\right]\right]\beta^{4p_{i}n}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{E}\!\left[1-\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})\neq h_{i}(x_{i})\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)-\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{N}_{n,i}>4p_{i}n\right]\right]\beta^{4p_{i}n}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(i)}_{n}(x_{i})\neq h_{i}(x_{i})\right)-\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{N}_{n,i}>4p_{i}n\right)\right)\beta^{4p_{i}n}.

(9)

Since each
X_{t}
has

\mathbf{P}(X_{t}\in\{x_{j}:j\geq i\})=\sum_{j\geq i}p_{j}=2p_{i}
,
and
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
are independent,
a Chernoff bound implies that

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{N}_{n,i}>4p_{i}n\right)\leq e^{-2p_{i}n/3}
.
Together with (
5
),
we have that (
9
) is no smaller than

\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{i}}\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}-e^{-2p_{i}n/3}\right)\beta^{4p_{i}n}.

Together with (
6
),
we have thus established that, for every
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(0)}_{n}\right)\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h)\geq(1-2\beta)p_{i}\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{i}}\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}-e^{-2p_{i}n/3}\right)\beta^{4p_{i}n}.

(10)

Note that, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
since
\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}\to 0
and
e^{-2p_{i}n/3}\to 0

as
n\to\infty
,
there exists
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
such that every
n\geq n_{i}

satisfies

\frac{1}{p_{i}}\epsilon^{(i)}_{n}+e^{-2p_{i}n/3}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

For any
n\geq n_{1}
,
define

i_{n}=\max\{i\in\mathbb{N}:n\geq\max\{n_{i},i^{2}\}\}.

Due to the
n\geq i^{2}
constraint, this is a well-defined finite value,
and moreover, since each
n_{i}
is finite,
we have
i_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Since (
10
) holds for every
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have for any
n\geq n_{1}

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}\!\left(\hat{h}^{(0)}_{n}\right)\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P_{0}}(h)\geq\frac{1-2\beta}{2}p_{i_{n}}\beta^{4p_{i_{n}}n}.

(11)

Defining
\psi(n)=\ln\!\left(\frac{2}{1-2\beta}\right)+\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{p_{i_{n}}}\right)+4p_{i_{n}}n\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)
,
the right hand side of (
11
)
is equal
e^{-\psi(n)}
.
Finally, since the definition of
i_{n}
implies
i_{n}\leq\sqrt{n}
,
we have
\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{p_{i_{n}}}\right)=i_{n}\ln(2)=o(n)

and since
i_{n}\to\infty
implies
p_{i_{n}}\to 0
,
we have
4p_{i_{n}}n\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)=o(n)
as well.
Since the remaining term in
\psi(n)
is an additive constant,
we conclude that
\psi(n)=o(n)
.
This completes the proof.

7
Near-Exponential Rate Upper Bound

Continuing toward establishing Theorem
3
,
we next establish the near-exponential rate upper bound
for concept classes which do not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree.

Theorem 22

If
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree,
then for every
\psi(n)=o(n)
,

\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable at rate
e^{-\psi(n)}
.

The formal proof of Theorem
22

is presented in Section
7.1
below.
Before getting into the details,
we first briefly outline the strategy underlying the algorithm

\hat{h}_{n}
achieving this
e^{-\psi(n)}
rate,
for any given
\psi(n)=o(n)
.
We divide the approach into two subroutines,
intending to address separately the cases where

P
is or is not
Bayes-realizable
,
recalling that we say
P
is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
if
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
(where the latter infimum ranges over all measurable
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
).
The first subroutine,
designed to address the case where
P
is not
Bayes-realizable w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
,
simply
trains a universally Bayes-consistent learning algorithm
\hat{h}^{0}_{n}
(Lemma
14
).
A second subroutine
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}
,
outlined below, is designed to address the case
where
P
is Bayes-realizable w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
.
We then select between
\hat{h}^{0}_{n}
and
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}
,
via a hypothesis test based on held-out data,
to obtain the final predictor
\hat{h}_{n}
.
Thus, the main technical novelty in the approach is in the design of the
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}
subroutine.

To understand the main ideas behind the
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}
algorithm,
it will be instructive to first recall the original realizable-case technique of
Bousquet et al. 2021
establishing
e^{-n}
rates,
before discussing the modifications needed to extend the technique to the agnostic setting.

The Realizable Case:

In the case of a distribution
P
realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
(where
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree),
the algorithm of
Bousquet et al. 2021

makes use of the procedure
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}

from Lemma
10

(which itself is based on a measurable winning strategy for an associated Gale-Stewart game).
Recall that Lemma
10

guarantees that
\exists b^{*}\in\mathbb{N}
such that,
for any
b\geq b^{*}
, for an i.i.d. data set
B^{b}\sim P^{b}
,
with probability at least
\frac{99}{100}
,
the classifier defined by
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}(B^{b})
has error rate
zero
.
To convert this
\frac{99}{100}
guarantee into an

e^{-n}
rate,
they use a fraction
m=n/2
of the training examples
S^{0}_{n}
,
break them into
m/b
disjoint
batches

B^{b}_{i}
(
i\in\{1,\ldots,m/b\}
),
which induce predictors
h_{b,i}=\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}(B^{b}_{i})
.
By a Chernoff bound, with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(m/b)}
,
at least
\frac{9}{10}
of these have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})=0
,
and thus the
majority vote
predictor

x\mapsto\mathrm{Majority}(h_{b,1}(x),\ldots,h_{b,m/b}(x))

would also have error rate zero.

All of this holds for any
b\geq b^{*}
.
However, since
b^{*}
is
P
-dependent,
their method also needs to select an appropriate
b

without knowledge of
b^{*}
.
For this, they construct the above predictors
h_{b,i}

for
every

b\leq m
, and use the remaining

m=n/2
training examples
S^{1}_{n}
to
select
which
b
to use:
namely,
\hat{b}
is the minimum
b
for which
at least
\frac{9}{10}
of the
h_{b,i}
predictors
have no mistakes on
S^{1}_{n}
.
From the above Chernoff bound, we know
b^{*}
satisfies
this, so
\hat{b}\leq b^{*}
.
Moreover, for any
b<b^{*}
having

\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})>0)>\frac{3}{10}
,
there is some
\epsilon_{b}>0
such that

\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})>\epsilon_{b})>\frac{2.5}{10}
,
so that (by a Chernoff bound) with probability at least
1-e^{-\Omega(m/b)}

at least
\frac{2}{10}
of these
h_{b,i}
have

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})>\epsilon_{b}
,
and hence (by a union bound)
with probability at least
1-\frac{m}{5b}e^{-\epsilon_{b}m}

each of these will make at least one mistake on
S^{1}_{n}
.
Altogether, with probability
1-\frac{m}{5b}e^{-\epsilon_{b}m}-e^{-\Omega(m/b)}=1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,
we conclude
\hat{b}\neq b
for any such
b
.
By a union bound over all such
b<b^{*}
,
we conclude that with probability at least
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,

\hat{b}
is a value
b
for which

\mathbf{P}(\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})=0)\geq\frac{7}{10}
,
and the Chernoff argument regarding the majority
vote yields the desired guarantee:
i.e., for
\hat{h}=\mathrm{Majority}(h_{\hat{b},1},\ldots,h_{\hat{b},m/\hat{b}})
,
with probability at least
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h})=0
,
implying
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h})\right]=e^{-\Omega(n)}
.

The Agnostic Case:

To extend the above technique to the agnostic case,
as mentioned above, as the main ingredient,
the algorithm
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}
only needs to address the case where
P
is
Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
:
that is,
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
The two main challenges in extending the technique of

Bousquet et al. 2021
to distributions
P

which are merely Bayes-realizable (rather than realizable)
are that the batches
B^{b}_{i}
are no longer realizable,
and when selecting an appropriate
b
,
even the good predictors
h_{b,i}
will still make

some
mistakes on the held-out data set
S^{1}_{n}
.

The principle we adopt in approaching the first of these issues is to
simulate
having batches
B^{b}_{i}

sampled from an appropriate
realizable
distribution.
Specifically, due to Lemmas
16
,
17
, and
18
,
in the Bayes-realizable case there exists a measurable function

h^{\star}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

with
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

and
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=0
.
If we then denote by
P_{h^{\star}}
the
distribution of
(X,h^{\star}(X))
(for
X\sim P_{X}
),
note that
P_{h^{\star}}
is
realizable
with respect to
\mathcal{H}
,
and any
h^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{er}_{P_{h^{\star}}}(h^{\prime})=0
has

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\prime})=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
We are therefore interested in applying
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}
to
batches
B^{b}_{i}
sampled i.i.d. from this realizable distribution
P_{h^{\star}}
.

Since
h^{\star}
is not known to the learner,
we will in fact apply
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}
to an entire
collection

of data sets, one of which is indeed
P_{h^{\star}}
-distributed.
Namely, for each
b\leq m
and
i\leq m/b
,
we construct
2^{b}
data sets:
for each
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{b})\in\{0,1\}^{b}
, define
B^{b}_{i}(\mathbf{y})
as
B^{b}_{i}
but with labels

Y

replaced
by the hypothetical label sequence
y_{t}
.
We then define
h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}=\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}(B^{b}_{i}(\mathbf{y}))
.
Since one of these
\mathbf{y}
sequences
agrees

with the
h^{\star}(X)
values of all
X
in
B^{b}_{i}
,
for
b\geq b^{*}
we can guarantee that
with probability at least
\frac{99}{100}

at least one
\mathbf{y}
has

\mathrm{er}_{P_{h^{\star}}}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})=0
,
and hence
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
In particular, by a Chernoff bound,
this occurs for at least
\frac{9}{10}
of the batches,
with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(m/b)}
.

Moreover, for each such
b,i
, we argue it is possible
to use the held-out
m
examples
S^{1}_{n}

to
select
an
h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
for which
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
For this, we note that, for any
b
,
there exists
\gamma_{b}>0

such that, with probability at least
\frac{99}{100}
,
every
\mathbf{y}
with

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

in fact has

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})>\gamma_{b}+\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
: i.e., its suboptimality has at least some
gap
.
By a Chernoff bound, this holds for at least
\frac{9}{10}
of the batches with probability

1-e^{-\Omega(m/b)}
.
In particular, combining these two parts,
for any
b\geq b^{*}
,
a standard Hoeffding and union bound argument implies that, for sufficiently large
n
,
simply taking
h_{b,i}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S^{1}_{n}}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})

suffices to guarantee
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
In particular, when this holds, returning

\hat{h}^{1}_{n}=\mathrm{Majority}(h_{b,1},\ldots,h_{b,m/b})

also satisfies
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}^{1}_{n})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
Thus, the main remaining issue is selecting an
appropriate
b
without direct knowledge of
the
P
-dependent value
b^{*}
.

In general (both for the above argument about
h_{b,i}
, and for selecting
b
)
we make use of a concentration inequality for the error rates of the
h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
classifiers (equation
14
in the proof).
Specifically, by Hoeffding’s inequality and the union bound,
with probability at least
1-e^{-\psi(n)}
,
for every
b\leq m
,
i\leq m/b
, and
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,

\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S^{1}_{n}}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\right)\right|\leq c\sqrt{\frac{\psi(n)+b+\ln(n)}{n}}

(12)

for a universal constant
c
.
In particular, for
b=b^{*}
and
n
sufficiently large to make the right hand side
<\gamma_{b}/2
,
this inequality supplies the stated
guarantee

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

for the
\frac{9}{10}
of batches satisfying
both the

\gamma_{b}
gap condition
and the existence of a good
\mathbf{y}
,
as described above.

We also use (
12
) to
select
the value
b
.
Specifically, note that for
b=b^{*}
,
on the above events,
at least
\frac{9}{10}
of the functions
h_{b,i}

(being optimal functions) have the property that,
for every
b^{\prime}>b
and
i^{\prime}\leq m/b^{\prime}
,

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S^{1}_{n}}(h_{b,i})\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S^{1}_{n}}(h_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}})+2c\sqrt{\frac{\psi(n)+b^{\prime}+\ln(n)}{n}}
.
We define
\hat{b}
as the smallest
b

for which this holds.
In particular, this implies
\hat{b}\leq b^{*}
.
Moreover, since any
b<b^{*}
has

b^{*}
as one of these
b^{\prime}
values we compare to,
since at least
\frac{9}{10}
of the
b
batches
satisfy the
\gamma_{b}
gap condition discussed above,
for
n
sufficiently large (to make the concentration inequality smaller than
\gamma_{b}/4
)
these comparisons
will not select
\hat{b}=b
for any
b<b^{*}

for which the majority of
h_{b,i}
functions
have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
Altogether, we conclude that
for
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}=\mathrm{Majority}(h_{\hat{b},1},\ldots,h_{\hat{b},m/\hat{b}})
,
with probability at least
1-e^{-\psi(n)}-e^{-\Omega(m/b^{*})}
,

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}^{1}_{n})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
,
which therefore implies the claimed rate
e^{-\psi(n)}
.

We present the formal details next, in Section
7.1
.

7.1
Proof of Theorem
22
:
e^{-o(n)}
Rates

We now present the formal details of the proof of Theorem
22
.

Proof of Theorem
22

Fix any
\psi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
satisfying
\psi(n)=o(n)
.
We will establish that
\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable at rate
e^{-\psi(n)}
.
Without loss of generality suppose
\psi(n)\to\infty

(establishing the claim for such functions also implies it
for functions
\psi(n)=o(n)
with
\psi(n)\not\to\infty

by applying the claim to
\psi^{\prime}(n)=\psi(n)\lor\sqrt{n}
).
We begin by describing the learning algorithm
f_{n}
.
Let
P
be any distribution on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
, let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
and let
S_{n}=\{(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})\}\sim P^{n}
be the i.i.d. training set input to the learning algorithm.
For simplicity, suppose
n
is an integer multiple of
3
; otherwise, replace
n
with
3\lfloor n/3\rfloor
in the description and analysis below (supposing
n\geq 3
).
We describe the construction of the function
\hat{h}_{n}=f_{n}(S_{n})

produced by
f_{n}
when given training set
S_{n}
.
We first segment the data
S_{n}
into chunks of size
n/3
: for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
, let

S_{n}^{k}=\{(X_{1+kn/3},Y_{1+kn/3}),\ldots,(X_{(k+1)n/3},Y_{(k+1)n/3})\}.

The algorithm has two main components, to handle two types of distributions
P
.
Formally, recall that we say
P
is
Bayes-realizable

with respect to
\mathcal{H}

if
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
,
where
h
on the right hand side ranges over all measurable
functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
.
The first component in the learning algorithm is designed to handle
the case that
P
is
not
Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.
Specifically, let
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
