be the classifier returned by a universally Bayes-consistent learning algorithm with training set
S_{n}^{0}
(as in Lemma
14
).
Thus, we have that

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})]\to\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
for all choices of the distribution
P
.
The intention behind this is that, in the arguments below,
it will be convenient to focus on the case of
P
that is
Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.
We will construct a predictor
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
below,
which is guaranteed to have the required rate when
P
is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.
When this is not the case, the universally consistent
learner
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
will have excess risk
(relative to the infimum error among
h\in\mathcal{H}
) which is
in fact converging to a
negative
value,
so that the non-Bayes-realizable case is handled
by
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
.
With both
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
and
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
defined,
we can then merely select between these two functions
in the end (using additional independent samples)
to always guarantee the required rate for the excess risk
(which may become negative in the case
P
is not Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
).

Next we describe the second component, defining the aforementioned predictor
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
.
For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
, let us further segment the sequence
S_{n}^{0}
into
batches

B^{b}_{i}
of size
b
:
let
I_{b}=\{1,\ldots,\lfloor n/(3b)\rfloor\}
,
and for each
i\in I_{b}
, define

B^{b}_{i}=\left\{(X_{1+(i-1)b},Y_{1+(i-1)b}),\ldots,(X_{ib},Y_{ib})\right\}.

For each
i\in I_{b}
,
and each
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{b})\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,
define

B^{b}_{i}(\mathbf{y})=\{(X_{1+(i-1)b},y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{ib},y_{b})\},

that is,
B^{b}_{i}
, but with the
Y
labels replaced by
y
labels.
Let
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}
be the function defined in Lemma
10
for the class
\mathcal{H}
. For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
,
i\in I_{b}
, and
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,
we define a function
h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

as follows: for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}(x)=\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{SOA}}(B^{b}_{i}(\mathbf{y}),x).

We will next use the data subset
S_{n}^{1}
to select one function

h_{b,i}
for each batch
B^{b}_{i}
,
from among these
h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
functions.
Specifically, for each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}

and
i\in I_{b}
,
define

h_{b,i}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}:\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}),

breaking ties in any way that preserves measurability
of the function mapping
B^{b}_{i}
and any
x

to
h_{b,i}(x)\in\{0,1\}

(e.g., breaking ties lexicographically by
\mathbf{y}
suffices).

We will also use
S_{n}^{1}
to select an appropriate batch size
\hat{b}_{n}
as follows.
For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
, define

\mathrm{GoodI}(b)
as the set of all
i\in I_{b}
such that,

\forall b^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
with
b^{\prime}>b
,
\forall i^{\prime}\in I_{b^{\prime}}
,

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h_{b,i})\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}})+2\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{\prime}+\ln(n)}{n}}.

Define

\hat{b}_{n}=\min\!\left\{b\in\left\{1,\ldots,\frac{n}{3}\right\}:|\mathrm{GoodI}(b)|\geq\frac{9}{10}|I_{b}|\right\},

if the set on the right hand side is non-empty,
and otherwise (for completeness of the definition of
\hat{h}_{n}
) simply define
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
and define
f_{n}(S_{n})=\hat{h}_{n}
(i.e., the algorithm returns this classifier
\hat{h}_{n}
), which completes the definition of the learning algorithm in this case.

Supposing
\hat{b}_{n}
is defined,
the interpretation of the above choice of
\hat{b}_{n}
is that,
for
b=\hat{b}_{n}
, at least
\frac{9}{10}
of the classifiers
h_{b,i}
are not verifiably worse
than some other classifier
h_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}
with
b^{\prime}>b
,
where this verification comes via a concentration inequality
holding (uniformly over all such comparisons) with probability at least
1-e^{-\psi(n)}
(as we will argue below).

Based on this choice of
\hat{b}_{n}
,
for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we define

\hat{h}_{n}^{1}(x)=\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\sum_{i\in I_{\hat{b}_{n}}}h_{\hat{b}_{n},i}(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}|I_{\hat{b}_{n}}|\right],

the majority vote of the
h_{\hat{b}_{n},i}(x)
predictions.

To complete the definition of the algorithm, we define
\hat{h}_{n}
by selecting one of
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
or
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
using the remaining data
S_{n}^{2}
, as follows.
If

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\sqrt{\frac{6\psi(n)}{n}},

define
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
,
and otherwise define
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
.
In either case, define
f_{n}(S_{n})
as this
\hat{h}_{n}

(i.e., the algorithm returns the classifier
\hat{h}_{n}
).
The algorithm is summarized in Figure
1
.

1. Let
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
be returned by a universally Bayes-consistent learner trained on
S_{n}^{0}

2. Let
\hat{b}_{n}=\min\!\left\{b\leq n/3:|\mathrm{GoodI}(b)|\geq\frac{9}{10}\left\lfloor\frac{n}{3b}\right\rfloor\right\}

(if
\hat{b}_{n}
does not exist, return
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
)

3. Let
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}=\mathrm{Majority}\!\left(h_{\hat{b}_{n},i}:i\leq n/(3\hat{b}_{n})\right)

5. If
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\sqrt{\frac{6\psi(n)}{n}}
, return
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
, else return
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}

Figure 1:
Algorithm achieving
e^{-\psi(n)}
rate for classes
\mathcal{H}
with no infinite Littlestone tree.

With the definition of the classifier
\hat{h}_{n}
now complete,
we proceed to prove that it satisfies the required rate
e^{-\psi(n)}
.
First, we remark that the claim of achieving rate
e^{-\psi(n)}

is
asymptotic
in nature: that is, it suffices to argue that,
for some
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
(possibly
P
-dependent),
there exists a constant
C>0
(also possibly
P
-dependent) such that,
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq n_{0}
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq Ce^{-\psi(n)}
.
This is due to the fact that,
since
\psi(n)
is finite for every
n
,
and
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq 1
,
we can simply define another (possibly
P
-dependent) constant value
C^{\prime}=\min\!\left\{Ce^{-\psi(n)}:n\in\{1,\ldots,n_{0}-1\}\right\}>0
,
and it would then follow that
every

n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq\frac{C}{\min\{1,C^{\prime}\}}e^{-\psi(n)}
(with this bound being no smaller than
1
for
n<n_{0}
, hence holding vacuously for these small
n
values).
For this reason, in parts of the proof, we will
explicitly focus on the case of
n
taken
sufficiently large

to satisfy certain constraints introduced in the analysis below.
We now proceed with the proof.

Let
E_{1}
denote the event that, either
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is undefined
(due to the early termination event in the algorithm, when the criterion for
\hat{b}_{n}
cannot be satisfied)
or else

\forall j\in\{0,1\},\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{j})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{j})\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{3\psi(n)}{2n}}.

(13)

Since
|S_{n}^{2}|=\frac{n}{3}
,
and
S_{n}^{2}
is independent of
(S_{n}^{0},S_{n}^{1})

(from which
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
and
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
are derived),
Hoeffding’s inequality
(applied under the conditional distribution given
S_{n}^{0}
and
S_{n}^{1}
)
and a union bound, with the law of total probability, imply that
E_{1}
holds with probability at least
1-4e^{-\psi(n)}
.

We first consider the case where
P
is
not
Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
: that is,

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

(where
h
on the right hand side ranges over all measurable functions
\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
).
In this case, let
\epsilon>0
be such that
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)+\epsilon
.
As mentioned above, it suffices to establish a bound
Ce^{-\psi(n)}

for all
sufficiently large

n
.
We may therefore focus on the case that
n
is sufficiently large that

\sqrt{\frac{3\psi(n)}{2n}}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{16}
holds;
since
\psi(n)=o(n)
, this inequality is satisfied for all sufficiently large
n
.
On the event
E_{1}
(and the event that
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is defined), if additionally we have

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})<\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})>\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}
,
then

\displaystyle\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})-\frac{\epsilon}{16}>\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{3\epsilon}{16}

\displaystyle>\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\frac{5\epsilon}{16}\geq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\geq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\sqrt{\frac{24\psi(n)}{n}},

so that the algorithm chooses
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
.
On the other hand, if

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})<\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}
,
then regardless of whether
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
or
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
,
we have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}
.
Moreover, if
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is not defined, then the algorithm chooses
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
.
Thus, in any case, on the event
E_{1}
, if
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})<\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}
,
we always have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}
.
In particular, since we always have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq 1
, this implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}+\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})\geq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)+\left(1-\mathbf{P}(E_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}+\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)+4e^{-\psi(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})-\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\right]+4e^{-\psi(n)},

where the second inequality follows from the definition of
\epsilon
,
satisfying
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)+\epsilon
,
and the bound on
\mathbf{P}(E_{1})
established above,
and the last line follows from Markov’s inequality,
noting
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}^{0}_{n})-\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
is non-negative.
Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}4e^{-\psi(n)}=0
and the Bayes consistency property of
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
guarantees

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})-\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\right]=0
,
we have that for all sufficiently large
n
the right hand side of the last line is at most
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{16}
.
Thus, for all sufficiently large
n
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)<0\leq e^{-\psi(n)}.

To complete the proof, we now turn to the remaining case,
in which
P
is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
:
that is,
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
We will assume this to be the case for the remainder of the proof.
Since
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree,
by Lemma
16

it satisfies the universal Glivenko-Cantelli (UGC) property (Definition
15
),
which further implies
\mathcal{H}
is totally bounded in
L_{1}(P_{X})

by Lemma
17
,
and therefore Lemma
18
implies there exists a measurable function
h^{\star}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
with

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
and
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=0
,
where
P_{X}
denotes the marginal of
P
on
\mathcal{X}
.
Moreover,
letting
P_{h^{\star}}
denote the distribution of
(X,h^{\star}(X))
, for
X\sim P_{X}
,
note that the property
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=0
further implies
that
P_{h^{\star}}
is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.

To argue the existence of
\hat{b}_{n}
,
and the quality of the functions
h_{\hat{b}_{n},i}
in the majority vote
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
,
we will need the following
argument about concentration of empirical error rates of all
h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
functions.
Let
E_{2}
denote the event that, for every
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
,
i\in I_{b}
, and
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,
we have

\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\right)\right|\leq\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b+\ln(n)}{n}}.

(14)

For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
,
i\in I_{b}
, and
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,
observing that

\sqrt{\frac{\psi(n)+\ln(b(b+1)2^{b+1}\lfloor n/(3b)\rfloor)}{2n/3}}\leq\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b+\ln(n)}{n}},

applying Hoeffding’s inequality under the conditional
distribution given
S_{n}^{0}

implies that (
14
) holds with conditional probability at least

1-\frac{1}{b(b+1)2^{b}|I_{b}|}e^{-\psi(n)}
.
Therefore,
by the union bound and the law of total probability,
(
14
)
holds simultaneously for all
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
,
i\in I_{b}
, and
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}

with probability at least

1-\sum_{b,i,\mathbf{y}}\frac{1}{b(b+1)2^{b}|I_{b}|}e^{-\psi(n)}=1-\sum_{b}\frac{1}{b(b+1)}e^{-\psi(n)}\geq 1-e^{-\psi(n)}.

That is,
E_{2}
holds with probability at least
1-e^{-\psi(n)}
.

Let
b^{*}=b^{*}_{99/100}\in\mathbb{N}

be defined as in Lemma
10
(with
\gamma=99/100
)
for the class
\mathcal{H}
and the distribution
P_{h^{\star}}

(recalling that
P_{h^{\star}}
is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
).
Since, as mentioned above, it suffices to establish a bound

Ce^{-\psi(n)}
for all
sufficiently large

n
,
for the remainder of the proof we suppose
n\geq 3b^{*}
.
For each
i\in I_{b^{*}}
,
let
\mathbf{y}^{*}_{b^{*},i}=(h^{\star}(X_{1+(i-1)b^{*}}),\ldots,h^{\star}(X_{ib^{*}}))
.
Noting that the elements of

B^{b^{*}}_{i}(\mathbf{y}^{*}_{b^{*},i})
are i.i.d.
P_{h^{\star}}
-distributed,
Lemma
10
implies that

\forall i\in I_{b^{*}}
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P_{h^{\star}}}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{b^{*},i}}_{b^{*},i}\right)=0\right)>\frac{99}{100}.

(15)

For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
and
i\in I_{b}
,
let

\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)=\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}:\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\right)=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right\}

and let

\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}:\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)\neq\emptyset\}.

In particular, note that if

\mathrm{er}_{P_{h^{\star}}}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{b^{*},i}}_{b^{*},i}\right)=0
, then by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{b^{*},i}}_{b^{*},i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\star}\right)+P_{X}\!\left(x:h^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{b^{*},i}}_{b^{*},i}(x)\neq h^{\star}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})+\mathrm{er}_{P_{h^{\star}}}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{b^{*},i}}_{b^{*},i}\right)=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}),

(16)

so that

\mathbf{y}^{*}_{b^{*},i}\in\mathrm{OPTY}(b^{*},i)
,
and hence
i\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b^{*})
.
Together, by (
15
) and (
16
),
we have that
\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b^{*})\right)>\frac{99}{100}
.

For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
, since
\{0,1\}^{b}
has finite size,
there exists
\gamma_{b}>0
such that

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{OPTY}(b,1)\!=\!\{0,1\}^{b}\text{ or }\min\!\left\{\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,1}\right)\!:\mathbf{y}\!\in\!\{0,1\}^{b}\!\setminus\!\mathrm{OPTY}(b,1)\right\}\!>\!\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\!+\!\gamma_{b}\right)>\frac{99}{100}.

In other words, with probability greater than
\frac{99}{100}
,
either
every

\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}
has
optimal error
\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,1}\right)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
,
or else the smallest
non-optimal
error rate among
h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,1}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}
, is greater than
\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)+\gamma_{b}
.
Let
\gamma^{*}=\min_{b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}}\gamma_{b}
, and note that
\gamma^{*}>0
since
\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}
is a finite set.
For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}
, define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Gap}(b)=

\displaystyle\left\{i\in I_{b}:\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)=\{0,1\}^{b}\text{ or }\right.

\displaystyle{\hskip 51.21495pt}\left.\min\!\left\{\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\right):\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}\setminus\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)\right\}>\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})+\gamma^{*}\right\}.

Also define

\mathrm{PrbGoodB}=\left\{b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}:\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\right)\geq\frac{7}{10}\right\}.

In particular, since
\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b^{*})\right)>\frac{99}{100}
(established above),
we have
b^{*}\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
.

Let
E_{3}
denote the event that
\forall b\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
, we have

|\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\cap\mathrm{Gap}(b)|\geq\left(\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\right)-\frac{1}{50}\right)|I_{b}|.

For any
b\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
,
recall that the samples
B^{b}_{i}
,
i\in I_{b}
,
are identically distributed.
Thus,
for any
i\in I_{b}
,
by the union bound (and recalling that
\gamma^{*}\leq\gamma_{b}
),

\mathbf{P}\!\left(i\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\cap\mathrm{Gap}(b)\right)>\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\right)-\frac{1}{100}.

Since
B^{b}_{1},B^{b}_{2},\ldots,B^{b}_{|I_{b}|}
are independent,
Hoeffding’s inequality, and the union bound (over
b
values in
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
),
imply that
E_{3}
has probability at least

1-b^{*}e^{-C_{1}n}
,
where
C_{1}=\frac{1}{100^{2}}\frac{1}{3b^{*}}
.

As discussed above, since it suffices to establish a bound

Ce^{-\psi(n)}
for all
sufficiently large

n
,
for the remainder of the proof we suppose
n
is sufficiently
large to satisfy the inequality

\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{*}+\ln(n)}{n}}<\frac{\gamma^{*}}{2}
, which is indeed satisfied for all sufficiently large
n
since
\psi(n)=o(n)
.
Note that, on the event
E_{2}
,
for every
b\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
and
i\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\cap\mathrm{Gap}(b)
,
for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)

and every
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{b}\setminus\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)

(if any such
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
exists), we have

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})<\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})+\frac{\gamma^{*}}{2}<\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}_{b,i})-\frac{\gamma^{*}}{2}<\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h^{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}_{b,i}),

where the first inequality is due to
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)
and (
14
),
the second inequality is due to
i\in\mathrm{Gap}(b)
,
and the third inequality is again due to (
14
).
In particular, this implies
h_{b,i}\neq h^{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}_{b,i}
.
Since
i\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)
guarantees
\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)
is non-empty,
we conclude that on
E_{2}
, for every
b\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
and
i\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\cap\mathrm{Gap}(b)
,
the classifier
h_{b,i}
equals some
h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
for some
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{OPTY}(b,i)
, and hence

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})
.
Equivalently, defining

\mathrm{OPTI}(b)=\left\{i\in I_{b}:\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b,i})=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right\},

we have shown that on the event
E_{2}
, every
b\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
has

\mathrm{OPTI}(b)\supseteq\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\cap\mathrm{Gap}(b).

Thus, by the definition of the event
E_{3}
,
we may also conclude that,
on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}
, every
b\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
has

|\mathrm{OPTI}(b)|\geq\left(\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\right)-\frac{1}{50}\right)|I_{b}|.

(17)

In particular, since
b^{*}\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}

and
\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b^{*})\right)>\frac{99}{100}

(both established above),
on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}

we have

|\mathrm{OPTI}(b^{*})|>\frac{97}{100}|I_{b^{*}}|>\frac{9}{10}|I_{b^{*}}|.

(18)

Additionally, note that on the event
E_{2}
, for any
i\in\mathrm{OPTI}(b^{*})
,
for any
b^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n/3\}
with
b^{\prime}>b^{*}
, for any
i^{\prime}\in I_{b^{\prime}}

and any
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{b^{\prime}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h_{b^{*},i})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})+\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{*}+\ln(n)}{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}})+\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{*}+\ln(n)}{n}}\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h^{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}})+2\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{\prime}+\ln(n)}{n}}.

In particular, since
\exists\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{b^{\prime}}
such that
h_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}=h^{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}
,
it follows that

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h_{b^{*},i})\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}})+2\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{\prime}+\ln(n)}{n}}.

Thus, recalling the definition of
\mathrm{GoodI}(b)
from the learning algorithm,
we conclude that on the event
E_{2}
,
we have
\mathrm{GoodI}(b^{*})\supseteq\mathrm{OPTI}(b^{*})
.
Together with (
18
),
we have that on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}
,

\hat{b}_{n}
is defined (and hence so is
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
)
and satisfies
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}
.

We must also argue that any “bad”
b<b^{*}
will not be selected as
\hat{b}_{n}
.
Specifically, we will establish that
\hat{b}_{n}\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
with high probability.
This is trivially satisfied if
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}=\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}
,
in which case we may skip the following argument and proceed from (
19
) below (defining
E_{4}
as a vacuous event of probability one in that case);
to address the remaining case, let us suppose

\mathrm{PrbGoodB}\neq\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}
.
For any
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}

and
i\in I_{b}
, define

\varepsilon(b,i)=\min_{\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}).

Since
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
,
by definition of
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
we have

\mathbf{P}(1\notin\mathrm{HasOPT}(b))>\frac{3}{10}
.
Also note that, since
\{0,1\}^{b}
is finite,
if
1\notin\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)
then
\varepsilon(b,1)>0
:
that is,

\mathbf{P}(\varepsilon(b,1)>0|1\notin\mathrm{HasOPT}(b))=1
.
This further implies that
there exists
\varepsilon^{*}(b)>0

such that

\mathbf{P}(\varepsilon(b,1)>\varepsilon^{*}(b)|1\notin\mathrm{HasOPT}(b))>\frac{2}{3}
.
Let

\varepsilon^{*}=\min\{\varepsilon^{*}(b):b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}\},

and note that
\varepsilon^{*}>0

since
\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
is finite.
Thus,
for any
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
,
since we also have
\mathbf{P}(1\notin\mathrm{HasOPT}(b))>\frac{3}{10}
,
altogether we have

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\varepsilon(b,1)>\varepsilon^{*}\right)=\mathbf{P}\!\left(\varepsilon(b,1)>\varepsilon^{*}\middle|1\notin\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\notin\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\right)>\frac{2}{3}\cdot\frac{3}{10}=\frac{1}{5}.

Moreover, since
B_{i}^{b}
,
i\in I_{b}
,
are identically distributed,
for any
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
,
every
i\in I_{b}

also satisfies
\mathbf{P}\!\left(\varepsilon(b,i)>\varepsilon^{*}\right)>\frac{1}{5}
.

For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
, define

\mathrm{FarFromOPTI}(b)=\left\{i\in I_{b}:\varepsilon(b,i)>\varepsilon^{*}\right\}.

Let
E_{4}
denote the event that every
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
has

|\mathrm{FarFromOPTI}(b)|\geq\frac{3}{20}|I_{b}|.

Recalling that, for each
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
,
the
B^{b}_{1},\ldots,B^{b}_{|I_{b}|}
samples are independent,
Hoeffding’s inequality implies
|\mathrm{FarFromOPTI}(b)|\geq\frac{3}{20}|I_{b}|

with probability at least
1-e^{-C_{2}n}

where
C_{2}=\frac{1}{400}\frac{1}{3b^{*}}
.
Applying this to every
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
,
the union bound implies
the event
E_{4}
has probability at least
1-(b^{*}-1)e^{-C_{2}n}
.

To complete this part of the argument, we show that the definition of
\hat{b}_{n}
prevents
\hat{b}_{n}\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
.
Again, as discussed above, since it suffices to establish a bound
Ce^{-\psi(n)}
for all
sufficiently large

n
,
for the remainder of the proof we suppose
n
is sufficiently large to satisfy

\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{*}+\ln(n)}{n}}<\frac{\varepsilon^{*}}{4}
, which indeed holds for all sufficiently large
n
since
\psi(n)=o(n)
.
On the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}
,
we know from (
18
) that
the set
\mathrm{OPTI}(b^{*})
is nonempty.
Moreover, also on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}
,
for any
i^{\prime}\in\mathrm{OPTI}(b^{*})
,
any
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
,
and any
i\in\mathrm{FarFromOPTI}(b)
and
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\right)>\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\right)-\frac{\varepsilon^{*}}{4}

\displaystyle>\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})+\frac{3\varepsilon^{*}}{4}=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{b^{*},i^{\prime}})+\frac{3\varepsilon^{*}}{4}

\displaystyle>\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h_{b^{*},i^{\prime}})+\frac{\varepsilon^{*}}{2}>\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h_{b^{*},i^{\prime}})+2\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{*}+\ln(n)}{n}},

where the first inequality is due to (
14
),
the second inequality is due to
i\in\mathrm{FarFromOPTI}(b)
,
the equality following this is due to
i^{\prime}\in\mathrm{OPTI}(b^{*})
,
and the inequality following this is again due to
(
14
).
In particular, this implies

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}\!\left(h_{b,i}\right)>\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(h_{b^{*},i^{\prime}})+2\sqrt{3\frac{\psi(n)+b^{*}+\ln(n)}{n}},

so that
\mathrm{GoodI}(b)\subseteq I_{b}\setminus\mathrm{FarFromOPTI}(b)
.
Thus, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,
every
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}

has

|\mathrm{GoodI}(b)|\leq\frac{17}{20}|I_{b}|<\frac{9}{10}|I_{b}|
,
so that
\hat{b}_{n}\neq b
.
Hence, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
, we have
\hat{b}_{n}\notin\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
.
Together with the above conclusion that
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}
,
on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,
we have that

\hat{b}_{n}\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}.

(19)

We have established this under the assumption that
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}\neq\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}
;
however, as mentioned above, (
19
) is trivially
satisfied on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}

when
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}=\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}

(due to the fact that
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}
, as established above).
Thus, the conclusion that (
19
) holds on
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
is valid in either case
(defining
E_{4}
to be a vacuous event of probability one
when
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}=\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}
).

We now resume the proof for the general case
(regardless of whether
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}=\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}
or not).
Since, on the above events, we have constrained the set of possible
\hat{b}_{n}
values to
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
,
let us now argue that any of these possible
\hat{b}_{n}
values would suffice to provide a guarantee on
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})
.
Consider any
b\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
.
Recall that we have established above that, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}
, every
b\in\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
satisfies (
17
),
which, together with the definition of
\mathrm{PrbGoodB}
, implies

|\mathrm{OPTI}(b)|\geq\left(\mathbf{P}\!\left(1\in\mathrm{HasOPT}(b)\right)-\frac{1}{50}\right)|I_{b}|\geq\frac{34}{50}|I_{b}|>\frac{3}{5}|I_{b}|.

Thus, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,
together with (
19
) we have

|\mathrm{OPTI}(\hat{b}_{n})|>\frac{3}{5}|I_{\hat{b}_{n}}|.

(20)

Additionally, as is well known, for any measurable function
g:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,
for
(X,Y)\sim P
,

\mathrm{er}_{P}(g)-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\mathbf{E}\!\Big[\left|1-2\mathbf{P}(Y=1|X)\right|\cdot\mathbbold{1}\!\left[g(X)\neq h^{\star}(X)\right]\Big],

which implies that if
\mathrm{er}_{P}(g)=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})
,
then with probability one,

\mathbf{P}(Y=1|X)\neq\frac{1}{2}\implies g(X)=h^{\star}(X)
.
In particular, since every
i\in\mathrm{OPTI}(\hat{b}_{n})
has
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h_{\hat{b}_{n},i})=\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})
,
by the union bound we have that,
for
(X,Y)\sim P
(independent of
S_{n}
),
with conditional probability one (given
S_{n}
),

\mathbf{P}(Y=1|X)\neq\frac{1}{2}\implies\forall i\in\mathrm{OPTI}(\hat{b}_{n}),h_{\hat{b}_{n},i}(X)=h^{\star}(X).

Since, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,
(
20
) implies
the set
\mathrm{OPTI}(\hat{b}_{n})
contains
at least
\frac{3}{5}
of the values in
I_{\hat{b}_{n}}
,
and since
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}(x)
is the majority vote of
\{h_{\hat{b}_{n},i}(x):i\in I_{\hat{b}_{n}}\}
,
we have that on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,
for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
such that every
i\in\mathrm{OPTI}(\hat{b}_{n})
has
h_{\hat{b}_{n},i}(x)=h^{\star}(x)
,
we also have
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}(x)=h^{\star}(x)
.
Altogether, we have that, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left|1-2\mathbf{P}(Y=1|X)\right|\cdot\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{h}_{n}^{1}(X)\neq h^{\star}(X)\right]\middle|S_{n}\right]=0.

(21)

It remains to bound
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})
in terms of
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})
.
On the event
E_{1}
, if
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is defined and
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})
, then

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})+\sqrt{\frac{3\psi(n)}{2n}}\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\sqrt{\frac{3\psi(n)}{2n}}\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{2}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\sqrt{\frac{6\psi(n)}{n}},

where the first inequality is due to (
13
),
the second inequality is due to
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}^{1}_{n})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})
,
and the third inequality is again due to (
13
).
In particular, in this case, by definition the algorithm chooses
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
.
On the other hand, if
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})>\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})
,
then regardless of whether
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
or
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
,
we have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})
.
Thus, since we have argued that
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is defined on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,
and since
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})-\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right)\mathbbold{1}_{E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}}\right]+\left(1-\mathbf{P}\!\left(E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbf{P}\!\left(E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}\right)\leq 4e^{-\psi(n)}+e^{-\psi(n)}+b^{*}e^{-C_{1}n}+(b^{*}-1)e^{-C_{2}n},

where the equality on the third line is due to (
21
) and the last inequality is by the union bound
and the respective bounds on the failure probabilities of each event

E_{i}
established above.
Since
\psi(n)=o(n)
, for any sufficiently large
n
we have
\psi(n)\leq\min\{C_{1}n,C_{2}n\}
,
so that this last expression is at most
(4+2b^{*})e^{-\psi(n)}
for all sufficiently large
n
.
This completes the proof.

8
Slower Than Near-exponential is not Faster Than Super-Root

As our next component in the proof of Theorem
3
,
we turn to establishing the super-root lower bounds
for classes which shatter an infinite Littlestone tree.

Theorem 23

If
\mathcal{H}
shatters an infinite Littlestone tree, then
for every
R(n)=o(n^{-1/2})
,

\mathcal{H}
is not agnostically learnable at rate
R
.

Proof
Fix any function
\phi(n)=o(n^{-1/2})
, and without loss of generality, suppose
\phi(n)\geq\frac{1}{n}
.
For any learning algorithm
f_{n}
we will construct a distribution
P

such that for
S_{n}\sim P^{n}
and
\hat{h}_{n}=f_{n}(S_{n})
,

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\geq C\phi(n)

for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
, for a constant
C=\frac{1}{30}
.
The theorem follows immediately, by defining
\phi(n)=\max\!\left\{30R(n),\frac{1}{n}\right\}
for any given
R(n)=o(n^{-1/2})
.

The construction is similar to a proof of
Bousquet et al. 2021
for
the realizable case (establishing an
n^{-1}
rate lower bound there),
in that we construct a distribution supported along a single random branch of the Littlestone tree.
The key difference here is that we add classification noise to the labels
Y

and adjust the marginal probability distribution on
\mathcal{X}
to decrease more rapidly along the branch.
The idea is that, unlike the realizable case, since there is classification
noise in this scenario,
the Bernoulli mean testing lower bound from Lemma
8
implies
the data set must contain at least some number
of copies (growing as the mean approaches
1/2
) of a given point in the support of
P_{X}
before the learner can identify
the Bayes optimal classification of that point.

We will describe a family of distributions with marginals on
\mathcal{X}
supported on the branches of a Littlestone tree.
Specifically, consider an infinite Littlestone tree shattered by
\mathcal{H}
,
as in Definition
4
:
namely, let

\{x_{\mathbf{u}}:\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{k},0\leq k<\infty\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}

be such that for every
1\leq d<\infty
and

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{d}
,
there exists

h_{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathcal{H}
such that

h_{\mathbf{y}}(x_{y_{<k}})=y_{k}
for

1\leq k\leq d
.

We first define a sequence
\{p_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
(0,1)

(where
p_{k}
will be the marginal probability
P_{X}
at depth
k
)
and an increasing sequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
(where
n_{k}
will correspond to a sample size
n
for which a lower bound may be obtained on average).
These are defined inductively, as follows.
Define
n_{1}=0
.
Define
p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
.
Inductively, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}
,
supposing
p_{k}
and
n_{k-1}
are already defined,
let
n_{k}>n_{k-1}
be any sufficiently large value in
\mathbb{N}
satisfying

\sqrt{n_{k}}\phi(n_{k})<\sqrt{\frac{p_{k}}{16}}.

(22)

Such a value must exist since
\phi(n)=o(n^{-1/2})
.
Additionally, define
p_{k+1}
as any value in
(0,1)
satisfying

p_{k+1}<\frac{1}{4n_{k}}.

(23)

This completes the inductive definition of
\{p_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}}
and
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Recalling that
\phi(n_{k})\geq\frac{1}{n_{k}}
,
the above constraint (
22
) implies

\frac{1}{n_{k}}<\frac{p_{k}}{16}

which, together with (
23
), implies

p_{k+1}<\frac{p_{k}}{64}
,
so that every
k\geq 2
satisfies
p_{k}\leq 2^{-6(k-2)-1}
,
and hence
\sum_{k\geq 2}p_{k}<1
.
Thus, we may define
p_{1}=1-\sum_{k\geq 2}p_{k}>0
,
so that altogether the sequence
\{p_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

satisfies
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}p_{k}=1
:
that is, it defines a probability distribution over
\mathbb{N}
.

Note that, since
p_{k+1}<\frac{p_{k}}{64}
for every
k\geq 2
,
we have, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sum_{k^{\prime}>k}p_{k^{\prime}}<\sum_{k^{\prime}>k}2^{-6(k^{\prime}-k-1)}p_{k+1}=\frac{64}{63}p_{k+1}<\frac{16}{63n_{k}},

(24)

where the last inequality is due to (
23
).
Moreover, since
\phi(n_{k})\geq\frac{1}{n_{k}}
for any
k\geq 2
,
the definition of
n_{k}
in (
22
) implies

\frac{p_{k}}{16}>n_{k}\phi(n_{k})^{2}\geq\phi(n_{k}).

(25)

For each
\mathbf{y}=\{y_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

sequence of values in
\{0,1\}

(corresponding to a branch in the Littlestone tree),
we define a distribution
P_{\mathbf{y}}
with
marginal distribution on
\mathcal{X}
supported along this path.
Specifically, define
P_{\mathbf{y}}
by the property that,
for
(X,Y)\sim P_{\mathbf{y}}
,
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
the point
x_{y_{<k}}
(i.e., the point at depth
k
along the branch indicated by
\mathbf{y}
)
has
\mathbf{P}(X=x_{y_{<k}})=p_{k}
.
The conditional distribution of
Y
given
X=x_{y_{<k}}
is defined as

\mathbf{P}(Y=y_{k}|X=x_{y_{<k}})=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\phi(n_{k})}{p_{k}},

(26)

which is in the range
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{17}{32})
due to (
25
).
Since
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}p_{k}=1
,
this completely specifies a probability measure
P_{\mathbf{y}}
.

The values above are constructed to serve the following argument.
Fix any learning algorithm
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
\mathbf{y}^{\star}=\{y^{\star}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\frac{1}{2})
.
Fix any
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}
.
Conditioned on
\mathbf{y}^{\star}
,
let
S_{n_{k}}=\{(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n_{k}},Y_{n_{k}})\}\sim P_{\mathbf{y}^{\star}}^{n_{k}}

and let
\hat{h}_{n_{k}}=f_{n_{k}}(S_{n_{k}})
.
Since the marginal distribution of
P_{\mathbf{y}^{\star}}
on
\mathcal{X}

is supported entirely on the branch
\{x_{y^{\star}_{<k^{\prime}}}:k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
we may denote by
K_{i}
the
X_{i}
-dependent
\mathbb{N}
-valued random variable
with value satisfying
X_{i}=x_{y^{\star}_{<K_{i}}}
(almost surely):
that is,
K_{i}
is the
depth
of the sample
X_{i}
along the branch
\mathbf{y}^{\star}

in the Littlestone tree.

Let
N_{k}=|\{i\leq n:K_{i}=k\}|
,
and let
i_{1},\ldots,i_{N_{k}}
be the subsequence of values
i\in\{1,\ldots,n_{k}\}

for which
K_{i}=k
.
We aim to apply Lemma
8
,
with
t^{*}=y^{\star}_{k}
,
n=N_{k}
,

\gamma=\frac{\phi(n_{k})}{2p_{k}}
,

\delta=\frac{1}{8e}
,

(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})=(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{N_{k}})
,
and
\hat{t}_{n}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n})=\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})
.
For this to be a valid use of the lemma,
we must apply the lemma under a
conditional
distribution.
Specifically, in the context of the lemma,
we intend to condition on the data
(X_{i},Y_{i})
having
K_{i}\neq k
;
we must be careful in this, since any elements
(X_{i},Y_{i})
with
K_{i}>k

would cause a dependence with
y^{\star}_{k}
(in particular,
the value of such an
X_{i}
would immediately reveal the value of
y^{\star}_{k}
).
To avoid this, we will restrict to the event

\max\{K_{i}:i\leq n_{k}\}\leq k
,
in which case the data subsequence

S_{\setminus k}:=\{(X_{i},Y_{i}):i\in\{1,\ldots,n_{k}\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{N_{k}}\}\}

is conditionally independent of
y^{\star}_{k}
given this event.
In particular, on this event,

y^{\star}_{k}
remains conditionally
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\frac{1}{2})
given

N_{k}
,
i_{1},\ldots,i_{N_{k}}
, and
S_{\setminus k}
.
Also note that the random variables

Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{N_{k}}}
are conditionally
i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bernoulli}\!\left(\frac{1}{2}+(2y^{\star}_{k}-1)\frac{1}{2}\frac{\phi(n_{k})}{p_{k}}\right)

given
y^{\star}_{k}
,
N_{k}
,
i_{1},\ldots,i_{N_{k}}
, and
S_{\setminus k}
.
Thus, by Lemma
8
,
on the events that

\max\{K_{i}:i\leq n_{k}\}\leq k
and
N_{k}<\frac{p_{k}^{2}}{2\phi(n_{k})^{2}}
,
we have

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\middle|N_{k},i_{1},\ldots,i_{N_{k}},S_{\setminus k}\right)>\frac{1}{8e}.

(27)

We now turn to bounding the probabilities these events fail to hold.
First, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\max\!\left\{K_{i}:i\leq n_{k}\right\}>k\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists i\leq n_{k}:K_{i}>k\right)\leq\sum_{i\leq n_{k}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(K_{i}>k\right)=n_{k}\sum_{k^{\prime}>k}p_{k^{\prime}}<\frac{16}{63},

where the first inequality is by the union bound,
and the last inequality is due to (
24
).
Second, we lower bound the probability of
N_{k}<\frac{p_{k}^{2}}{2\phi(n_{k})^{2}}
.
Note that
N_{k}
is a
\mathrm{Binomial}(n_{k},p_{k})

random variable.
By (
22
),
we have
\mathbf{E}[N_{k}]=p_{k}n_{k}<\frac{p_{k}^{2}}{16\phi(n_{k})^{2}}
.
Therefore, a Chernoff bound implies

\mathbf{P}\!\left(N_{k}\geq\frac{p_{k}^{2}}{2\phi(n_{k})^{2}}\right)\leq\exp\!\left\{-\frac{49}{9}\frac{p_{k}^{2}}{16\phi(n_{k})^{2}}\right\}<\exp\!\left\{-\frac{49\cdot 16}{9}\right\}<\frac{1}{63},

where the second inequality is due to (
25
) which implies
\frac{p_{k}}{\phi(n_{k})}>16
.

Let us now combine the above elements into a single unconditional lower bound: namely,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\middle|N_{k},i_{1},\ldots,i_{N_{k}},S_{\setminus k}\right)\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\max\{K_{i}:i\leq n_{k}\}\leq k\text{ and }N_{k}<\frac{p_{k}^{2}}{2\phi(n_{k})^{2}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8e}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\max\{K_{i}:i\leq n_{k}\}\leq k\text{ and }N_{k}<\frac{p_{k}^{2}}{2\phi(n_{k})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8e}\left(1-\mathbf{P}\!\left(\max\{K_{i}:i\leq n_{k}\}>k\right)-\mathbf{P}\!\left(N_{k}\geq\frac{p_{k}^{2}}{2\phi(n_{k})^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{8e}\left(1-\frac{16}{63}-\frac{1}{63}\right)>\frac{1}{30},

(28)

where the second inequality is due to (
27
),
the third inequality is by the union bound,
and the fourth inequality is by the analysis of the
probabilities of these two events above.

Our next step will be to remove the randomness
in the choice of branch
\mathbf{y}^{\star}
, via Fatou’s lemma.
Conditioned on
\mathbf{y}^{\star}
,
let
(X,Y)\sim P_{\mathbf{y}^{\star}}

be conditionally independent of
S_{n_{k}}
given
\mathbf{y}^{\star}
.
Denote by
K
the
X
-dependent
\mathbb{N}
-valued random variable
satisfying
X=x_{y^{\star}_{<K}}
.
Noting that
K
is independent of
\mathbf{y}^{\star}

(since the marginal probability mass under
P_{\mathbf{y}}
of the set of all nodes at any given depth
k
is
p_{k}
, which is invariant to
\mathbf{y}^{\star}
),
we have (almost surely)

\frac{1}{p_{k}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\text{ and }K=k\middle|\mathbf{y}^{\star}\right)\leq\frac{1}{p_{k}}\mathbf{P}(K=k)=1.

In particular, this value is
bounded
(almost surely), which will enable us to make use of a
variant of Fatou’s lemma for the
\mathop{\rm limsup}
of bounded sequences of random variables.
Specifically, by applying the above analysis to each
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}
,
together with Fatou’s lemma and the law of total probability, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathop{\rm limsup}_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{p_{k}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\text{ and }K=k\middle|\mathbf{y}^{\star}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\rm limsup}_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{p_{k}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\text{ and }K=k\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm limsup}_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{p_{k}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(K=k\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm limsup}_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\right)\geq\frac{1}{30},

where the equality on the third line follows from independence of
K

from
\hat{h}_{n_{k}}
and
\mathbf{y}^{\star}
(as discussed above),
the second inequality is due to the definition of
P_{\mathbf{y}^{\star}}
(i.e.,
\mathbf{P}(K=k)=p_{k}
),
and the final inequality is due to
(
28
).
Therefore, there exists a realization of
\mathbf{y}^{\star}

such that

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y^{\star}_{<k}})\neq y^{\star}_{k}\text{ and }K=k\middle|\mathbf{y}^{\star}\right)\geq\frac{p_{k}}{30}

infinitely often.
Let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}^{\star}
for this realization:
that is,
\mathbf{y}
is a
non-random
choice of
a sequence
\mathbf{y}=\{y_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

of values in
\{0,1\}
such that
for
P=P_{\mathbf{y}}
,

S_{n}\sim P^{n}
and
\hat{h}_{n}=f_{n}(S_{n})

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
for
(X,Y)\sim P
independent of every
S_{n}
,
it holds that

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y_{<k}})\neq y_{k}\text{ and }X=x_{y_{<k}}\right)\geq\frac{p_{k}}{30}

for infinitely many
k
.

Also note that, since the Littlestone tree above is
shattered by
\mathcal{H}
, every
k\in\mathbb{N}
has some

h_{y_{\leq k}}\in\mathcal{H}
with

\forall k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
h_{y_{\leq k}}(x_{y_{<k^{\prime}}})=y_{k^{\prime}}
.
Since the marginal distribution of
P
is supported on the branch
\{x_{y_{<k}}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
letting
h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}
denote a measurable function

\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
with
h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(x_{y_{<k}})=y_{k}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have that

\displaystyle\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(h(X)\neq h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(X)\right)\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\!\left(h_{y_{\leq k}}(X)\neq h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(X)\right)=0.

Note that (
26
)
implies
h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}
is a
Bayes optimal

function:
that is, for every measurable
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(X)\neq Y\middle|X\right)\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(h(X)\neq Y\middle|X\right)
almost surely,
and therefore also
\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}\right)\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
It follows that

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}\right).

Moreover, for any measurable
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

and any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
letting
K
be as above (that is,
K
is defined to satisfy
X=x_{y_{<K}}
almost surely)

\displaystyle\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(h(X)\neq Y\middle|X\right)-\mathbf{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(X)\neq Y\middle|X\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left(\mathbf{P}\!\left(h(X)\neq Y\middle|X\right)-\mathbf{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(X)\neq Y\middle|X\right)\right)\mathbbold{1}\!\left[K=k\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\phi(n_{k})}{p_{k}}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\phi(n_{k})}{p_{k}}\right)\right)\mathbbold{1}\!\left[h(X)\neq h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(X)\right]\mathbbold{1}\!\left[K=k\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi(n_{k})}{p_{k}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(h(x_{y_{<k}})\neq y_{k}\text{ and }K=k\right),

where the equality on the fourth line follows from (
26
)
and the last equality follows from the definitions of
K
and
h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}

(i.e.,
K=k
implies
X=x_{y_{<k}}
and
h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(X)=h^{\star}_{\mathbf{y}}(x_{y_{<k}})=y_{k}
).
Altogether, we have that for infinitely many
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}\right)-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{\phi(n_{k})}{p_{k}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n_{k}}(x_{y_{<k}})\neq y_{k}\text{ and }X=x_{y_{<k}}\middle|\hat{h}_{n_{k}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{30}\phi(n_{k}).

Since
n_{k}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
,
we have established that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}\right)\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\geq\frac{1}{30}\phi(n)

for infinitely many
n
.
Since this conclusion holds for any learning algorithm
f_{n}
,
this completes the proof.

9
Super-Root Rates

As the final component needed to establish Theorem
3
,
we present the proof that classes which do not shatter an infinite VCL tree are learnable at super-root rates.
This represents by-far the most technically-challenging
proof in this work.
In particular, a key piece of the proof
requires a new analysis of universal rates for

partial
concept classes of finite VC dimension,
involving careful reasoning about relations of
conditional error rates given data prefixes
X_{\leq m}

of varying sizes.

Formally, this section is dedicated to establishing the following theorem.

Theorem 24

If
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite VCL tree, then
\mathcal{H}
is agnostically learnable with
o\!\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
rates:
that is,
there exists a learning algorithm
\hat{h}_{n}
such that,
for every distribution
P
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=o\!\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
.

The formal proof of Theorem
24

is developed in detail below.
Before getting into the details,
we first briefly outline the strategy underlying the algorithm

\hat{h}_{n}
achieving this
o\!\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
rate.
Similarly to the strategy in Theorem
22
,
we divide the approach into two subroutines:
one designed to address the case where
P
is not
Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
, which simply
trains a universally Bayes-consistent learning algorithm
\hat{h}^{0}_{n}
(Lemma
14
),
and a second algorithm
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}

designed to address the case
where
P
is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.
We then select between
\hat{h}^{0}_{n}
and
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}

using held-out data to obtain the final predictor
\hat{h}_{n}
.
Most of the technical contributions of the approach
concern the
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}
algorithm.

The strategy underlying this
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}
algorithm
is inspired by the realizable-case analysis of
Bousquet et al. 2021
.
It will be instructive to first summarize that
original strategy, before discussing the significant modifications required to extend to the agnostic setting.

The Realizable Case:

In the case of a distribution
P
realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
,
the key insight of
Bousquet et al. 2021
is that,
if
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite VCL tree,
then (by making use of a winning strategy for an associated Gale-Stewart game)
Lemma
12
supplies a procedure
for which, given a large enough i.i.d. data set
B^{b}\sim P^{b}
(
b\geq b^{*}_{\gamma}
),
with some probability
\gamma>0
the procedure produces
a value
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}

and a function
\mathbb{A}^{b,\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B^{b}):\mathcal{X}^{\mathbf{k}}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbf{k}}

for which

P^{\mathbf{k}}\!\left((x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{\mathbf{k}},y_{\mathbf{k}}):\mathbb{A}^{b,\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B^{b},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathbf{k}})\neq(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\mathbf{k}})\right)=1.

(29)

The next observation is that one can use this function

\mathbb{A}^{b,\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B^{b})
to induce a

partial concept class

\mathbb{G}
of finite VC dimension
(i.e., a class of measurable functions
g:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1,*\}
),
for which
P
is realizable.
Specifically, consider the set
\mathbb{G}
of
all
partial concepts

g:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1,*\}
with finite
support

\mathrm{supp}(g):=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:g(x)\neq*\}

such that every
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathbf{k}}\}\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(g)

satisfies
\mathbb{A}^{b,\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B^{b},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathbf{k}})\neq(g(x_{1}),\ldots,g(x_{\mathbf{k}}))
.
Then note that, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
for
\{(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{m},Y_{m})\}\sim P^{m}
,
it follows from (
29
),
and a union bound over the
\binom{m}{\mathbf{k}}
subsets of size
\mathbf{k}

that, with probability one,
\exists g\in\mathbb{G}

with
(g(X_{1}),\ldots,g(X_{m}))=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})
.
We refer to this scenario by saying
P
is
realizable
with respect to
\mathbb{G}
.
Moreover, by its definition,
the VC dimension of
\mathbb{G}
is at most
\mathbf{k}-1

(i.e., for any
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathcal{X}
,
no
g\in\mathbb{G}
realizes the labels

\mathbb{A}^{b,\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B^{b},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\mathbf{k}})
),
which we may coarsely bound by
b
since
\mathbf{k}\leq b

(by definition in Lemma
12
).

Bousquet et al. 2021
then use a held-out training set of size
\Omega(n)
,
applying a general realizable-case learning algorithm
for partial concept classes
\mathbb{G}
of finite VC dimension (namely, the
one-inclusion graph
predictor of
Haussler et al. 1994
),
to obtain a conditional expected error guarantee
O\!\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)

given
B^{b}
.

Some technicalities need be addressed for this strategy to succeed:
namely, choosing a size
b
sufficiently large
(
b\geq b^{*}_{\gamma}
) for Lemma
12
to be applicable (recalling
b^{*}_{\gamma}
is a
P
-dependent value),
and addressing the fact that the property (
29
) (and hence realizability of
P
with respect to
\mathbb{G}
)
only holds with a constant probability
\gamma>0
.
These are addressed via similar techniques as in
the proof of Theorem
22
,
by using multiple (
\propto n/b
)
independent batches
B^{b}_{i}
of size
b

(with a
\gamma>1/2
),
and aggregating the resulting predictors via a majority vote,
and selecting the batch size
b
using additional held-out data.

The Agnostic Case:

As mentioned above, the algorithm
\hat{h}^{1}_{n}
will
only need to address the case where
P
is
Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
:
that is,
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
In particular, due to Lemmas
16
,
17
, and
18
,
in this case there exists a measurable function

h^{\star}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

with
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

and
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=0
.
This further implies that, if we denote by
P_{h^{\star}}
the
distribution of
(X,h^{\star}(X))
(for
X\sim P_{X}
),

P_{h^{\star}}
is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.

Several aspects of the above realizable-case strategy are
made more challenging in the case when
P

is merely
Bayes-realizable
with respect to
\mathcal{H}

rather than realizable.
First, since
P
is not necessarily
realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
,
we cannot simply apply the procedure from Lemma
12

to induce an appropriate partial concept class
\mathbb{G}
.
We address this issue
similarly to the use of Lemma
10
in the proof of Theorem
22
.
Specifically,
letting
B^{b}=\{(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{b},Y_{b})\}\sim P^{b}
,
we consider applying the procedure from Lemma
12

under all
2^{b}

relabelings
of
B^{b}
:
that is, for each
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{b})\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,
we let
B^{b}(\mathbf{y}):=\{(X_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{b},y_{b})\}
,
and apply the procedure from Lemma
12

to obtain a
\mathbf{k}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b}\in\mathbb{N}

and function
\mathbb{A}^{b,\mathbf{k}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b}}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B^{b}(\mathbf{y})):\mathcal{X}^{\mathbf{k}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b}}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbf{k}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b}}
,
which induces a partial concept class
\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b}

of VC dimension
\mathbf{k}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b}-1
as above.
We then define a partial concept class

\mathbb{G}_{b}:=\bigcup_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b}
.
In particular, since each
\mathbf{k}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b}\leq b

(by definition),
we can argue that this partial concept class
\mathbb{G}
will have VC dimension
O(b)
.
Moreover, since one of these
\mathbf{y}
labelings
satisfies
\mathbf{y}=h^{\star}(X_{\leq b})
,
and the distribution
P_{h^{\star}}

is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
,
the guarantee from Lemma
12
provides that,
with probability
\gamma>0
,

P_{h^{\star}}
is also realizable with respect to
\mathbb{G}_{b}
.

Given the above partial concept class
\mathbb{G}_{b}
,
we arrive at what is perhaps the most challenging part of the proof:
namely, we require a learning rule for partial concept classes
\mathbb{G}
of finite VC dimension achieving
o(n^{-1/2})

rate for all distributions Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathbb{G}
.
Such a learning rule is not known in the literature (indeed, even
n^{-1/2}
is not known; see
Alon et al. 2021
),
and thus the design and analysis of such a learner is itself a novel contribution of this work.
Fortunately, the Bayes-realizability condition
turns out to make this problem approachable by a natural
learning strategy based on
transductive
empirical risk minimization: that is, for a data set
S
of size
n
,
plus a test point
x
we wish to predict for,
we consider the set of all classifications of
S
plus
x

realizable by
\mathbb{G}
, and among these we choose the classification having minimum empirical error rate on the
first
n/2
examples in
S
, and predict the label of
x

in this classification.
The analysis of this method, showing the
o(n^{-1/2})
rate,
relies on reasoning about the problem
in the framework of
transductive
learning,
arguing that any reasonably-good function in the class
has a near-Bayes function relatively close to it in empirical
L_{1}
distance (specifically, at a distance vanishing as
n\to\infty
), so that a
uniform Bernstein inequality implies an excess risk which decreases slightly faster than
n^{-1/2}
.
This reasoning focuses on concentration of
differences of empirical error rates around their
conditional
expectations given
X_{\leq n}
,
together with concentration of such conditional expectations
(and related quantities)
as a function of the sample size
n
.

With the
o(n^{-1/2})
rate thereby established
for any partial concept class of finite VC dimension,
we can use this learning rule in combination with the
partial concept class
\mathbb{G}_{b}
induced by the first part above.
As with the realizable case,
for this strategy to be successful,
we need to address some additional technicalities:
most notably, the fact that the realizability of

P_{h^{\star}}
with respect to
\mathbb{G}_{b}
only holds with
a constant probability
\gamma>0
,
and the fact that this guarantee only holds for
choices of batch size
b\geq b^{*}_{\gamma}

(a
P_{h^{\star}}
-dependent value).
We address these analogously to the realizable-case strategy,
though the argument is significantly more nuanced
in the case of non-realizable distributions.
Specifically, rather than a single batch
B^{b}
,
we use multiple (
\propto n/b
)
independent batches
B^{b}_{i}
of size
b
,
each of which leads to a partial concept class
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}

and corresponding predictor
\hat{h}_{n,i}

via the above strategy (with a
\gamma>1/2
),
and we aggregate the resulting predictors
\hat{h}_{n,i}

by a majority vote
(which, we argue, retains the
o(n^{-1/2})
rate guarantee,
using the Bayes-realizability of
P
).
To select an appropriate batch size
\hat{b}_{n}
,
we use a held-out data set
S^{1}_{n}
of size
\Omega(n)

to compare the minimal empirical error rates

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S^{1}_{n}}(\mathbb{G}_{b,i})

achievable by partial concepts in
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
,
and select the smallest
b
for which
a significant fraction of indices
i

have
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S^{1}_{n}}(\mathbb{G}_{b,i})

not much larger than
every

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S^{1}_{n}}(\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}})
,
b^{\prime}>b
.
We argue that such a selection
\hat{b}_{n}

will indeed (with high probability)
result in
P_{h^{\star}}
being Bayes-realizable
with respect to most of the
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
classes,
so that the above guarantees on the
resulting predictors
\hat{h}_{n,i}
are valid.

We present the formal details in the subsections below,
beginning with the proof of
o(n^{-1/2})
rates
for partial concept classes of finite VC dimension
in the case of Bayes-realizable
P

(Section
9.1
).
We then present the main proof of Theorem
24

in Section
9.2
.

9.1
Super-root Rates for Partial Concept Classes of Finite VC Dimension

As discussed above, as one of the key steps in the proof of Theorem
24
,
we will need a
o(n^{-1/2})
rate guarantee for any
partial
concept class of finite VC dimension, under the assumption that
P
is
Bayes-realizable
(defined formally below). Such partial concept classes arise in the proof of Theorem
24
with data-dependent definitions,
having bounded VC dimension with high probability.

We first introduce some notation for partial concept classes
based on the works of
Alon et al. 2021
;
Bartlett and Long 1998
;
Long 2001
.
A
partial concept
is a function

g:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1,*\}
,
where we interpret ‘
*
’ as meaning the value is “undefined”.
For any partial concept
g
, the
support
of
g

is defined as the set
\{x\in\mathcal{X}:g(x)\in\{0,1\}\}
.
A
partial concept class

\mathbb{G}
is a set of partial concepts.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}
,
denote by

\mathbb{G}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\{(g(x_{1}),\ldots,g(x_{n})):g\in\mathbb{G}\}\cap\{0,1\}^{n},

that is,
the set of
\mathbb{G}
-realizable
binary
classifications of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
For ease of notation, for a sequence

S=\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n},y_{n})\}\in(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{n}
,
we also define
\mathbb{G}(S)=\mathbb{G}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.
We will say
\mathbb{G}
is
universally measurable
if the
function
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto\mathbb{G}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

is universally measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
In all contexts below,
we restrict our focus to universally measurable
partial concept classes; for brevity,
we omit this explicit qualification below,
simply using the term “partial concept class” to mean
“universally measurable partial concept class”.

The VC dimension of
\mathbb{G}
, denoted by
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
is the maximum
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
such that

\exists S_{X}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
with
\mathbb{G}(S_{X})=\{0,1\}^{n}
,
or else
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})=\infty
if no such maximum exists
(
Bartlett and Long 1998
;
Long 2001
;
Alon et al. 2021
)
.

For any partial concept class
\mathbb{G}
,
we say a distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
is
Bayes-realizable
with respect to
\mathbb{G}
if,

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
, for
S=\{(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})\}\sim P^{n}
, it holds that
2
2

2

It is a straightforward exercise to verify that this coincides with the definition of Bayes-realizable for total concept classes introduced above, in the special case that
\mathbb{G}
is a total concept class which does not shatter an infinite VCL tree (follows from Lemmas
16
,
17
, and
18
), though this will not be important for our purposes.

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{G}(S):\forall t\leq n,\mathbf{P}(Y_{t}=y_{t}|X_{t})\geq\frac{1}{2}\right)=1.

We also denote by
h^{\star}_{P}
a
Bayes optimal
classifier: namely, for
(X,Y)\sim P
, for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
, define

h^{\star}_{P}(x):=\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\mathbf{P}(Y=1|X=x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\right].

Alon et al. 2021
study agnostic learning for partial concept classes, where they find that the appropriate notion
“excess error rate” for a predictor
\hat{h}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
relative to a partial concept class
\mathbb{G}
is

\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P}(\mathbb{G})
,
where
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\mathbb{G})=\sup_{n}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbold{1}[g(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]\right]
,
for
(X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2}),\ldots
independent
P
-distributed random variables.
They propose a learning rule guaranteeing
a uniform bound of order
\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log^{2}(n)}{n}}
,
and it remains open whether this can be improved to

\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}}
.
Fortunately, for our purposes (in the proof of Theorem
24
),
we can focus on the case where
P

is
Bayes-realizable
with respect to
\mathbb{G}
,
which greatly simplifies the problem of designing learning rules with appropriate convergence rate guarantees.
In this context (and assuming
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})<\infty
), one can show that
the definition of
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\mathbb{G})

simplifies to merely
\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{P}\right)

(follows from Theorem 14 of
Alon et al. 2021
and
Lemma
29
below),
and thus we can simply aim to
bound
\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{P}\right)

for an appropriate predictor
\hat{h}
.

Formally, we have the following result, which will be a central part
of the proof of Theorem
24
,
and is also of independent interest.

Lemma 25

Let
\mathbb{G}
be any partial concept class with
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})<\infty
.
There exists a sequence
\{\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of universally measurable functions

\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

(defined explicitly below)
with the following property.
For any distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}

that is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathbb{G}
,
there exists a function
\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)=o\!\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
such that
\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)\leq c\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}}
(for a universal constant
c
), and
for
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
independent
P
-distributed random variables,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})\right)\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{P}\right)\leq\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P).

We construct the learning rule
\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}
for Lemma
25
explicitly,
based on the classic method of
transductive empirical risk minimization

(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1974
;
Vapnik 1998
)
as follows.
Let
\mathbb{G}
be a partial concept class
with
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})<\infty
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define a
function
\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\times\mathcal{X}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}\to\{0,1\}^{n+1}

(called
transductive empirical risk minimization
) with the property that,
for any
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\lceil n/2\rceil})\in\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
and

x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1})=\!\!\mathop{\rm argmin}_{(g(x_{1}),\ldots,g(x_{n+1}))\in\mathbb{G}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1})}\!\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\!\mathbbold{1}[g(x_{i})\!\neq\!y_{i}].

In other words,
\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}
selects a classification of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1}
among
\mathbb{G}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1})
which minimizes the empirical error rate on the first
\lceil n/2\rceil
examples.
For a returned sequence

(\hat{g}(x_{1}),\ldots,\hat{g}(x_{n+1}))=\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\lceil n/2\rceil},x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1})
,
for each
i\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,n\!+\!1\}

we denote by
3
3

3

Note that if a value
x_{i}
occurs multiple times in the sequence,
\hat{g}(x_{i})
is necessarily the same for each (since
\hat{g}
is consistent with some
g\in\mathbb{G}
), so that there is no ambiguity expressing this as a function of
x_{i}
rather than
i
.

\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\lceil n/2\rceil},x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1})(x_{i})=\hat{g}(x_{i}).

Though it does not affect our analysis,
for completeness we may define
\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}

to return the classification
(0,\ldots,0)
in the event that
\mathbb{G}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1})=\emptyset
.
More importantly for our analysis, we require that the
\mathop{\rm argmin}
in the definition of
\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}
breaks ties
in any way that ensures universal measurability of
\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}

and

which has no dependence on the
order
of
x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1}
,
4
4

4

For instance, this may be achieved
by first constructing the order statistic

x_{(1)},\ldots,x_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1)}

of the sequence

x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1}

based on a measurable total ordering of
\mathcal{X}

(to obscure the original order)
before applying any measurable tie-breaking strategy
for evaluating
\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},x_{(1)},\ldots,x_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1)})
,
and then reversing this re-ordering to produce the output sequence for

\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1})
.

meaning any bijection
\pi:\{\lceil n/2\rceil+1,\ldots,n+1\}\to\{\lceil n/2\rceil+1,\ldots,n+1\}
satisfies that if

(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n+1})=\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1})

then

\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},x_{\pi(\lceil n/2\rceil+1)},\ldots,x_{\pi(n+1)})\!=\!(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\pi(\lceil n/2\rceil+1)},\ldots,y_{\pi(n+1)}).

We then define the learning rule
\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}
satisfying Lemma
25
explicitly as follows.
For any
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n},y_{n})\in\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
and
x_{n+1}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq n},y_{\leq n},x_{n+1})=\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(x_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},x_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,x_{n+1})(x_{n+1}).

(30)

In other words, the algorithm ignores the labels of the last

\lfloor n/2\rfloor
training examples, and runs transductive ERM.
The intuitive reasoning behind this approach is that,
due to the
above order-invariance property and exchangeability of the i.i.d. data sequence,
letting
\hat{g}=\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},Y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},X_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},\ldots,X_{n+1})
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})\right)-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})\right]=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{g}(X_{n+1})\neq Y_{n+1}\right]-\mathbbold{1}\!\left[h^{\star}_{P}(X_{n+1})\neq Y_{n+1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}\sum_{i=\lceil n/2\rceil+1}^{n+1}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{g}(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}\right]-\mathbbold{1}\!\left[h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}\sum_{i=\lceil n/2\rceil+1}^{n+1}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{g}(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}\right]-\mathbbold{1}\!\left[h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}\right]\right],

so that we can focus the analysis on the
excess empirical loss on the entire half-sample
(X_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},Y_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1}),\ldots,(X_{n+1},Y_{n+1})
.
Moreover, the learner minimizes the empirical loss on the first
half-sample
(X_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},Y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil})
.
By exchangeability, this first half-sample can be viewed as a random sample (without replacement) from the full data set
(X_{\leq n+1},Y_{\leq n+1})
,
so that (by uniform concentration for sampling without replacement)
it is unlikely that the excess empirical loss on the second half-sample

(X_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},Y_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1}),\ldots,(X_{n+1},Y_{n+1})

is significantly different from that on the first half-sample

(X_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil},Y_{\leq\lceil n/2\rceil})
.
This latter type of analysis is common to the literature on

transductive
learning, a setting in which the data sequence

(X_{\leq n+1},Y_{\leq n+1})
is considered fixed, and the learner
observes all of
X_{\leq n+1}
along with the labels
Y_{i}

for a uniform random (without replacement)
sample of
m<n+1
of the indices
i\leq n+1
,
and performance is measured by the empirical error on the remaining
n+1-m
samples
(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1974
;
Vapnik 1998
)
.
In the case of i.i.d. samples, by exchangeability the
m
observable labels can be
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}
without loss of generality.

Outline of the proof of Lemma
25
:

It remains to prove that the properties claimed in the lemma are indeed satisfied by this learning rule
\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}
.
We briefly outline the argument, before proceeding with the formal details.
As mentioned above, the core idea is to proceed by analysis of the
transductive

learning problem: namely, it suffices to argue that the
excess empirical error rate on
(X_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1},Y_{\lceil n/2\rceil+1}),\ldots,(X_{n+1},Y_{n+1})
is
O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}}\right)
and
o(n^{-1/2})
in expectation,
in which case the same is true of
the expected excess error rate
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})\right)\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})

by the above argument.

The
\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}}
bound follows from an
extension of the classic
chaining
analysis to transductive
learning, and is known
(
El-Yaniv and Pechyony 2009
, e.g.,)
.
However, the
o(n^{-1/2})
bound is significantly more involved.
In essence, this part of the proof follows a
localization
-based analysis for empirical risk minimization (see e.g.,
Bartlett et al. 2004
;
Bartlett et al. 2005
;
Koltchinskii 2006
).
However, the details are made more challenging
due to the strictly empirical nature of the transductive setting.
While extensions of localization-based analysis are known for transductive
learning
(
Tolstikhin et al. 2014
)
, the key component enabling the
o(n^{-1/2})
rate
does not follow from prior works.

Total concept classes:

To outline the basic idea of the analysis, consider first how this analysis would proceed
in the much simpler case of a
total
concept class
\mathcal{H}
of finite VC dimension,
for the standard (inductive)
empirical risk minimization
algorithm:
namely,

\hat{h}_{n}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}}(h)
,
for
S_{n}=\{(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})\}
i.i.d.
P
.
A rate of
o(n^{-1/2})
for this method is established in the work of
Hanneke and Xu 2025
, by the following argument.
The first key component is the well-known
uniform Bernstein inequality

for excess error rates
(
Bousquet 2002
;
van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
, based on)
,
which states that,
with probability at least
1-\delta
,
every
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy that

\displaystyle\left|\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}}(g)\right)-\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(f)-\mathrm{er}_{P}(g)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{P_{X}(x:f(x)\neq g(x))\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{X}(x:f(x)\neq g(x))}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

(31)

Together with the fact that
\hat{h}_{n}
has minimal
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}}(\hat{h}_{n})
,
it follows that with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n}
,

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\leq\epsilon^{\prime}_{n}
for a value
\epsilon^{\prime}_{n}=O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})+\log(n)}{n}}\right)
.
Denote by
\mathcal{H}(\epsilon):=\left\{h\in\mathcal{H}:\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\inf_{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\prime})\leq\epsilon\right\}
for all
\epsilon>0
,
and, for a value
\tilde{\epsilon}_{n}=O\!\left(\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)\right)
,
define

\sigma^{2}_{n}:=\tilde{\epsilon}_{n}\lor\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n})}\inf_{h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(\tilde{\epsilon}_{n})}P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\prime}(x)).

Then, again based on the inequality (
31
), this further implies that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\lesssim\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{n}\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{n}}\right)\right)},

(32)

As a second key component,
by a simple topological argument,
using the fact that total concept classes
\mathcal{H}
of finite VC dimension are
totally bounded
in the pseudo-metric
(f,g)\mapsto P_{X}(x:f(x)\neq g(x))
,

Hanneke and Xu 2025
argue that
\sigma^{2}_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
,
establishing the rate
o(n^{-1/2})
.

Partial concept classes:

There are several challenges in extending this analysis to
partial
concept classes
\mathbb{G}
.
As a first issue,
abbreviating by
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbbold{1}[g(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
we
cannot
rely on concentration of the empirical error rates

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}(g)
to the population error rate
\mathrm{er}_{P}(g)
for
g\in\mathbb{G}
: for instance, there may be partial concepts
g\in\mathbb{G}

whose support is precisely restricted to the data
X_{\leq n+1}
,
so that even if they have low empirical error
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{\lceil n/2\rceil}(g)
,
they may have
g(X)=*
with probability one for an independent
X\sim P_{X}
.
Thus, we need a purely
empirical
analysis,
which only argues about performance
on the data
.
Naturally, we may consider replacing
\mathrm{er}_{P}(g)

with the empirical loss
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(g)

on the data set
(X_{\leq n+1},Y_{\leq n+1})
,
which at least avoids the above issue of the supports.
Likewise, we may replace
P_{X}(x:f(x)\neq g(x))

with the empirical distances
P_{n+1}(f\neq g):=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i})\neq g(X_{i})]
.
However, the second issue, which is not addressed by this,
is the argument that
\sigma^{2}_{n}\to 0
. This is inherently a
property of a
population

P
.
Extending this aspect of the argument is significantly more challenging,
and most of the proof will be dedicated toward this issue.

Our approach to handling this is in two parts,
separating the analysis of the random
Y_{i}

labels from the analysis of the random
X_{i}
sequence.
We outline these parts briefly here,
and explain each part more explicitly after,
and in full detail in Section
9.1.2
.
As the first part,
we provide a concentration inequality analogous to the
uniform Bernstein inequality in (
31
),
but rather than concentration of differences of empirical errors

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)
around
differences of
population
errors
for
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq m})
,
we instead argue concentration around
differences
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(f)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)

of the respective
conditional
error rates given
the
X_{i}
’s:

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g):=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)\middle|X_{\leq m}\right]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq g(X_{i})|X_{i}),

a quantity we refer to as the
semi-empirical
error rate.
From there, as the second part of the analysis,
we focus on the randomness in the
X_{i}
sequence.
In this context, it turns out the relevant quantity to replace
\sigma^{2}_{n}

is the maximum
empirical
distance
P_{n+1}(g\neq g^{\prime})

from any
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})

with
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\lesssim\epsilon_{n}=\tilde{O}\!\left(n^{-1/2}\right)

to the closest
g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})

having
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(g^{\prime})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\lesssim\epsilon_{n}^{2}

(see
\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}
defined below).
To show this distance is small, we again must proceed by arguments
purely based on the data sequence.
Toward this end, we imagine the
(X_{\leq n+1},Y_{\leq n+1})
data as a prefix of
an infinite i.i.d. data sequence
(X_{<\infty},Y_{<\infty})
,
and reason that, for some diverging sequence
M_{n}\leq n
and some
n^{\prime}=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{2})
,
for each classification
g
in
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})

with
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq\epsilon_{n}
,
there exists a classification
g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n^{\prime}})

with
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime}}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime}}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq\epsilon_{n^{\prime}}

such that
g
and
g^{\prime}

agree
on a
prefix

X_{\leq M_{n}}
.
Together with reasoning about
uniform concentration for sampling without replacement,
this leads to the desired result.

More explicitly,
for the first part,
by an extension of the proof of (
31
)
to independent but
non-identically distributed
samples (Proposition
26
below),
we are able to prove
(in Lemma
29
)
a variant of the uniform Bernstein inequality
(
31
),
establishing that,
with conditional probability at least
1-\delta
(given

X_{\leq m}
), every
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq m})
satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)\right)-\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(f)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{P_{m}(f\neq g)\frac{1}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{m}(f\neq g)}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right),

(33)

where

P_{m}(f\neq g):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i})\neq g(X_{i})].

Letting
m=\lceil n/2\rceil
,
it follows from (
33
)
that
