\mathbf{E}\!\left[\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}))-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\middle|X_{\leq m}\right]=O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{m}}\right).

Moreover, since

\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq g(X_{i})|X_{i})-\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})|X_{i})\in[0,1]
for every
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})
and
i\leq n+1
,
we can employ a uniform
multiplicative
Chernoff bound
for sampling without replacement (Lemma
31
)
to find that,
with probability at least
1-\delta
,
every
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})
satisfies

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq 2\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)+O\!\left(\frac{1}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)\right).

(34)

A combination of these two facts already recovers
(by exchangeability of
X_{m+1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
)
the claimed guarantee

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})\right)\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})=O\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}}\right).

Turning to the issue of extending the
o(n^{-1/2})
argument to partial concept classes,
for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
\epsilon>0
,
let
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{t}(\epsilon):=\{g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq t}):\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{t}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{t}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq\epsilon\}
.
Letting
\epsilon_{t}=\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{t}\log\!\left(\frac{t}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)}
,
we define (for appropriate universal constants
c,c^{\prime}
)

\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{t}:=\epsilon_{t}^{2}\lor\max_{g\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{t}(c\epsilon_{t})}\min_{f\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{t}(c^{\prime}\epsilon_{t}^{2})}P_{t}(f\neq g).

Then, analogously to (
32
),
a localization argument based on
(
33
),
together with (
34
)
and an analogous bound relating
P_{m}(g\neq g^{\prime})
to
P_{n+1}(g\neq g^{\prime})
,
implies
(Lemma
33
)
that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}\!\left(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})\right)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\right]\lesssim\sqrt{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]}\right)\right)}.

(35)

Since (
33
) and (
34
)
imply
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}\!\left(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})\right)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq c\epsilon_{n+1}
with probability
1-O\!\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
,
the above inequality will suffice for the
o(n^{-1/2})
guarantee if we can
argue that
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\right]\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
The latter is perhaps the most challenging
aspect of this analysis.
The argument for total concept classes,
showing
\sigma^{2}_{n}\to 0

(
Hanneke and Xu 2025
)
,
relies on the topology of the total concept class
\mathcal{H}

induced by the
the
L_{1}(P_{X})
pseudo-metric:
namely, total boundedness of
\mathcal{H}
.
In the case of partial concept classes, these arguments
do not apply, and we must formulate an argument
based purely on the
empirical
behaviors of the
patterns in
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
.

Our argument establishing this employs several novel techniques,
which may be of independent interest.
Specifically, we first provide a uniform concentration
inequality for sampling without replacement
(via a technique we call
sample size chaining
),
guaranteeing that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
with

m\leq n
,
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
,
every
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
with
f(X_{\leq m})=g(X_{\leq m})

have
P_{n}(f\neq g)\lesssim\epsilon_{m}
(Lemma
35
).
Thus, denoting by
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon)\big|_{m}:=\{g(X_{\leq m}):g\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon)\}
(the projection of
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon)
to
X_{\leq m}
),
if we can argue that there exists a sequence

M_{n}\to\infty
such that, with probability
1-O\big(\frac{1}{M_{n}}\big)
, we have

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c\epsilon_{n})\big|_{M_{n}}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c^{\prime}\epsilon_{n}^{2})\big|_{M_{n}}
,
then together with the above we will have that

\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\leq\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}

with probability
1-O\big(\frac{1}{M_{n}}\big)
.

Toward establishing this, as mentioned above,
we first imagine the sequence

(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})
to be a
prefix
of a (hypothetical)

infinite
i.i.d. sequence

(X_{<\infty},Y_{<\infty})\sim P^{\infty}
.
Then note that,
for any outcome of the data sequence
X_{<\infty}
,
there must exist an
\hat{N}_{m}\geq m
such that every

n\geq\hat{N}_{m}
has infinitely many
t>n
with

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{t}(c\epsilon_{t})\big|_{m}=\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c\epsilon_{n})\big|_{m}
: to see this, simply take
\hat{N}_{m}

one larger than the largest
n\geq m

for which
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c\epsilon_{n})\big|_{m}
appears finitely many
times in the sequence of sets
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{t}(c\epsilon_{t})\big|_{m}

(noting that
\hat{N}_{m}
is well-defined since there are only finitely many possible subsets of
\{0,1\}^{m}
).
We may then define
N_{m}
as a
(\mathbb{G},P)
-dependent
sequence such that, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
,

\hat{N}_{m}\leq N_{m}
.
This implies that for any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}

with
n\geq N_{m}
, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
,
there exist arbitrarily large values
n^{\prime}

with
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}(c\epsilon_{n^{\prime}})\big|_{m}=\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c\epsilon_{n})\big|_{m}

(Lemma
36
).
In particular,
taking such a value
n^{\prime}
with
n^{\prime}=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{2})
,
we have
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}(c\epsilon_{n^{\prime}})\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}(c\epsilon_{n}^{2})
.
Moreover, by additional uniform concentration arguments
for sampling without replacement
(Lemmas
31
and
35
,
the latter again based on sample size chaining),
we show that with probability at least
1-O\!\left(\frac{1}{m}\right)
,
any such
n^{\prime}=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{2})

has
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}(c\epsilon_{n}^{2})\big|_{n}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c^{\prime}\epsilon_{n}^{2})
.
Altogether, for any
n\geq N_{m}
,
with probability at least

1-O\!\left(\frac{1}{m}\right)
, we have

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c\epsilon_{n})\big|_{m}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c^{\prime}\epsilon_{n}^{2})\big|_{m}

(Lemma
37
).
Thus, letting
M_{n}=\max\{m:n\geq N_{m}\}
,
combining the above arguments yields that

\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\lesssim\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}

with probability at least
1-O\big(\frac{1}{M_{n}}\big)
,
so that
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\right]\lesssim\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}+\frac{1}{M_{n}}
(Lemma
38
),
which indeed establishes
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\right]\to 0

by observing that
M_{n}\to\infty
.
Combining this with (
35
)
completes the proof of Lemma
25
.

We present the details of this argument in a series of lemmas below.

9.1.1
A Uniform Bernstein Inequality for Non-identically Distributed Data

Before presenting the proof of Lemma
25
,
we first provide a general concentration inequality,
which extends the
uniform Bernstein inequality
— stated in (
31
) above —
to the case of independent non-identically distributed random variables.
In addition to being a crucial part of the proof of Lemma
25
,
this result is also useful in other contexts,
such as multi-task learning
(
Hanneke and Kpotufe 2022
, e.g.,)

or learning with distribution shifts
(
Barve and Long
;
Mohri and noz Medina 2012
;
Hanneke and Yang 2019
, e.g.,)
.
In particular, it refines a
\log
factor in Lemma 1 of
Hanneke and Kpotufe 2022
(stated in Lemma
27
below).
As such, we state the result in a slightly stronger form than
needed for our purposes in the present work.
Its proof follows a familiar line from the literature on
localized uniform concentration inequalities, adapted to
account for non-identically distributed samples.

Proposition 26

Let
\mathcal{H}
be any concept class with
\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})<\infty
.
Fix any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in(0,1)
.
Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
be any probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}

and denote by
\bar{P}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{i}
(the uniform mixture distribution).
Let
S=\{(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})\}\sim P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n}
.
For every
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
define
\sigma^{2}(f,g)=\bar{P}((x,y):f(x)\neq g(x))
,

\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i})\neq g(X_{i})]
,
and
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)=\sigma^{2}(f,g)\land\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)
.
Also define

\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta):=\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)
.
Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,
every
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(f)-\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(g)\right)-\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(g)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{n}\!\left(\!\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)}\!\land\!\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)\!+\!\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)

and
\dfrac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}(f,g)-c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)\leq\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)\leq 2\sigma^{2}(f,g)+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)
,

where
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\geq 1
is a universal constant.

As mentioned, a slightly weaker form of the above proposition
(insufficient for our purposes in the present work) was given in the
work of
Hanneke and Kpotufe 2022
: namely,
they show the following lemma.

Lemma 27
(
Hanneke and Kpotufe 2022
)

Consider the same setup as in Proposition
26
.
With probability at least
1-\delta
,
every
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(f)-\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(g)\right)-\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(g)\right)\right|\leq\sqrt{\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)}+C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)

and
\dfrac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}(f,g)-C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)\leq\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)\leq 2\sigma^{2}(f,g)+C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)
,

where
C\geq 1
is a universal constant.

Our proof of Proposition
26

will directly rely on the portion of this lemma relating
\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)
and
\sigma^{2}(f,g)
,
rather than retracing the proof of this part
(we include this relation in the statement of Proposition
26
merely for the convenience of unifying the events).

We additionally rely on another basic result,
stated by
Hanneke and Kpotufe 2022

(as equation 30 therein, specialized to our present context by taking

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
,

\mathcal{F}=\{(x,y)\mapsto\mathbbold{1}[f(x)\neq y]-\mathbbold{1}[g(x)\neq y]:f,g\in\mathcal{H}\}
,
and all
\alpha_{i}=1
, in their notation),
which follows immediately from a result of

Klein and Rio 2005
(see also Section 12.5 of
Boucheron et al. 2013
),
a variant of a concentration inequality of

Bousquet 2002
generalized to
handle independent but non-identically distributed samples.

Lemma 28
(
Klein and Rio 2005
;
Hanneke and Kpotufe 2022
)

Consider the same setup as in Proposition
26
.
For any
\beta>0
,
letting
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}=\{(f,g)\in\mathcal{H}^{2}:n\sigma^{2}(f,g)\leq\beta^{2}\}

and

L_{\beta}=n\cdot\sup_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}\left(\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(g)\right)-\left(\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(f)-\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(g)\right)\right)
,
with probability at least
1-\delta
,

L_{\beta}\leq\mathbf{E}[L_{\beta}]+24\max\!\left\{\sqrt{\left(\mathbf{E}[L_{\beta}]+\beta^{2}\right)\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)},\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right\}.

We now proceed with the proof of Proposition
26
.

Proof of Proposition
26

We adapt a well-known technique for obtaining similar inequalities
in the case of i.i.d. samples (moreover, the essential difference compared to
the proof of
Hanneke and Kpotufe 2022
is merely the use of
a sharper inequality, based on chaining, to refine the log factor).
The general idea applies a combination of localization
(
Bartlett et al. 2004
;
Bartlett et al. 2005
;
Koltchinskii 2006
)
,
the concentration inequality from Lemma
28
,
and an entropy integral bound on the rate of uniform convergence which also accounts for variances of loss differences
(
van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
;
Giné and Koltchinskii 2006
;
van der Vaart and Wellner 2011
)
,
together with well-known bounds on the covering numbers of VC classes
(
Haussler 1995
)
.

The claimed inequality is vacuously true for
n<e^{2}\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})
(for
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\geq e^{2}
)
or
\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})=0
(i.e.,
|\mathcal{H}|=1
);
to address the remaining case, for the remainder of the proof
we suppose
n\geq e^{2}\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})>0
.

Fix any
\delta\in(0,1)
and any
\beta>0
,
let
\delta_{\beta}=\frac{\delta\beta^{2}}{4n}
,
and let
L_{\beta}
be as in Lemma
28
.
By Lemma
28
,
on an event
E_{\beta}
of probability at least

1-\delta_{\beta}
,

L_{\beta}\leq\mathbf{E}[L_{\beta}]+24\max\!\left\{\sqrt{\left(\mathbf{E}[L_{\beta}]+\beta^{2}\right)\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\beta}}\right)},\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\beta}}\right)\right\}.

(36)

Next we turn to bounding
\mathbf{E}[L_{\beta}]
.
Let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}
be independent

\mathrm{Uniform}(\{-1,1\})
random variables
(also independent of
S
).
By the classic
symmetrization
argument (see e.g., Lemma 11.4 of
Boucheron et al. 2013
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[L_{\beta}\right]\leq 2\mathbf{E}\!\left[\sup_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\left(\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]-\mathbbold{1}[g(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]\right)\right].

(37)

Next we apply an inequality based on the
classic
chaining
argument:
specifically, Corollary 2.2.8 of
van der Vaart and Wellner 1996
.
For each
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
define a random variable

Z_{h}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\mathbbold{1}[h(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]
.
Note that, for any
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
,
for any
\gamma>0
,
since each
\left|\xi_{i}\left(\mathbbold{1}[h(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]-\mathbbold{1}[g(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]\right)\right|\leq\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i})\neq g(X_{i})]
,
Hoeffding’s inequality implies that

\mathbf{P}\!\left(|Z_{f}-Z_{g}|>\gamma\Big|S\right)\leq 2e^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}/n\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)}.

Therefore, letting
\hat{D}(\gamma)
denote the

\gamma
-packing number
of
\mathcal{H}

(for any
\gamma>0
)
under the pseudo-metric
(f,g)\mapsto\sqrt{n\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)}
,
Corollary 2.2.8 of
van der Vaart and Wellner 1996

implies that,
letting
\hat{\beta}^{2}=\sup_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}n\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\sup_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\left(\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]-\mathbbold{1}[g(X_{i})\neq Y_{i}]\right)\middle|S\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\sup_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}\left|Z_{f}-Z_{g}\right|\middle|S\right]\leq C_{1}\int_{0}^{\hat{\beta}}\sqrt{\log\hat{D}(\gamma)}\mathrm{d}\gamma

for a universal constant
C_{1}
.
Moreover,
Haussler 1995
has shown that,
for any
\gamma>0
,

\hat{D}(\gamma)\leq\left(\frac{nC_{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right)^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}

for a universal constant
C_{2}
,
so that the last expression above is at most

\displaystyle C_{1}\int_{0}^{\hat{\beta}}\sqrt{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{nC_{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right)}\mathrm{d}\gamma\leq C_{3}\sqrt{\hat{\beta}^{2}\cdot\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\hat{\beta}^{2}}\right)}

for a universal constant
C_{3}
.

Together with (
37
) and the law of total expectation,
we have that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[L_{\beta}\right]\leq 2C_{3}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\sqrt{\hat{\beta}^{2}\cdot\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\hat{\beta}^{2}}\right)}\right]\leq 2C_{3}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\hat{\beta}^{2}\right]\cdot\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\hat{\beta}^{2}\right]}\right)},

(38)

where the second inequality is due to
Jensen’s inequality
(noting that
x\mapsto\sqrt{x\log(1/x)}
is concave).

Our next step is to bound
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\hat{\beta}^{2}\right]
.
A bound sufficient for our purposes
follows directly from Lemma
27
.
Specifically, since any
(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}

have
n\sigma^{2}(f,g)\leq\beta^{2}
,
Lemma
27

implies that,
for any
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,e^{-1})
,
with probability at least

1-\delta^{\prime}
,

\frac{\hat{\beta}^{2}}{n}=\sup_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)\leq\sup_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}}2\sigma^{2}(f,g)+C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta^{\prime})\leq\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}+C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta^{\prime}).

Since this holds for any choice of
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,e^{-1})
,
it can be stated equivalently as a tail bound:
namely, for any
\gamma>n\epsilon^{2}(n,e^{-1})=\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)+1
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{\beta}^{2}>2\beta^{2}+\gamma\right)\leq\exp\!\left\{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)-\gamma\right\}=\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}e^{-\gamma}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\hat{\beta}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{\beta^{2}}>\tau\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta^{2}+n\epsilon^{2}(n,e^{-1})+\int_{n\epsilon^{2}(n,e^{-1})}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{\beta}^{2}>2\beta^{2}+\gamma\right)\mathrm{d}\gamma

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta^{2}+n\epsilon^{2}(n,e^{-1})+\int_{n\epsilon^{2}(n,e^{-1})}^{\infty}\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}e^{-\gamma}\mathrm{d}\gamma

\displaystyle=2\beta^{2}+n\epsilon^{2}(n,e^{-1})+e^{-1}.

Denoting by
\epsilon^{2}_{n}=\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)
,
we may further note that for
\beta^{2}\geq(1/2)n\epsilon^{2}_{n}
,

2\beta^{2}+n\epsilon^{2}(n,e^{-1})+e^{-1}\leq 6\beta^{2}
.

Plugging this back into (
38
),
together with monotonicity of
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
,
yields that for
\beta^{2}\geq(1/2)n\epsilon^{2}_{n}
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[L_{\beta}\right]\leq 2C_{3}\sqrt{6\beta^{2}\cdot\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{6\beta^{2}}\right)}.

(39)

This bound will be used to bound the additive
\mathbf{E}\!\left[L_{\beta}\right]

term in (
36
).
For the
\mathbf{E}\!\left[L_{\beta}\right]
term inside the square root
in (
36
),
a simpler coarse bound will suffice:
namely, for
\beta^{2}\geq(1/2)n\epsilon^{2}_{n}
,
the expression on the right hand side of (
39
)
is at most

2C_{3}\sqrt{6\beta^{2}\cdot n\epsilon^{2}_{n}}\leq C_{3}\sqrt{48}\beta^{2}.

Plugging these into (
36
) yields that,
on the event
E_{\beta}
,

\displaystyle L_{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{3}\sqrt{6\beta^{2}\cdot\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{6\beta^{2}}\right)}+24\max\!\left\{\sqrt{\left(C_{3}\sqrt{48}+1\right)\beta^{2}\cdot\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\beta}}\right)},\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\beta}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\sqrt{\beta^{2}\cdot\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\beta^{2}}\right)+\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\beta}}\right)\right)}+C_{4}\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\beta}}\right)

for a universal constant
C_{4}
.
Recalling the definition

\delta_{\beta}=\frac{\delta\beta^{2}}{4n}
,
this further implies that for

\beta^{2}\geq(1/2)n\epsilon^{2}_{n}
,
on the event
E_{\beta}
,

L_{\beta}\leq C_{5}\sqrt{\beta^{2}\cdot\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\beta^{2}}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+C_{5}n\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)

(40)

for a universal constant
C_{5}
.

Let
B_{n}=[(1/2)n\epsilon^{2}_{n},n]\cap\{n\cdot 2^{1-k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
and note that
B_{n}\neq\emptyset
by the assumption
n\geq e^{2}\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})
.
Define an event
E=\bigcap_{\beta^{2}\in B_{n}}E_{\beta}
.
By the union bound, the event
E
has probability at least

1-\sum_{\beta^{2}\in B_{n}}\delta_{\beta}\geq 1-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\delta}{4}2^{1-k}=1-\frac{\delta}{2}
.

Consider any
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
,
and let
\beta^{2}(f,g)=\min\!\left\{\beta^{2}\in B_{n}:n\sigma^{2}(f,g)\leq\beta^{2}\right\}
(which is well-defined since
n\sigma^{2}(f,g)\leq n\in B_{n}
).
In particular, we have
(f,g),(g,f)\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta(f,g)}
,
so that

\left|\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(g)\right)-\left(\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(f)-\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(g)\right)\right|\leq\frac{1}{n}L_{\beta(f,g)}.

(41)

Thus, on the event
E
,
(
40
) and (
41
) imply

\displaystyle\left|\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(g)\right)-\left(\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(f)-\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(g)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}\sqrt{\beta^{2}(f,g)\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\beta^{2}(f,g)}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+C_{5}\cdot\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta).

(42)

We consider two cases.
First, if
\beta^{2}(f,g)=\min B_{n}
,
then
n\sigma^{2}(f,g)\leq\beta^{2}(f,g)\leq n\epsilon^{2}_{n}
,
so that (by monotonicity of
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
for
x>0
)
the expression in (
42
) is at most

C_{5}\sqrt{\epsilon^{2}_{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}_{n}}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+C_{5}\cdot\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)\leq 2C_{5}\cdot\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta).

For the remaining case, if
\beta^{2}(f,g)>\min B_{n}
,
then
n\sigma^{2}(f,g)\leq\beta^{2}(f,g)\leq 2n\sigma^{2}(f,g)
,
so that the expression in (
42
) is at most

\displaystyle C_{5}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(f,g)\cdot\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}(f,g)}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+C_{5}\cdot\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta).

Since this second case, where
\beta^{2}(f,g)>\min B_{n}
, necessarily has

\sigma^{2}(f,g)>(1/2)\epsilon^{2}_{n}>\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}{2n}
,
we have in both cases that, on the event
E
,
the expression in (
42
) is at most

C_{5}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}(f,g)\cdot\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}(f,g)}\land\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+2C_{5}\cdot\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta).

(43)

It remains only to replace
\sigma^{2}(f,g)
with
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)

in the above inequality.
For this, we again rely on Lemma
27
, which, noting that

\epsilon^{2}\!\left(n,\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\leq 2\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)
,
guarantees that,
on an event
E^{\prime}
of probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{2}
,
every
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy

\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}(f,g)-2C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)\leq\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)\leq 2\sigma^{2}(f,g)+2C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta).

(44)

In particular, this also implies

\sigma^{2}(f,g)\leq 2\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)+4C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)\leq\max\!\left\{4\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g),8C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)\right\}.

On this event, by monotonicity of
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
,
the expression in (
43
)
is at most

\displaystyle C_{5}\sqrt{\left(8\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)\!\lor\!16C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)\right)\!\frac{1}{n}\!\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{4\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)\!\lor\!8C\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)}\right)\!\!+\!\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\!\right)}\!+\!2C_{5}\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta)

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}\sqrt{8\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)\cdot\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}(f,g)}\land\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathcal{H})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+\left(8\sqrt{C}\!+\!2\right)C_{5}\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta).

(45)

By the union bound, the event
E\cap E^{\prime}
has probability at least
1-\delta
.
Since we have established that,
on the event
E\cap E^{\prime}
,
every
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
satisfy
(
44
)
and also satisfy that

\left|\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S}(g)\right)-\left(\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(f)-\mathrm{er}_{\bar{P}}(g)\right)\right|

is upper bounded by both
(
43
)
and (
45
),
the result follows by defining

c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}=\max\!\left\{\left(8\sqrt{C}+2\right)C_{5},2C\right\}
.

9.1.2
Detailed Proof of Lemma
25

Throughout this subsection, we let
\mathbb{G}
be any partial concept class with
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})<\infty
,
let
P
be any distribution on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}

which is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathbb{G}
,
and let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2}),\ldots
be i.i.d.
P
-distributed random variables.
For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
and for
g=(g(X_{1}),\ldots,g(X_{t}))\in\{0,1\}^{t^{\prime}}

and
g^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}(X_{1}),\ldots,g^{\prime}(X_{t^{\prime}}))\in\{0,1\}^{t^{\prime\prime}}
(for
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\geq t
),
define

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{t}(g):=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbold{1}[Y_{i}\neq g(X_{i})]

and

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{t}(g)=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq g(X_{i})|X_{i})
(the
semi-empirical
error rate)
and

P_{t}(g\neq g^{\prime})=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbbold{1}[g(X_{i})\neq g^{\prime}(X_{i})]
.
To be clear, in the context of a
(X_{\leq t},Y_{\leq t})
-dependent random
\{0,1\}^{t}
-valued
\hat{g}
,
we define
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{t}(\hat{g})=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbf{P}(Y^{\prime}_{i}\neq\hat{g}(X_{i})|X_{i},\hat{g})

where
Y^{\prime}_{i}
is conditionally independent of
(X_{\leq t},Y_{\leq t})

given
X_{i}
, with conditional distribution given
X_{i}
identical to that of
Y_{i}
.
Also define the
Bayes classifier

x\mapsto h^{\star}(x):=\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\mathbf{P}(Y=1|X=x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\right]

(for
(X,Y)\sim P
independent of the
(X_{i},Y_{i})
sequence),
and define

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{t}(h^{\star}_{P})=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})|X_{i})=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\min_{y\in\{0,1\}}\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}=y|X_{i})
.

Many of the results below will rely on the basic event
implied by the Bayes-realizability assumption.
Formally, for any
t\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
E_{t}^{\star}

denote the event of probability one (by Bayes-realizability) that

\exists g_{t}^{\star}\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq t})\text{ s.t. }\forall i\leq t,\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}=g_{n}^{\star}(X_{i})|X_{i})=\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}=h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})|X_{i}).

(46)

Note that this event depends only on the

set
of values
\{X_{i}:i\leq t\}
(not their order in
X_{\leq t}
).

We remark that Lemma
25
holds rather trivially
in the case of
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})=0
.
For simplicity, in all remaining lemmas in this subsection,
we will suppose
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\geq 1
.
The proof of Lemma
25
will address the
general case of any
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

The proof of Lemma
25

relies on several lemmas concerning concentration of empirical and semi-empirical errors (Lemma
29
),
and concentration of semi-empirical errors for different sample sizes
(Lemmas
31
and
35
).
We remark that, for these lemmas and many others below,
it in fact suffices for the
X_{i}
sequence to
merely be
exchangeable
,
with the
Y_{i}
variables conditionally independent
given the respective
X_{i}
variables (and supposing
an appropriate analogue of Bayes-realizability).
For instance, for the lemmas only involving finite
prefixes of the data sequence,
it would suffice for the
X_{i}
’s to be a
uniform random
permutation
of some fixed sequence
(as is commonly studied in the literature on
transductive

learning).
For simplicity, we do not discuss the formal details of this
generalization of the results.

We begin with the following lemma,
representing a
uniform Bernstein inequality

for the empirical and semi-empirical error rates.

Lemma 29

Fix any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in(0,1)
.
With conditional probability at least
1-\delta

given
X_{\leq n}
,
every
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(f)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)\right)-\left(\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{P_{n}(f\neq g)\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{n}\!\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{n}(f\neq g)}\land\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\epsilon^{2}(n,\delta),

where
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
is the universal constant from Proposition
26
.

Proof
For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
let
P_{i}
denote the conditional distribution of
(X_{i},Y_{i})
given
X_{i}
.
Note that, given
X_{\leq n}
,

(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})
has conditional distribution

P_{1}\times\cdots\times P_{n}
.
Applying Proposition
26

yields the claimed inequality, holding simultaneously
for all
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,
with conditional probability at least
1-\delta

given
X_{\leq n}
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\epsilon_{n}:=\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)}.

The following lemma is an immediate consequence of
Lemma
29
.

Lemma 30

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, letting
m=\lceil n/2\rceil

and
\hat{g}_{n}=\mathrm{TERM}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(X_{\leq m},Y_{\leq m},X_{m+1},\ldots,X_{n+1})
,
on the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}
,
with conditional probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
given
X_{\leq n+1}
,

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\epsilon_{m},

where

c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=2\sqrt{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}

is a universal constant.

Proof
Supposing the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}
,
let
g^{\star}=g_{n+1}^{\star}
for
g_{n+1}^{\star}
as in (
46
).
In particular, this implies
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})\neq\emptyset

and moreover that

g^{\star}(X_{i})=h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})
for each
i\leq n+1
with
\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}=1|X_{i})\neq\frac{1}{2}
,
whereas for those
i\leq n+1
with
\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}=1|X_{i})
, we have
\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq g^{\star}(X_{i})|X_{i})=\frac{1}{2}=\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})|X_{i})
.
In particular, these observations imply

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g^{\star})=\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})
.
Noting that

\hat{g}_{n}(X_{\leq m}),g^{\star}(X_{\leq m})\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq m})
,
Lemma
29
(with
\delta=\frac{1}{m}
)
implies that with conditional probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
given
X_{\leq n+1}
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})=\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g^{\star})+\sqrt{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq g^{\star})\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq g^{\star})}\land\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log(m)\right)}

\displaystyle{\hskip 34.1433pt}+\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log(m)\right).

Since both
\hat{g}_{n},g^{\star}\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})

and (by definition)
\hat{g}_{n}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)
,
we have that

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g^{\star})\leq 0
.
We can also coarsely bound
P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq g^{\star})\leq 1
, which moreover implies

P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq g^{\star})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq g^{\star})}\right)\leq 1

(noting that
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)=x\ln((1/x)\lor e)
is increasing in
x>0
).
Altogether, on the above event, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq\sqrt{\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})+\log(m)\right)}+\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log(m)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{m}\log\!\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)}+\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{m}\log\!\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)=\sqrt{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}\epsilon_{m}+2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\epsilon_{m}^{2}.

If
2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\epsilon_{m}^{2}\leq 1
,
taking square root of the last term above yields

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq 2\sqrt{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}\epsilon_{m},

whereas if
2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\epsilon_{m}^{2}>1
,
then the above inequality trivially holds
since
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq 1
.
The result thus follows, defining
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=2\sqrt{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}
.

We next state several lemmas based on the observation that,
for any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\leq n
,
by exchangeability
the prefix
X_{\leq m}
may be viewed as a uniform random
sample (without replacement) from the
n
-element

multiset
of values
X_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
We denote by
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
this multiset of values.
Moreover, we may note that the set
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
is
(up to permutations of indices) only dependent on
this multiset
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
, not the particular order in which these elements occur in the sequence
X_{\leq n}
.
To formalize the arguments based on these observations,
it will be convenient to introduce notation
to study random
permutations
of
the sequence
X_{\leq n}
.

Toward this end, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
denote by
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}

and let
\mathrm{Sym}(n)
denote the set of all bijections
\pi:[n]\to[n]
(i.e., the set of all permutations of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
).
For each
\pi\in\mathrm{Sym}(n)
,
for any
m\in[n]
,
denote by
\pi([m])=(\pi(1),\ldots,\pi(m))
,
and similarly

X_{\pi([m])}=(X_{\pi(1)},\ldots,X_{\pi(m)})
.
As mentioned, we will rely on the observation
that
\mathbb{G}(X_{\pi([n])})=\{g(X_{\pi([n])}):g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})\}
,
where
g(X_{\pi([n])}):=(g(X_{\pi(1)}),\ldots,g(X_{\pi(n)}))
:
that is, the sets of

n
-element
multisets

\lbag(X_{\leq n},g(X_{\leq n}))\rbag
of classifications
are the same for
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
and
\mathbb{G}(X_{\pi([n])})
.

We now state the lemmas based on these ideas.

Lemma 31

For any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq m
,
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,
with conditional probability at least
1-\delta
given
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
,
every
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq 2\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)+\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)

and

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq 2\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)+\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right),

and every
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
satisfy

\displaystyle P_{n}(f\neq g)\leq 2P_{m}(f\neq g)+\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)

and

\displaystyle P_{m}(f\neq g)\leq 2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right),

where
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=24
is a universal constant.

Proof of Lemma
31

If
m<\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
, the bound trivially holds (the right hand sides being greater than
1
); to focus on the remaining case, suppose
m\geq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
.
For any
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
and
i\leq n
,
denote by

\ell(g;i):=\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq g(X_{i})|X_{i})-\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})|X_{i}),

and for any
k\leq n
and any sequence

(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{k}
,
denote by
\bar{L}(g;i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\ell(g;i_{j})
.
In particular, note that

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star}_{P})=\bar{L}(g;[n])

and

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})=\bar{L}(g;[m])
.

Fix a value
\epsilon=\frac{8}{m}\left(2\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{4}{\delta}\right)\right)
.
Let
\bm{\pi}
be a
\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathrm{Sym}(n))
random variable
independent of
X_{\leq n}
.
Note that, by exchangeability,
given
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag

the sequence
X_{\leq n}
is a uniform random ordering of the
multiset
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
.
Moreover, recall that for any
\pi\in\mathrm{Sym}(n)
we always have

\mathbb{G}(X_{\pi([n])})=\{(g(X_{\pi([n])}):g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})\}
,
and that
\bar{L}(g;\pi([n]))=\bar{L}(g;[n])
.
We therefore have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\bar{L}(g;[m])\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}\bar{L}(g;[n])-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\right]\middle|\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}\bar{L}(g;[n])-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\right]\middle|X_{\leq n}\right).

By the union bound, this last quantity is at most

\sum_{g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}\bar{L}(g;[n])-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\right]\middle|X_{\leq n}\right).

(47)

We bound each term in this sum
by a multiplicative Chernoff bound for sampling without replacement.
Specifically,
for each
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
, by the union bound we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}\bar{L}(g;[n])-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\right]\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))+\frac{\epsilon}{2}<\frac{1}{2}\bar{L}(g;[n])\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)+\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))>2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\phantom{\frac{1}{2}}\!\!\!\middle|X_{\leq n}\right).

Then note that, conditioned on
X_{\leq n}
,
for any
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,
the value

\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell(g;\bm{\pi}(i))

is an average of
m
values
\ell(g;i)

sampled uniformly without replacement from
the multiset
\lbag\ell(g;i):i\leq n\rbag
.
Further noting that each
\ell(g;i)\in[0,1]
,
applying a multiplicative Chernoff bound
for sampling without replacement
(
Hoeffding 1953
)

under the conditional distribution given
X_{\leq n}

yields that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))\!+\!\frac{\epsilon}{2}\!<\!\frac{1}{2}\bar{L}(g;[n])\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\leq\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\bar{L}(g;[n])\!>\!\epsilon\right]\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))\!<\!\frac{1}{2}\bar{L}(g;[n])\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\bar{L}(g;[n])\!>\!\epsilon\right]e^{-\bar{L}(g;[n])m/8}\leq e^{-\epsilon m/8}.

Likewise, if
\bar{L}(g;[n])>\epsilon
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))>2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))>2\bar{L}(g;[n])\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\leq e^{-\bar{L}(g;[n])m/3}\leq e^{-\epsilon m/3},

whereas if
0<\bar{L}(g;[n])\leq\epsilon
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))>2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))>\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{\bar{L}(g;[n])}\right)\bar{L}(g;[n])\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\!\left\{-\bar{L}(g;[n])m\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\bar{L}(g;[n])}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{3}\right\}=\exp\!\left\{-m\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\bar{L}(g;[n])}\frac{1}{3}\right\}\leq e^{-\epsilon m/3}.

Moreover, if
\bar{L}(g;[n])=0
, we clearly have

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))>2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))>0\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)=0.

Plugging these back into (
47
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\bar{L}(g;[m])\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}\bar{L}(g;[n])-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2\bar{L}(g;[n])+\epsilon\right]\middle|\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[|\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})|\right]2e^{-\epsilon m/8}\leq\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}2e^{-\epsilon m/8}\leq\frac{\delta}{2},

where the second inequality is due to
Sauer’s lemma
(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
;
Sauer 1972
)
,
which states that

|\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})|\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\binom{n}{i}\leq\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}
,
and the final inequality is due to our choice of
\epsilon
.
Choosing
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=24
, we have that

\epsilon\leq\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)
,
which establishes the first two inequalities on an event of conditional probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{2}
given
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
.

The remaining inequalities are established similarly.
Specifically, denote by

P_{\pi([m])}(f\neq g)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{\pi(i)})\neq g(X_{\pi(i)})]

and
P_{\pi([n])}(f\neq g)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{\pi(i)})\neq g(X_{\pi(i)})]

for any
\pi\in\mathrm{Sym}(n)
and
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
.
Noting that
P_{\pi([n])}(f\neq g)=P_{n}(f\neq g)
,
together with the above observations relating
\mathbb{G}(X_{\pi([n])})
and
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,
by exchangeability of
X_{\leq n}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):P_{m}(f\neq g)\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}P_{n}(f\neq g)-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\epsilon\right]\middle|\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}P_{n}(f\neq g)-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\epsilon\right]\middle|X_{\leq n}\right).

By the union bound, this last quantity is at most

\sum_{f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})}\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}P_{n}(f\neq g)-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\epsilon\right]\middle|X_{\leq n}\right).

We now proceed to bound each term in the sum.
For each
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
, by the union bound,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}P_{n}(f\neq g)-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\epsilon\right]\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)\!+\!\frac{\epsilon}{2}<\frac{1}{2}P_{n}(f\neq g)\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\!+\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)>2P_{n}(f\neq g)\!+\!\epsilon\middle|X_{\leq n}\right).

Noting that
P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{\bm{\pi}(i)})\neq g(X_{\bm{\pi}(i)})]

is an average of
m
binary values
\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i})\neq g(X_{i})]

sampled uniformly without replacement from the multiset

\lbag\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i})\neq g(X_{i})]:i\leq n\rbag
,
applying a multiplicative Chernoff bound for sampling without replacement

(
Hoeffding 1953
)
under the conditional distribution given
X_{\leq n}

yields that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}<\frac{1}{2}P_{n}(f\neq g)\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\leq\mathbbold{1}\!\left[P_{n}(f\neq g)>\epsilon\right]e^{-P_{n}(f\neq g)m/8}\leq e^{-\epsilon m/8}.

Similarly for the other term, if
P_{n}(f\neq g)>\epsilon
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)>2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\epsilon\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\leq e^{-P_{n}(f\neq g)m/3}\leq e^{-\epsilon m/3},

whereas if
0<P_{n}(f\neq g)\leq\epsilon
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)>2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\epsilon\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)>\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{P_{n}(f\neq g)}\right)P_{n}(f\neq g)\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\!\left\{P_{n}(f\neq g)m\left(\frac{\epsilon}{P_{n}(f\neq g)}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{3}\right\}=\exp\!\left\{-m\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{P_{n}(f\neq g)}\frac{1}{3}\right\}\leq e^{-\epsilon m/3}.

If
P_{n}(f\neq g)=0
, we trivially have

\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)>2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\epsilon\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)>0\middle|X_{\leq n}\right)=0.

Altogether, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):P_{m}(f\neq g)\notin\left[\frac{1}{2}P_{n}(f\neq g)-\frac{\epsilon}{2},2P_{n}(f\neq g)+\epsilon\right]\middle|\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag\right)

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})|^{2}2e^{-\epsilon m/8}\leq\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{2\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}2e^{-\epsilon m/8}\leq\frac{\delta}{2},

where the second inequality is again due to Sauer’s lemma
(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
;
Sauer 1972
)
,
and the final inequality is due to our choice of
\epsilon
.
Thus, the third and fourth claimed inequalities in the lemma are established,
on an event of conditional probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{2}
given
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
.
By the union bound, both of these events occur simultaneously
with conditional probability at least
1-\delta
,
which completes the proof.

Next we combine the above lemmas to obtain a bound on
the excess error rate of
\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}
,
which is our starting point for the proof of the
o(n^{-1/2})
rate.
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\epsilon>0
, define

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon):=\left\{g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq\epsilon\right\},

and define

\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}:=\epsilon_{n}^{2}\lor\max_{g\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n})}\min_{f\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n}^{2})}P_{n}(f\neq g),

(48)

where
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
and
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
are universal constants (informed by the analysis below):
namely,
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}=4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}+8c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}

and
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}=\sqrt{8}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7})+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}

for
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}=\sqrt{2e}\left(5+\sqrt{12}\right)
.
For completeness, define
\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}=1

in the event that either of

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n})
or
\bar{\mathbb{G}}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n}^{2})

is empty (though this is unimportant for our analysis,
since this event has probability zero under the Bayes-realizability assumption).

Lemma 32

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, letting
m=\lceil n/2\rceil
,
and
\hat{g}_{n}\!=\!\mathrm{TERM}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(X_{\leq m},Y_{\leq m},X_{m+1},\ldots,X_{n+1})
,
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,
on the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}
,
with conditional probability at least
1-\frac{4}{n}-\delta
given
\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag
,

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{1}{n}\!\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\!\left(\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)\!,

where

c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}+12c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+2\sqrt{8c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}

is a universal constant.

Proof
The claim is trivially satisfied if
n\leq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\lor 2e

(as the right hand side is greater than
1
);
to address the remaining case, suppose
n>\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\lor 2e
.
Suppose the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}
occurs.
In particular, this implies
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})\neq\emptyset
,
so that (by definition)
\hat{g}_{n}\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})

and is of minimal
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)
among
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})
.
The event
E_{n+1}^{\star}

also implies
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n+1}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n+1}^{2})\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\bar{g}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{g\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n+1}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n+1}^{2})}P_{n+1}(g\neq\hat{g}_{n})
, breaking ties arbitrarily.

By Lemma
30
and the law of total probability,
on an event
E
of conditional probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
given
\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag
,
we have

\hat{g}_{n}(X_{\leq m})\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{m}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\epsilon_{m})
.
Moreover, Lemma
29
and the law of total probability imply that,
on an event
E^{\prime}
of conditional probability at least
1-\delta
given
\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag
,
every
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq m})
satisfy

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(f)

\displaystyle-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(f)-\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{P_{m}(f\neq g)\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{m}(f\neq g)}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\epsilon_{m}^{2}+\frac{1}{m}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

In particular, on the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}\cap E^{\prime}
,
since (by definition)
\forall g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})
,

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\bar{g})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{m}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\left(\epsilon_{m}^{2}+\frac{1}{m}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

(49)

Lemma
31

implies that, on an event
E^{\prime\prime}
of conditional probability at least
1-\frac{2}{n}
given
\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag
, every
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}

(50)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}

(51)

and
\forall f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})
,

P_{m}(f\neq g)\leq 2P_{n+1}(f\neq g)+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}.

(52)

In particular,
since
\bar{g}\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n+1}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n+1}^{2})
,
on
E_{n+1}^{\star}\cap E^{\prime\prime}

(
51
) implies

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\bar{g})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq(2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\epsilon_{n}^{2}.

Together with
(
49
), we have
that on the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}\cap E^{\prime}\cap E^{\prime\prime}
,
letting
C=2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})=\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\bar{g})+\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\bar{g})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+C\left(\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

Combined with (
50
),
this further implies that on
E_{n+1}^{\star}\cap E^{\prime}\cap E^{\prime\prime}
,
letting
C^{\prime}=2C+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+C^{\prime}\left(\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

Additionally, (
52
)
implies that on this same event,

P_{m}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})\leq 2P_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}
.
Since
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
is increasing in
x\geq 0
,
altogether (after some basic relaxations to simplify the expression)
we have that on
E_{n+1}^{\star}\cap E^{\prime}\cap E^{\prime\prime}
,
letting

c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=C^{\prime}+2\sqrt{8c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}=4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}+12c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+2\sqrt{8c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{P_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{P_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\left(\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

Recalling that, on the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}\cap E
,

\hat{g}_{n}\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{m}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\epsilon_{m})
,
(
50
) implies that
on the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}\cap E\cap E^{\prime\prime}
,

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq 2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\epsilon_{m}+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}\leq(4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}+8c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\epsilon_{n+1}=c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n+1},

so that
\hat{g}_{n}\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n+1}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n+1})
.
In particular, on this event,
since by definition

\bar{g}
has minimal
P_{n+1}(g\neq\hat{g}_{n})

among all
g\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n+1}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n+1}^{2})
,
by definition of
\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}

we have

P_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n}\neq\bar{g})\leq\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}.

Altogether, recalling that
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
is increasing in
x\geq 0
,
on the event

E_{n+1}^{\star}\cap E\cap E^{\prime}\cap E^{\prime\prime}
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)+\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\left(\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

To complete the proof,
we note that
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\geq 4\sqrt{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}}
and that, by the union bound,
on
E_{n+1}^{\star}

the event
E\cap E^{\prime}\cap E^{\prime\prime}

has conditional probability at least

1-\frac{1}{m}-\frac{2}{n}-\delta\geq 1-\frac{4}{n}-\delta
.

In particular, the following lemma is an immediate
implication of Lemma
32
.

Lemma 33

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, letting
m=\lceil n/2\rceil

and
\hat{g}_{n}=\mathrm{TERM}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(X_{\leq m},Y_{\leq m},X_{m+1},\ldots,X_{n+1})
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\right]\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]}\right)},

where

c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}=\sqrt{18}\left(2+e^{-1}\right)c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+\sqrt{32}

is a universal constant.

Proof
By Lemma
32
and a relaxation of
a+b\leq 2\max\{a,b\}
(for
a,b\geq 0
),
for any
\delta\in(0,e^{-1})
,
on the event
E_{n+1}^{\star}
(of probability one),
with conditional probability at least
1-\frac{4}{n}-\delta

given
\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle\leq\max\!\left\{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)+\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)},2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\left(\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)\right\}.

This implies the following tail bound:
namely, letting

\tau_{n}=\max\!\left\{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)+1\right)},2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\left(\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\right)\right\},

for any
\tau>\tau_{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\Big(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})>\tau~\Big|~\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{n}+\exp\!\left\{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)-\frac{\tau^{2}n}{4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right\}+\exp\!\left\{\epsilon_{n}^{2}n-\frac{\tau n}{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}}\right\}.

We can therefore bound the conditional expectation as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})~\Big|\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag\right]=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{P}\!\Big(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})>\tau~\Big|~\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag\Big)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq\tau_{n}+\frac{4}{n}+\int_{\tau_{n}}^{1}\exp\!\left\{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)-\frac{\tau^{2}n}{4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right\}\mathrm{d}\tau+\int_{\tau_{n}}^{1}\exp\!\left\{\epsilon_{n}^{2}n-\frac{\tau n}{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}}\right\}\mathrm{d}\tau.

Then note that

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{n}}^{1}\exp\!\left\{\epsilon_{n}^{2}n-\frac{\tau n}{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}}\right\}\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}}{n}\exp\!\left\{\epsilon_{n}^{2}n-\frac{\tau_{n}n}{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}}\right\}\leq\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}}{n}\exp\!\left\{\epsilon_{n}^{2}n-\left(\epsilon_{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\right)n\right\}=\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}e^{-1}}{n}.

Also,

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{n}}^{1}\exp\!\left\{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)-\frac{\tau^{2}n}{4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right\}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq\exp\!\left\{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)\right\}\int_{\tau_{n}}^{\infty}\frac{\tau}{\tau_{n}}\exp\!\left\{-\frac{\tau^{2}n}{4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right\}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\exp\!\left\{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)\right\}\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}{\tau_{n}n}\int_{\tau_{n}^{2}n/(4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1})}^{\infty}e^{-u}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}{\tau_{n}n}\exp\!\left\{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)-\frac{\tau_{n}^{2}n}{4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right\}.

Since
\tau_{n}\geq 2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)+1\right)}
,
this last expression is at most

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}e^{-1}}{\tau_{n}n}\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}e^{-1}\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{1}{n}}.

Altogether, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})~\Big|~\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag\right]\leq\tau_{n}+\frac{4}{n}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}e^{-1}\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{1}{n}}+\frac{2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}e^{-1}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2+e^{-1}\right)\sqrt{2}c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)}+\left(\left(4+2e^{-1}\right)c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+4\right)\epsilon_{n}^{2}.

Recalling that (by definition)
\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\geq\epsilon_{n+1}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}
,
and that
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
is increasing in
x>0
,
we have that

\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)}\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\epsilon_{n}^{2}.

Thus, we conclude that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})~\Big|~\lbag X_{\leq n+1}\rbag\right]\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\sqrt{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}}\right)}

for a universal constant

c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}=\sqrt{18}\left(2+e^{-1}\right)c_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+\sqrt{32}
.
The result now follows by the law of total expectation and Jensen’s inequality,
noting that
x\mapsto\sqrt{x\log(1/x)}
is concave
in
x>0
.

Together with exchangeability of the samples

(X_{m+1},Y_{m+1}),\ldots,(X_{n+1},Y_{n+1})
,
we arrive at the following result.

Lemma 34

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, letting
\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}
be as in (
30
),

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}))\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq 2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]}\right)}.

Proof
Let
m=\lceil n/2\rceil

and let
\hat{g}_{n}=\mathrm{TERM}(X_{\leq m},Y_{\leq m},X_{m+1},\ldots,X_{n+1})
.
Our main goal in this proof is relating

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}))\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})

to
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\right]
.
First note that, by the law of total expectation,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}))\right]=\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{n+1}\neq\hat{g}_{n}(X_{n+1})\right).

(53)

For each
i\in\{m+1,\ldots,n+1\}
,
let
\pi_{i}:\{m+1,\ldots,n+1\}\to\{m+1,\ldots,n+1\}

denote the bijection which has
\pi_{i}(n+1)=i
,
\pi_{i}(i)=n+1
,
and every
j\in\{m+1,\ldots,n+1\}\setminus\{i,n+1\}

has
\pi_{i}(j)=j
(i.e.,
\pi_{i}
merely swaps
i
and
n+1
).
Let
\hat{g}_{n}^{i}=\mathrm{TERM}(X_{\leq m},Y_{\leq m},X_{\pi_{i}(m+1)},\ldots,X_{\pi_{i}(n+1)})
.
By exchangeability of the random variables

(X_{m+1},Y_{m+1}),\ldots,(X_{n+1},Y_{n+1})

(and independence from
(X_{\leq m},Y_{\leq m})
),
we have that (
53
) equals

\frac{1}{n+1-m}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{\pi_{i}(n+1)}\neq\hat{g}_{n}^{i}(X_{\pi_{i}(n+1)})\right)=\frac{1}{n+1-m}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq\hat{g}_{n}^{i}(X_{i})\right).

(54)

Recall that the definition of
\mathrm{TERM}
stipulates that,
for any bijection
\pi:\{m+1,\ldots,n+1\}\to\{m+1,\ldots,n+1\}
,

\mathrm{TERM}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq m},Y_{\leq m},X_{\pi(m+1)},\ldots,X_{\pi(n+1)})=(\hat{g}_{n}(X_{\leq m}),\hat{g}_{n}(X_{\pi(m+1)}),\ldots,\hat{g}_{n}(X_{\pi(n+1)})).

We therefore have that (
54
) equals

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1-m}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq\hat{g}_{n}(X_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n+1-m}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq\hat{g}_{n}(X_{i})\middle|X_{i},\hat{g}_{n}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{n+1-m}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq\hat{g}_{n}(X_{i})\middle|X_{i},\hat{g}_{n}\right)\right],

where the first equality is by the law of total probability and the second is by linearity of the expectation.
Similarly, by exchangeability, the law of total probability, and linearity of the expectation, we have that

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})=\frac{1}{n+1-m}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i}))=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{n+1-m}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})\middle|X_{i}\right)\right].

Altogether, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}_{n}^{\mathbb{G}}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}))\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{n+1-m}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\Big(\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq\hat{g}_{n}(X_{i})\middle|X_{i},\hat{g}_{n}\right)-\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})\middle|X_{i}\right)\Big)\right].

Since
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\geq 0
,
the last expression above is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n+1-m}\mathbf{E}\!\left[m\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)+\!\!\sum_{i=m+1}^{n+1}\!\!\left(\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq\hat{g}_{n}\!(X_{i})\middle|X_{i},\hat{g}_{n}\right)-\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})\middle|X_{i}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n+1-m}\mathbf{E}\!\left[(n+1)\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)\right]\leq 2\mathbf{E}\!\left[\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n+1}(h^{\star}_{P})\right].

The claimed result now follows from the above,
in combination with Lemma
33
.

The remainder of the lemmas in this subsection
build toward establishing that

\mathbf{E}[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}]\to 0
as
n\to\infty
,
to obtain the claimed
o(n^{-1/2})
rate.

Lemma 35

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
,
for every
n\geq m
,
every
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
has

\displaystyle\left|\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)-\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)\right|\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{m},

and every
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
have

\displaystyle\left|P_{m}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)\right|\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{m},

where
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}=\sqrt{2e}\left(5+\sqrt{12}\right)
is a universal constant (as defined above, following (
48
)).

Proof
Since
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\geq\sqrt{2e}
,
the result is trivially satisfied if

m\leq 2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
since then the term
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{m}
on the right hand side is greater than one (whereas the quantities on the left hand sides of the two inequalities are both at most one).
To focus the remainder of the proof on the remaining case
we suppose
m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
.

We continue to use the notation introduced in the proof of Lemma
31
:
namely, for any
n,i\in\mathbb{N}
with
i\leq n

and any
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,

\ell(g;i):=\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq g(X_{i})|X_{i})-\mathbf{P}(Y_{i}\neq h^{\star}_{P}(X_{i})|X_{i})
,
and for any
k\leq n
and distinct
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in[n]
,

\bar{L}(g;i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}):=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\ell(g;i_{j})
.
In particular, we have
\bar{L}(g;[n])=\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star}_{P})
and for
m\leq n
we have
\bar{L}(g;[m])=\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})
.
Also recall that we always have
\ell(g;i)\in[0,1]
.
Likewise, for any distinct
i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in[n]
,
denote by
P_{\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\}}(f\neq g):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbbold{1}[f(X_{i_{j}})\neq g(X_{i_{j}})]
.
Also, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\lfloor n\rfloor_{2}=2^{\lfloor\log_{2}(n)\rfloor}
:
that is,
\lfloor n\rfloor_{2}
rounds
n
down to a power of
2
.

The essential strategy is to first establish the claimed relation for any fixed
n,m
with
m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})

and
n\geq m\geq\lfloor n-1\rfloor_{2}
,
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{3m^{2}}
.
For fixed
m
, we then extend this (by a union bound) to hold simultaneously for all
n
satisfying this relation to
m
,
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{3m}
.
For the case of general
n\geq m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
we then chain together these relations (in a telescoping series) among consecutive
m^{\prime},n^{\prime}
powers of
2
in the interval
[m,n]
,
which establishes the claim since the sum of these
\epsilon_{n^{\prime}}
values is
O(\epsilon_{m})

and the sum of their failure probabilities is at most

\frac{1}{m}
.
We refer to this idea as
sample size chaining
.

To start, consider any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}

with
m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
and

n\geq m\geq\lfloor n-1\rfloor_{2}
.
As discussed above, the set
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
is
determined (up to permutations) by the multiset

\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
of values in
X_{\leq n}
,
and each
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})

have
P_{n}(f\neq g)
and
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star}_{P})
invariant to permutations of this sequence (and similarly
P_{m}(f\neq g)
and
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{m}(h^{\star}_{P})
are invariant to permutations of
X_{\leq m}
).
Moreover, by exchangeability,
given the multiset
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
of values in
X_{\leq n}
,
the sequence
X_{\leq n}
is conditionally a uniform random ordering of the multiset
\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag
.
Let
\bm{\pi}
be a
\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathrm{Sym}([n]))

random variable independent of
X_{\leq n}
.
By the above observations, for any
\epsilon>0
,
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\exists g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\left|\bar{L}(g;[m])-\bar{L}(g;[n])\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\Big(\exists f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\left|\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))-\bar{L}(g;[n])\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~X_{\leq n}\Big).

By the union bound, this last quantity is at most

\sum_{g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})}\mathbf{P}\Big(\left|\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))-\bar{L}(g;[n])\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~X_{\leq n}\Big).

We bound each term in this sum by Hoeffding’s inequality
for sampling without replacement
(
Hoeffding 1953
)
.
Specifically, for each
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
, we have

\mathbf{P}\Big(\left|\bar{L}(g;\bm{\pi}([m]))-\bar{L}(g;[n])\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~X_{\leq n}\Big)\leq 2e^{-2\epsilon^{2}m}.

Sauer’s lemma
(
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
;
Sauer 1972
)

implies that
|\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})|\leq\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}
,
so that altogether we have that

\mathbf{P}\Big(\exists g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\left|\bar{L}(g;[m])-\bar{L}(g;[n])\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag\Big)\leq 2\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}e^{-2\epsilon^{2}m}.

(55)

Similarly, for the claim about pairs
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,
for any
\epsilon>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\exists f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\left|P_{m}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\Big(\exists f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\left|P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~X_{\leq n}\Big).

By the union bound, this last quantity is at most

\sum_{f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})}\mathbf{P}\Big(\left|P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~X_{\leq n}\Big).

By Hoeffding’s inequality for sampling without replacement
(
Hoeffding 1953
)
,
for each
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
, we have

\mathbf{P}\Big(\left|P_{\bm{\pi}([m])}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~X_{\leq n}\Big)\leq 2e^{-2\epsilon^{2}m}.

Again, Sauer’s lemma implies that
|\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})|\leq\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}
,
so that altogether we have that

\mathbf{P}\Big(\exists f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}):\left|P_{m}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)\right|>\epsilon~\Big|~\lbag X_{\leq n}\rbag\Big)\leq 2\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{2\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}e^{-2\epsilon^{2}m}.

(56)

Letting
\epsilon=\sqrt{2e}\epsilon_{m}
,
and noting that (since
m\geq n/2
and
m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{2\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\!\!e^{-4e\epsilon_{m}^{2}m}=\left(\frac{en}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{2\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\!\!\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{\!-4e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\!\!\!\!\leq(2e)^{2\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{\!-8\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12m^{2}}\cdot 12\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})^{2}(2e)^{2\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\left(\frac{m}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)^{2-8\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\!\!\leq\frac{1}{12m^{2}}\cdot 12\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})^{2}(2e)^{2-6\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\leq\frac{1}{12m^{2}},

we conclude that, by the union bound (over these two failure events from (
55
) and (
56
)),
on an event
E^{\prime}_{m,n}
of probability at least
1-\frac{1}{3m^{2}}
,

\forall g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}),~\left|\bar{L}(g;[m])-\bar{L}(g;[n])\right|\leq\sqrt{2e}\epsilon_{m}

(57)

and

\forall f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n}),~\left|P_{m}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)\right|\leq\sqrt{2e}\epsilon_{m}.

(58)

Before proceeding to general
n,m
, we first slightly strengthen the above guarantee,
to hold simultaneously for all
n
satisfying this relation to a given
m
.
For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
define an event
E^{\prime}_{m}=\bigcap_{n=m+1}^{2\lfloor m\rfloor_{2}}E^{\prime}_{m,n}
.
The values
n\in\{m+1,\ldots,2\lfloor m\rfloor_{2}\}

are merely those
n>m
ranging up to the next power of
2
above
m
;
equivalently, these are all values
n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

n>m\geq\lfloor n-1\rfloor_{2}
.
Therefore, by the union bound and the above analysis,
the event
E^{\prime}_{m}
has probability at least

1-\frac{1}{3m^{2}}\left(2\lfloor m\rfloor_{2}-m\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{3m}
.
We then have that, for any
m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
on the event
E^{\prime}_{m}
,
(
57
) and
(
58
)
hold for every
n\in\{m,\ldots,2\lfloor m\rfloor_{2}\}

(noting that they hold vacuously for
n=m
).

Next, for any fixed
m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
we extend (
57
) and
(
58
)
(with a slight increase in the constant factor)
to hold for
all

n\geq m

by a
sample size chaining
technique:
chaining together each of the above inequalities
in a telescoping series, for consecutive
powers of two

m^{\prime},n^{\prime}
in
[m,n]
.
Let
E_{m}=E^{\prime}_{m}\cap\bigcap_{k=1+\lfloor\log_{2}(m)\rfloor}^{\infty}E^{\prime}_{2^{k}}
,
and note that, by the union bound and the fact that
\lfloor m\rfloor_{2}>m/2
,
the event
E_{m}
has probability at least

1-\frac{1}{3m}-\sum_{k=1+\lfloor\log_{2}(m)\rfloor}^{\infty}\frac{1}{3\cdot 2^{k}}=1-\frac{1}{3m}-\frac{1}{3\lfloor m\rfloor_{2}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{m}.

Consider any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq m
.
Let
K=\lfloor\log_{2}(n-1)\rfloor
.
As we have already established above that
(
57
) and
(
58
)
hold in the case
m\geq 2^{K}
on the event
E_{m}
,
let us suppose
2^{K}>m
.
Let
K^{\prime}=\lfloor\log_{2}(m)\rfloor
.
Define
m_{K^{\prime}}=m
and
m_{K+1}=n
,
and for values
k\in\{K^{\prime}+1,\ldots,K\}
,
define
m_{k}=2^{k}
.
Note that for each
k\in\{K^{\prime},\ldots,K\}
,
we have
m_{k}\leq m_{k+1}\leq 2\lfloor m_{k}\rfloor_{2}
.
Therefore, since
E_{m}\subseteq\bigcap_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}E^{\prime}_{m_{k}}
,
on the event
E_{m}
it holds that,
for every
k\in\{K^{\prime},\ldots,K\}
,
we have

\forall g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq m_{k+1}}),~\left|\bar{L}(g;[m_{k}])-\bar{L}(g;[m_{k+1}])\right|\leq\sqrt{2e}\epsilon_{m_{k}}

and

\forall f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq m_{k+1}}),~\left|P_{m_{k}}(f\neq g)-P_{m_{k+1}}(f\neq g)\right|\leq\sqrt{2e}\epsilon_{m_{k}}.

Moreover, we may express
\bar{L}(g;[m])-\bar{L}(g;[n])

and
P_{m}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)

as telescoping series: namely,

\forall g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,

\bar{L}(g;[m])-\bar{L}(g;[n])=\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\left(\bar{L}(g;[m_{k}])-\bar{L}(g;[m_{k+1}])\right)

and
\forall f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,

P_{m}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)=\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\left(P_{m_{k}}(f\neq g)-P_{m_{k+1}}(f\neq g)\right).

Thus, on the event
E_{m}
,

\forall g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,

\displaystyle\left|\bar{L}(g;[m])-\bar{L}(g;[n])\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\left(\bar{L}(g;[m_{k}])-\bar{L}(g;[m_{k+1}])\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\left|\bar{L}(g;[m_{k}])-\bar{L}(g;[m_{k+1}])\right|\leq\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\sqrt{2e}\epsilon_{m_{k}}

and similarly,

\forall f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})
,

\displaystyle\left|P_{m}(f\neq g)-P_{n}(f\neq g)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\left(P_{m_{k}}(f\neq g)-P_{m_{k+1}}(f\neq g)\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\left|P_{m_{k}}(f\neq g)-P_{m_{k+1}}(f\neq g)\right|\leq\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\sqrt{2e}\epsilon_{m_{k}}.

Also note that, for any
k\in\{K^{\prime}+2,\ldots,K\}
,
we have
\epsilon_{m_{k}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}}\epsilon_{m_{k-1}}
(using the fact that
m_{k-1}\geq m>2e\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
),
whereas
\epsilon_{m_{K^{\prime}+1}}\leq\epsilon_{m}

(by monotonicity of
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
for
x>0
).
We therefore have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=K^{\prime}}^{K}\sqrt{2e}\epsilon_{m_{k}}\leq\sqrt{2e}\left(\epsilon_{m}+\sum_{k=K^{\prime}+1}^{K}\!\left(\epsilon_{m}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{\!(k-K^{\prime}-1)/2}\right)\right)\leq\sqrt{2e}\left(5+\sqrt{12}\right)\epsilon_{m}=c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{m},

which completes the proof.

For any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq m
,
for any
\epsilon>0
,
define the
projection
of
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon)
to
X_{\leq m}
:

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon)\big|_{m}:=\left\{g(X_{\leq m}):g\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon)\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq m}).

Lemma 36

For any sequence
\{\epsilon^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
(0,1]
,
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists
N_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
with
N_{m}\geq m
such that,
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
,
for every
n\geq N_{m}
,
there exists an infinite sequence

n<n_{1}<n_{2}<\cdots
such that

\forall i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n_{i}}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n_{i}})\big|_{m}=\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n})\big|_{m}~.

Proof
Fix any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since each
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n})\big|_{m}\in 2^{\{0,1\}^{m}}
(the set of all subsets of
\{0,1\}^{m}
),
and
2^{\{0,1\}^{m}}
has finite size,
we observe that there must exist
\hat{N}_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
with
\hat{N}_{m}\geq m

such that,
\forall n\geq\hat{N}_{m}
,
the set
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n})\big|_{m}

occurs infinitely often in the sequence

\{\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})\big|_{m}\}_{n^{\prime}=m}^{\infty}
.
To see this, note that for each
G\in 2^{\{0,1\}^{m}}

which occurs in the sequence

\{\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})\big|_{m}\}_{n^{\prime}=m}^{\infty}

but only finitely often,
there must be a largest value
\hat{n}(G)
for which

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{\hat{n}(G)}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{\hat{n}(G)})\big|_{m}=G
;
it therefore suffices to take
\hat{N}_{m}
as one larger than the
maximum
\hat{n}(G)
among all
G\in 2^{\{0,1\}^{m}}

which occur finitely often in this sequence
(or else
\hat{N}_{m}=m
if every
G
occurs either zero or infinitely many times).
Equivalently, the above guarantees that, for every
n\geq\hat{N}_{m}
,
there exists an infinite sequence
n<n_{1}<n_{2}<\cdots

for which
\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n_{i}}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n_{i}})\big|_{m}=\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(\epsilon^{\prime}_{n})\big|_{m}
.
Since
\hat{N}_{m}
is a finite
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
-dependent random variable,
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}(\hat{N}_{m}>n)=0
,
so that there exists
N_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbf{P}(\hat{N}_{m}>N_{m})\leq\frac{1}{m}
.
Thus, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
,

\hat{N}_{m}\leq N_{m}
,
so that every
n\geq N_{m}
has
n\geq\hat{N}_{m}
,
