and the claim in the lemma follows.

We are now ready to start combining the above lemmas to
obtain that
\mathbf{E}[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}]\to 0

via the following two lemmas.

Lemma 37

Fix any
m\in\mathbb{N}

and let
N_{m}
be as in Lemma
36
for the sequence

\epsilon^{\prime}_{n}:=c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq N_{m}
and
n\geq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
with probability at least
1-\frac{3}{m}
,

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n})\big|_{m}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n}^{2})\big|_{m}~,

where
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}=\sqrt{8}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7})+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}

is a universal constant (as defined above, following (
48
)).

Proof
Fix any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq N_{m}
and
n\geq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
and let
n^{\prime}=\left\lceil\frac{n^{2}}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log(n/\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G}))}\right\rceil

(and note that
n^{\prime}\geq n
due to
n\geq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
).
Lemma
35

implies that, with probability at least

1-\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{m}
,
every
n^{\prime\prime}\geq n^{\prime}
satisfies that,

\forall g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n^{\prime\prime}})
,

\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime}}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime}}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(h^{\star}_{P})+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{n^{\prime}}.

In particular, this implies that

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n^{\prime\prime}})\big|_{n^{\prime}}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n^{\prime\prime}}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{n^{\prime}})\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}((c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7})\epsilon_{n^{\prime}}).

(59)

By Lemma
31
(applied to
n^{\prime}
and
n
, and with
\delta=\frac{1}{m}
),
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
,
every
g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n^{\prime}})
has

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime}}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime}}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)+\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}{n}\left(\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})\log\!\left(\frac{n^{\prime}}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)+\log(m)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime}}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n^{\prime}}(h^{\star}_{P})\right)+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}.

In particular,
noting that

\epsilon_{n^{\prime}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})^{2}}{n^{2}}\log\!\left(\frac{n}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\right)\log\!\left(\frac{n^{2}}{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})^{2}}\right)}\leq\sqrt{2}\epsilon_{n}^{2},

this implies that

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}((c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7})\epsilon_{n^{\prime}})\big|_{n}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(2(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7})\epsilon_{n^{\prime}}+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\epsilon_{n}^{2})\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}\!\left(\left(\sqrt{8}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7})+4c_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\right)\epsilon_{n}^{2}\right)=\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n}^{2}).

(60)

Finally, by Lemma
36
,
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{m}
,
there exists
n^{\prime\prime}\geq n^{\prime}
with

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n^{\prime\prime}})\big|_{m}=\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n})\big|_{m}.

(61)

By the union bound, all three of the above events occur simultaneously
with probability at least
1-\frac{3}{m}
.
Supposing this occurs, then for the
n^{\prime\prime}
satisfying (
61
), we have that

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n})\big|_{m}=\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n^{\prime\prime}})\big|_{m}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n^{\prime}}((c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7})\epsilon_{n^{\prime}})\big|_{m}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n}^{2})\big|_{m},

where the first equality is due to (
61
)
and the subsequent subset inclusions are due to
(
59
) and (
60
),
respectively.

Lemma 38

For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
N_{m}

be defined as in Lemma
36

for the sequence
\epsilon^{\prime}_{n}:=c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq N_{1}
and
n\geq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
letting
M_{n}=\max\{m\in\mathbb{N}:n\geq N_{m}\}
,
it holds that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\right]\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}+\frac{4}{M_{n}}.

In particular, since each
N_{m}<\infty
,
we have
M_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
,
so that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\right]=0.

Proof
We first note that
M_{n}
is well-defined since

N_{m}\geq m
implies
\{m\in\mathbb{N}:n\geq N_{m}\}
is a finite set
(and is non-empty since
n\geq N_{1}
).
Moreover, by definition of
M_{n}
,
it holds that
n\geq N_{M_{n}}
.
Since, by assumption, we also have
n\geq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
,
Lemma
37

implies that, with probability at least
1-\frac{3}{M_{n}}
,
we have

\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n})\big|_{M_{n}}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n}^{2})\big|_{M_{n}}
.
In other words,
for every
g\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n})
,
there exists
f\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n}^{2})

with
f(X_{\leq M_{n}})=g(X_{\leq M_{n}})
.

Also, since
n\geq N_{M_{n}}\geq M_{n}
,
Lemma
35
(applied to
M_{n}
)
implies that, with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{M_{n}}
,
every
f,g\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n})

with
f(X_{\leq M_{n}})=g(X_{\leq M_{n}})

(hence
P_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}(f\neq g)=0
)
have

P_{n}(f\neq g)\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}
.

By the union bound, the above two events occur simultaneously
with probability at least
1-\frac{4}{M_{n}}
.
Supposing this occurs,
we have that for every
g\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\epsilon_{n})
,
there exists
f\in\bar{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(c_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\epsilon_{n}^{2})

with
P_{n}(f\neq g)\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}
.
Since
n\geq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})
(hence
\epsilon_{n}\leq 1
)
and
n\geq N_{M_{n}}\geq M_{n}
,
and since
c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\geq 1
,
we have
\epsilon_{n}^{2}\leq\epsilon_{n}\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}
.
Thus, on the above event,

\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}
.

To conclude, we note that if either of the above events fails,
we still have
\bar{\sigma}_{n}^{2}\leq 1
(again due to
\epsilon_{n}\leq 1
),
and therefore

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n}\right]\leq c_{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\epsilon_{{\scriptscriptstyle M_{n}}}+\frac{4}{M_{n}}
.

We are now ready to present the proof of Lemma
25
, establishing that the learning rule
\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}
based on
\mathrm{TERM}

achieves excess error that is

o(n^{-1/2})
.

Proof of Lemma
25

We consider the learning rule

\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}
as defined in (
30
).
Let
P
be any distribution that is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathbb{G}
.
We first address the trivial case of
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})=0
.
In this case, note that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the set
\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})
has size at most one.
Moreover, the assumption that
P
is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathbb{G}
implies that,
with probability one,

\exists g^{\star}\in\mathbb{G}(X_{\leq n+1})

such that the value
y_{n+1}=g^{\star}(X_{n+1})

satisfies
\mathbf{P}(y_{n+1}\neq Y_{n+1}|X_{n+1})=\mathbf{P}(h^{\star}_{P}(X_{n+1})\neq Y_{n+1}|X_{n+1})
.
In particular, these two observations together imply that,
with probability one,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n},X_{n+1})\neq Y_{n+1}\middle|X_{\leq n+1},Y_{\leq n}\right)=\mathbf{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{P}(X_{n+1})\neq Y_{n+1}\middle|X_{n+1}\right).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n})\right)\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n},X_{n+1})\neq Y_{n+1}\middle|X_{\leq n+1},Y_{\leq n}\right)-\mathbf{P}\!\left(h^{\star}_{P}(X_{n+1})\neq Y_{n+1}\middle|X_{n+1}\right)\right]=0.

Thus, defining
\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)=0
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

completes the proof for this case.

To address the remaining case, suppose
1\leq\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})<\infty
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)=2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]}\right)}.

By Lemma
34
,
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}}_{n}(X_{\leq n},Y_{\leq n}))\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star}_{P})\leq\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P).

It remains only to argue that
\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)\leq c\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}}
(for a universal constant
c
)
and
\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)=o(n^{-1/2})
.
Since
0\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]\leq 1
,
monotonicity of
x\mapsto x\log(1/x)
for
x\geq 0
implies

\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)\leq 2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}{n}}
(recalling that
\log(x)=\ln(\max\{x,e\})
),
so that
c=2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}
suffices for the first claim.
Moreover,
by Lemma
38
,
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]=0
,
and since
\sqrt{x\log(1/x)}\to 0
as
x\to 0
,
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{1/2}\cdot\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)=2c_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\sqrt{\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G})}\lim_{n\to\infty}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{n+1}\right]}\right)}=0,

so that we indeed have
\phi(n;\mathbb{G},P)=o(n^{-1/2})
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
25
.

9.2
Proof of Theorem
24
:
o(n^{-1/2})
Rates

We are now ready for the proof of Theorem
24
,
which combines the above analysis of partial concept classes
with the strategy outlined in the paragraphs
following the statement of the theorem above.

Proof of Theorem
24

We begin by describing the learning algorithm.
Let
P
be any distribution on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
and let
S_{n}=\{(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})\}\sim P^{n}
be the i.i.d. training data input to the learning algorithm.
For simplicity, suppose
n
is an integer multiple of
4
; otherwise, replace
n
with
4\lfloor n/4\rfloor
in the description and analysis below (supposing
n\geq 4
).
We first segment the data into chunks of size
n/4
:
for
k\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, let

S_{n}^{k}=\{(X_{1+kn/4},Y_{1+kn/4}),\ldots,(X_{(k+1)n/4},Y_{(k+1)n/4})\}.

Recall that we say a distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}

is
Bayes-realizable
with respect to
\mathcal{H}
if

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
,
where
h
on the right hand side ranges over all measurable functions

\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
.
The algorithm has two main components.
The first component is designed to handle the case that
P
is
not
Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.
In this case, since
\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)<\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
,
it is possible to converge to a strictly
negative
excess error rate

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h})-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

if we employ a learning algorithm guaranteed to converge to the

Bayes error

\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
Specifically, let
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
be the classifier
returned by a universally Bayes-consistent learning algorithm
with training set
S_{n}^{0}
(as in Lemma
14
).
Thus, we have the property that

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})]\to\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

for all distributions
P
(where the
h
in the infimum ranges over all measurable
h:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
).
The intention is that, once we also specify a predictor
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
below,
which is guaranteed to have the required rate when
P
is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
,
we can merely select between these two functions
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
,
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
in the end to always guarantee the required rate for the excess error rate
(indeed, the rate we actually achieve may become negative in the case
P
is not Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
).

Next we describe the second component, defining the aforementioned predictor
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
suited to the case that
P
is Bayes-realizable w.r.t.
\mathcal{H}
.
For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
,
let us further segment the sequence
S_{n}^{0}
into
batches

B^{b}_{i}
of size
b
:
let
I_{b}:=\{1,\ldots,\lfloor n/(4b)\rfloor\}
,
and for each
i\in I_{b}
define

B^{b}_{i}=\left\{(X_{1+(i-1)b},Y_{1+(i-1)b}),\ldots,(X_{ib},Y_{ib})\right\}.

For each
i\in I_{b}
,
for each
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{b})\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,
define

B^{b}_{i}(\mathbf{y})=\{(X_{1+(i-1)b},y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{ib},y_{b})\},

that is,
B^{b}_{i}
, but with the
Y
labels replaced by
y
labels.
Let
k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}
and
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{VCL}}^{b,k}
be as in Lemma
12
(for the class
\mathcal{H}
),
and define
k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}=k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B^{b}_{i}(\mathbf{y}))

and
f^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}=\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{VCL}}^{b,k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}(B^{b}_{i}(\mathbf{y}))
.
Recall (from Lemma
12
)
that
k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
is a (
B^{b}_{i}
-dependent) value in
\mathbb{N}

and

f^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
is a (
B^{b}_{i}
-dependent)
function
\mathcal{X}^{k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}\to\{0,1\}^{k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}
.
Additionally define a function

G^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}:\bigcup_{t=1}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{t}\to\{0,1\}

defined by the property that,

\forall t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\forall(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
,

G^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{t},y_{t})=1
iff both of the following hold:

\displaystyle 1.~~

\displaystyle\forall i,j\leq t\text{ with }i\neq j,\text{ if }x_{i}=x_{j}\text{ then }y_{i}=y_{j},

\displaystyle 2.~~

\displaystyle\forall\text{ distinct }x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}}\in\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\},f^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}})\neq(y_{i_{1}},\ldots,y_{i_{k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}}).

In particular, note that
G^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{t},y_{t})=1
iff every distinct
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
have
G^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}(x_{i_{1}},y_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{1}},y_{i_{k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}})=1
.
The interpretation is that each
f^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
defines a set of
forbidden patterns
,
and hence defines a partial concept class
\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}

of all (finite-support) partial concepts which avoid the forbidden patterns.
Formally,
\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
is comprised of all partial concepts
g:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1,*\}
with finite support

\mathrm{supp}(g):=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:g(x)\neq*\}

such that
\forall t
,
\forall\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\}\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(g)
,

G^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}(x_{1},g(x_{1}),\ldots,x_{t},g(x_{t}))=1
.

For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
and
i\in I_{b}
,
define a function
G_{b,i}:\bigcup_{t=1}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{t}\to\{0,1\}

defined by the property that,

\forall t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\forall(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{t},y_{t})\in\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}
,

G_{b,i}(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{t},y_{t})=1\text{ iff }\exists\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}\text{ s.t. }G^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}(x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{t},y_{t})=1.

The function
G_{b,i}
thus specifies a partial concept class

\mathbb{G}_{b,i}:=\bigcup_{\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}}\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
:
the set of all finite-support partial concepts
g
for which
at least one of the forbidden-pattern functions
f^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}

does not forbid any of the patterns in its support.
Moreover, since each
k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}\leq b
(by its definition in Lemma
12
), and
f^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
forbids at least one pattern on any
distinct
k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
points
x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}}}
,
each
\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
has
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i})\leq b-1
.
To bound
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G}_{b,i})
, suppose there is a set

\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\}
of size

t\geq b-1
shattered by
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
.
By Sauer’s lemma
(
Sauer 1972
;
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
)
,
each
\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
has at most

\left(\frac{et}{b-1}\right)^{b-1}

realizable classifications of
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\}
.
Since each of the
2^{t}
classifications of
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\}

realizable by
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
is realizable by at least
one
\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}}_{b,i}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{b}
,
we have
2^{t}\leq 2^{b}\left(\frac{et}{b-1}\right)^{b-1}
,
which, by Lemma 4.6 of
Vidyasagar 2003
,
implies

\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G}_{b,i})\leq 2b+2(b-1)\log_{2}(e)\leq 5b
.

Next we use
S_{n}^{1}
to select an appropriate batch size
\hat{b}_{n}
as follows.
For each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
and
i\in I_{b}
,
recall that we define

\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})=\left\{(g(X_{1+n/4}),\ldots,g(X_{n/2})):g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}\right\}\cap\{0,1\}^{n/4},

the set of
total
classifications of
X_{1+n/4},\ldots,X_{n/2}
realizable by
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
.
For each
b\!\in\!\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
, define
\mathrm{GoodI}(b)
as the set of all
i\in I_{b}
such that,

\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})
is non-empty, and

\forall b^{\prime}>b
with
b^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
,
\forall i^{\prime}\in I_{b^{\prime}}
for which
\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})
is non-empty,

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n/4})\\
\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})\end{subarray}}\frac{4}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n/4}\mathbbold{1}[y_{t}\neq Y_{t+n/4}]\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n/4})\\
\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})\end{subarray}}\frac{4}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n/4}\mathbbold{1}[y_{t}\neq Y_{t+n/4}]+8\sqrt{\frac{b^{\prime}\log(n)}{n}}.

Define
\hat{b}_{n}
to be the minimum
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
such that
|\mathrm{GoodI}(b)|\geq\frac{9}{10}|I_{b}|
,
if such a
b
exists,
and otherwise simply define
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
and in this case the algorithm returns this classifier
\hat{h}_{n}
.

Supposing
\hat{b}_{n}
exists,
the interpretation is that, for
b=\hat{b}_{n}
, at least
\frac{9}{10}
of the partial concept classes
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
,
i\in I_{b}
, are not verifiably worse
(in their ability to fit the data
S_{n}^{1}
) than some other partial concept class
\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}
with
b^{\prime}>b
,
where this verification comes via a uniform convergence bound
we establish below
(using the fact that
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G}_{b,i})
and
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}})
are both at most
5b^{\prime}
)
holding uniformly over all such comparisons
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n}
.

Based on this choice of
\hat{b}_{n}
,
for each
i\in I_{\hat{b}_{n}}
,
let
\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}
denote the function
\hat{f}_{n/4}^{\mathbb{G}_{\hat{b}_{n},i}}

from Lemma
25
, defined for the partial concept class
\mathbb{G}_{\hat{b}_{n},i}
.
To define
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
,
we will use
S_{n}^{2}
as the training set for the learning
algorithms
\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i},i\in I_{\hat{b}_{n}}
,
and aggregate the resulting predictors by a majority vote.
Formally, for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we define

\hat{h}_{n}^{1}(x)=\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\sum_{i\in I_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},x)\geq\frac{1}{2}|I_{\hat{b}_{n}}|\right],

the majority vote of the
\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},x)
predictions.

To complete the definition of the algorithm, we define
\hat{h}_{n}
by selecting one of
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
or
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
using the remaining data
S_{n}^{3}
, as follows.
If

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{1}\right)\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{0}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{32\ln(n)}{n}},

define
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
,
and otherwise define
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
.
In either case, the algorithm returns the classifier
\hat{h}_{n}
.
The algorithm is summarized in Figure
2
.
In particular, we note that the algorithm may be expressed
as a universally measurable function
f_{n}:(\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\})^{n}\times\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}

mapping
S_{n}
and a test point
X
to a prediction
\{0,1\}
,
since evaluation of
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}(x)
ultimately
requires only the use of
the universally measurable functions

B\mapsto k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B)
and
(B,x_{1},\ldots,x_{k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B)})\mapsto\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{VCL}}^{b,k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B)}(B,x_{1},\ldots,x_{k^{b}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B)})
,
and
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}(x)
is a universally measurable function of

(S_{n}^{0},x)
.

1. Let
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
be returned by a universally Bayes-consistent learner trained on
S_{n}^{0}

2. Let
\hat{b}_{n}=\min\!\left\{b\leq n/4:|\mathrm{GoodI}(b)|\geq\frac{9}{10}|I_{b}|\right\}

(if
\hat{b}_{n}
does not exist, return
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
)

3. For each
i\leq n/(4\hat{b}_{n})
, let
\hat{f}^{i}_{n/4}(S_{n}^{2})=\hat{f}_{n/4}^{\mathbb{G}_{\hat{b}_{n},i}}(S_{n}^{2})
(from Lemma
25
)

4. Let
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}=\mathrm{Majority}\!\left(\hat{f}^{i}_{n/4}(S_{n}^{2}):i\leq n/(4\hat{b}_{n})\right)

5. If
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\sqrt{\frac{32\ln(n)}{n}}
, return
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
, else return
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}

Figure 2:
Algorithm achieving
o(n^{-1/2})
rate for classes
\mathcal{H}
with no infinite VCL tree.

With the definition of the returned classifier
\hat{h}_{n}
now complete,
we proceed to prove that it satisfies the required rate
o(n^{-1/2})
.
Let
E_{1}
denote the event that, either
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is undefined
(due to the early termination event in the algorithm, when the criterion for
\hat{b}_{n}
cannot be satisfied)
or else

\forall j\in\{0,1\},\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{j})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{j})\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(n)}{n}}.

Since
S_{n}^{3}
is independent of
(S_{n}^{0},S_{n}^{1},S_{n}^{2})
(from which
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
and
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
are derived),
Hoeffding’s inequality (applied under the conditional distribution given
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
and
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
)
and a union bound, with the law of total probability, imply that
E_{1}
holds with probability at least
1-\frac{4}{n}
.

We first consider the case where
P
is
not
Bayes-realizable
with respect to
\mathcal{H}
: that is,

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
.
In this case, let
\epsilon>0
be such that
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)>\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)+\epsilon
.
Since the
o(n^{-1/2})
rate is asymptotic in nature,
we may focus on the case that
n\geq\frac{2^{10}}{\epsilon^{2}}\ln\!\left(\frac{2^{9}}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
,
so that
\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(n)}{n}}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{16}
.
On the event
E_{1}
(and the event that
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is defined), if additionally,

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})<\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})>\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}
,
then

\displaystyle\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{1}\right)-\frac{\epsilon}{16}>\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{3\epsilon}{16}

\displaystyle>\mathrm{er}_{P}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{0}\right)+\frac{5\epsilon}{16}\geq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{0}\right)+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\geq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}\!\left(\hat{h}_{n}^{0}\right)+\sqrt{\frac{32\ln(n)}{n}},

so that the algorithm chooses
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
.
On the other hand, if

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})<\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}

and
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}
,
then regardless of whether
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
or
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
,
we have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}
.
Moreover, if
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is not defined, then the algorithm chooses
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
.
Thus, in any case, on the event
E_{1}
, if
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})<\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}
,
we always have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}
.
Since we always have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq 1
, this implies

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]\leq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{8}+\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})\geq\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)+\left(1-\mathbf{P}(E_{1})\right).

(62)

Recall we have
1-\mathbf{P}(E_{1})\leq\frac{4}{n}
.
Moreover, noting that
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})-\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\geq 0
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})\!\geq\!\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)\!\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})-\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\!>\!\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)\!\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})-\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\right]

where the last inequality follows from Markov’s inequality,
Since the Bayes consistency property of
\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
guarantees

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})-\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)\right]=0
,
and clearly
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{4}{n}=0
,
we have for all sufficiently large
n

the right hand side of (
62
) is at most

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)-\frac{\epsilon}{16}
.
Thus, for all sufficiently large
n
,

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)<0=o\!\left(n^{-1/2}\right).

To complete the proof, we now turn to the remaining case, in which

P
is Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
:
that is,

\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
,
and henceforth assume this to be the case.
Since
\mathcal{H}
does not shatter an infinite VCL tree,
Lemmas
16
,
17
, and
18

imply that
there exists a measurable function
h^{\star}:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
with

\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)

and
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=0
,
where
P_{X}
denotes the marginal of
P
on
\mathcal{X}
.
In particular, let
E_{2}
denote the event that
\forall t\leq n/2
,
\mathbf{P}(Y_{t}=h^{\star}(X_{t})|X_{t})\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
As is well known, the fact that
\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})=\inf_{h}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)
implies
E_{2}
is satisfied with probability one.
Moreover, note that because
\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}P_{X}(x:h(x)\neq h^{\star}(x))=0
,
letting
P_{h^{\star}}
denote the distribution of
(X,h^{\star}(X))
, for
X\sim P_{X}
,
we have that
P_{h^{\star}}
is realizable with respect to
\mathcal{H}
.

Let
b^{*}:=b^{*}_{99/100}\in\mathbb{N}
be the value defined in
Lemma
12

under the realizable distribution
P_{h^{\star}}
(and
\gamma=\frac{99}{100}
).
For the remainder of the proof, suppose
n\geq 4b^{*}
(w.l.o.g., since the
o(n^{-1/2})
claim is asymptotic in nature).
We first argue that
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}
with high probability.
For each
i\in I_{b^{*}}
,
let
\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}=(h^{\star}(X_{1+(i-1)b^{*}}),\ldots,h^{\star}(X_{ib^{*}}))
.
Then note that, for each such
i
,
the distribution
P_{h^{\star}}
will be Bayes-realizable
with respect to the partial concept class
\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}

if, for
k=k^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}
,

\forall t\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=P_{X}^{t}\!\left((x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})\in\mathcal{X}^{t}:G^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}(x_{1},h^{\star}(x_{1}),\ldots,x_{t},h^{\star}(x_{t}))=0\right)

\displaystyle=P_{X}^{t}\Big((x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})\in\mathcal{X}^{t}:\exists\text{ distinct }x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k}}\in\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\}\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle{\hskip 125.19212pt}f^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k}})=(h^{\star}(x_{i_{1}}),\ldots,h^{\star}(x_{i_{k}}))\Big),

where the last equality is by definition of
G^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}
.
Since there are only finitely many choices of distinct

i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
,
by the union bound the quantity above is
0
if

P_{X}^{k}\!\left((x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{X}^{k}:f^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=(h^{\star}(x_{1}),\ldots,h^{\star}(x_{k}))\right)=0.

(63)

Recalling that
k=k^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}=k^{b^{*}}_{\mathrm{VCL}}(B^{b^{*}}_{i}(\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}))

and
f^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}=\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{VCL}}^{b^{*},k}(B^{b^{*}}_{i}(\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}))
,
and noting that
B^{b^{*}}_{i}(\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i})\sim P_{h^{\star}}^{b^{*}}
,
and that
(X,Y)\sim P_{h^{\star}}
has
Y=h^{\star}(X)
(by definition),
the defining property of
b^{*}
from Lemma
12

implies (
63
) holds with probability
greater than
\frac{99}{100}
.
Altogether, we have

\mathbf{P}\!\left(P_{h^{\star}}\text{ is Bayes-realizable with respect to }\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}\right)>\frac{99}{100}.

Based on this fact,
we will argue that
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}

with high probability. Let
E^{\prime}_{3}
denote the event that the set

I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}=\left\{i\in I_{b^{*}}:P_{h^{\star}}\text{ is Bayes-realizable with respect to }\mathbb{G}^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}_{b^{*},i}\right\}

satisfies
|I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}|\geq\frac{9}{10}|I_{b^{*}}|
.
Noting that the data sets
B^{b^{*}}_{i}(\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i})
,
i\in I_{b^{*}}
, are independent,
a Chernoff bound implies that
the event
E^{\prime}_{3}
has probability at least
1-e^{-C_{1}n}
,
where
C_{1}=\frac{99}{100}\frac{1}{11^{2}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{8b^{*}}
.
Thus, to show
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}
, on this event
E^{\prime}_{3}

it suffices to argue that
\mathrm{GoodI}(b^{*})\supseteq I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
.
Additionally, denote by
E^{\prime\prime}_{3}
the event that
every
i\in I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
has

(h^{\star}(X_{1+n/4}),\ldots,h^{\star}(X_{n/2}))

\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i}^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}(S_{n}^{1})
.
Note that, conditional on
S_{n}^{0}
, for
i\in I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
,
the definitions of
P_{h^{\star}}
and Bayes-realizability of

P_{h^{\star}}
with respect to
\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i}^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}

imply that
with conditional probability one given
S_{n}^{0}

we have
(h^{\star}(X_{1+n/4}),\ldots,h^{\star}(X_{n/2}))\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i}^{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}}(S_{n}^{1})
.
Therefore, by the union bound and law of total probability,

E^{\prime\prime}_{3}
has probability one.
Denote by
E_{3}=E^{\prime}_{3}\cap E^{\prime\prime}_{3}
,
and note that, by the union bound,

E_{3}
has probability at least
1-e^{-C_{1}n}
.
Also note that one implication of
E_{3}
is that
every
i\in I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
has
\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i}(S_{n}^{1})\neq\emptyset

(one of the properties necessary for establishing
\mathrm{GoodI}(b^{*})\supseteq I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
).

As in Section
9.1.2
,
it will simplify the discussion to introduce convenient notation in discussing the partial concept classes
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
,
specifically in the context of the projections

\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})
to the data set
S_{n}^{1}
.
For any
g\in\{0,1\}^{n/4}
,
to make explicit the correspondence of entries of
g

to the variables
X_{t+n/4}
in
S_{n}^{1}
,
we denote the
t^{\mathrm{th}}
entry of
g
by
g(X_{t+n/4})
(for
t\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
):
that is,
g=:(g(X_{1+n/4}),\ldots,g(X_{n/2}))
.
Additionally,
denote by
\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n/4}\mathbbold{1}[g(X_{t+n/4})\neq Y_{t+n/4}]

(the empirical error rate of
g
on
S_{n}^{1}
),
and denote by
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g)=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n/4}\mathbf{P}(Y_{t+n/4}\neq g(X_{t+n/4})|X_{t+n/4})

(the semi-empirical error rate of
g
on
S_{n}^{1}
).
Also extend this notation to
h^{\star}
,
defining
\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(h^{\star})=\frac{4}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n/4}\mathbf{P}(Y_{t+n/4}\neq h^{\star}(X_{t+n/4})|X_{t+n/4})
.
In particular, in this notation,
for each
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
,
the set
\mathrm{GoodI}(b)
is comprised of all
i\in I_{b}

such that
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})
is non-empty,
and
\forall b^{\prime}>b
with
b^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
,

\forall i^{\prime}\in I_{b^{\prime}}
,
for which
\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})
is non-empty,

\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)\leq\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)+8\sqrt{\frac{b^{\prime}\log(n)}{n}}.

For every
b\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
and
i\in I_{b}
,
applying Hoeffding’s inequality under the conditional distribution
given
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n/2}\}
implies that,
for each
g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})
,
with conditional probability at least
1-2n^{-8b}
,

\left|\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g)\right|\leq 4\sqrt{\frac{b\log(n)}{n}}.

(64)

Since
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G}_{b,i})\leq 5b
,
Sauer’s lemma
(
Sauer 1972
;
Vapnik and Chervonenkis 1971
)
implies that

|\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})|\leq n^{5b}
,
so that the union bound,
together with the law of total probability,
imply that with probability at least
1-2n^{-3b}
,
the inequality (
64
) holds
simultaneously for every
g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})
.
Let
E_{4}
denote the event
that (
64
) holds simultaneously for all

b\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
,

i\in I_{b}
,
and
g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})
.
Noting that

\sum_{b\leq n/4}\sum_{i\leq n/(4b)}2n^{-3b}\leq\frac{1}{n}
,
the union bound implies
E_{4}
has probability at least

1-\frac{1}{n}
.

On the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
, for all
i\in I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
, it holds that

\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)-4\sqrt{\frac{b^{*}\log(n)}{n}}\leq\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i}(S_{n}^{1})}\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g)\leq\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(h^{\star})=\min_{g\in\{0,1\}^{n/4}}\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g),

(65)

where the first inequality is due to
E_{4}
,
the second inequality is due to
i\in I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}

and the event
E_{3}
,
and the last equality is due to the event
E_{2}
.
Moreover, on the event
E_{4}
,
any
b^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
and
i^{\prime}\in I_{b^{\prime}}
with
\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})\neq\emptyset
have

\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)+4\sqrt{\frac{b^{\prime}\log(n)}{n}}\geq\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})}\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g)\geq\min_{g\in\{0,1\}^{n/4}}\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g).

Together, these facts imply that, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,
every
i\in I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
, every
b^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n/4\}
with
b^{\prime}>b^{*}
, and every
i^{\prime}\in I_{b^{\prime}}

with
\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})\neq\emptyset
satisfy

\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)\leq\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{\prime},i^{\prime}}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)+8\sqrt{\frac{b^{\prime}\log(n)}{n}}.

Thus, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,
we have
\mathrm{GoodI}(b^{*})\supseteq I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}

and
|\mathrm{GoodI}(b^{*})|\geq|I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}|\geq\frac{9}{10}|I_{b^{*}}|
,
so that
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}
.
In particular, on this event, the function
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is defined.

Having established that
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}
,
we next need to argue that any “bad”
b<b^{*}

will
not
be selected as
\hat{b}_{n}
.
Recall our definition of
Bayes-realizable

for partial concept classes, introduced in Section
9.1
.
Let
\mathrm{PrbBadB}
denote the set of all
b<b^{*}
for which

\mathbf{P}\!\left(P\text{ is Bayes-realizable with respect to }\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\right)<\frac{7}{10}.

We aim to show that
\hat{b}_{n}\notin\mathrm{PrbBadB}
.
If
\mathrm{PrbBadB}=\emptyset
, this trivially holds;
we focus the next portion of the proof on the nontrivial
case that
\mathrm{PrbBadB}\neq\emptyset
.

Let
S^{\prime}_{<\infty}=\{(X^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}),(X^{\prime}_{2},Y^{\prime}_{2}),\ldots\}
be an infinite sequence of independent
P
-distributed random variables
(with
S^{\prime}_{<\infty}
independent of
S_{n}^{0}
),
and for any
m\in\mathbb{N}

denote by
S^{\prime}_{m}=\{(X^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(X^{\prime}_{m},Y^{\prime}_{m})\}
.
For any
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
, by definition,
if
P
is
not
Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathbb{G}_{b,1}
,
then
\exists m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\in\mathbb{N}

and
\Delta(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})>0
(both independent of
S^{\prime}_{<\infty}
)
such that,
denoting by
S^{\prime}_{b,1}=S^{\prime}_{m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})}
(which is conditionally distributed as
P^{m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})}
given
\mathbb{G}_{b,1}
),

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\forall(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})})\in\mathbb{G}_{b,1}(S^{\prime}_{b,1}),\exists t\leq m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\text{ s.t. }\mathbf{P}(Y^{\prime}_{t}=y_{t}|X^{\prime}_{t})<\frac{1}{2}\middle|\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\right)>\Delta(\mathbb{G}_{b,1}).

In particular, if we define (for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in I_{b}
)

\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,i},S^{\prime}_{m})=\min_{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S^{\prime}_{m})}\max_{1\leq t\leq m}\mathbf{P}(Y^{\prime}_{t}\neq y_{t}|X^{\prime}_{t})-\frac{1}{2},

or
\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,i},S^{\prime}_{m})=1
if
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S^{\prime}_{m})=\emptyset
,
then since
\mathbb{G}_{b,1}(S^{\prime}_{b,1})
has finite size,
the above can equivalently be stated as

\mathbf{P}\Big(\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1},S^{\prime}_{b,1})>0\Big|\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)>\Delta(\mathbb{G}_{b,1}).

Due to the strict inequality in the event
\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1},S^{\prime}_{b,1})>0
,
this further implies
\exists\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})>0

such that

\mathbf{P}\Big(\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1},S^{\prime}_{b,1})>\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\Big|\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)>\frac{\Delta(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})}{2}.

For each
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,
let
m^{*}_{b}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\epsilon^{*}_{b}\in(0,1]
, and
\Delta^{*}_{b}\in(0,1]

be such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(m^{*}_{b}\geq m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\Big|P\text{ not Bayes-realizable wrt }\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)>\frac{17}{18}

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\geq\epsilon^{*}_{b}\Big|P\text{ not Bayes-realizable wrt }\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)>\frac{17}{18}

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\Delta(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\geq\Delta^{*}_{b}\Big|P\text{ not Bayes-realizable wrt }\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)>\frac{17}{18},

so that, by the union bound,

\mathbf{P}\Big(m^{*}_{b}\!\geq\!m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\text{ and }\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\!\geq\!\epsilon^{*}_{b}\text{ and }\Delta(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\!\geq\!\Delta^{*}_{b}\Big|P\text{ not Bayes-realizable wrt }\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)>\frac{5}{6}.

Let

m^{*}=\max\limits_{b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}}m^{*}_{b},~~~~~\epsilon^{*}=\min\limits_{b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}}\epsilon^{*}_{b},~~~~\text{ and }~\Delta^{*}=\min\limits_{b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}}\Delta^{*}_{b},

and note that since
\mathrm{PrbBadB}
is finite (being a subset of
\{1,\ldots,b^{*}-1\}
), we have

m^{*}<\infty
,
\epsilon^{*}>0
, and
\Delta^{*}>0
.
By the above properties, for any
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(m^{*}\!\geq\!m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\text{ and }\mathbf{P}\Big(\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1},S^{\prime}_{b,1})\!>\!\epsilon^{*}\Big|\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)\!>\!\frac{\Delta^{*}}{2}\middle|P\text{ not Bayes-realizable wrt }\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\!\right)\!>\!\frac{5}{6}.

Also note that
\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1},S^{\prime}_{m})
is non-decreasing in
m
,
so that the above implies

\displaystyle\frac{5}{6}

\displaystyle<\mathbf{P}\!\left(m^{*}\!\geq\!m(\mathbb{G}_{b,1})\text{ and }\mathbf{P}\Big(\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1},S^{\prime}_{m^{*}})\!>\!\epsilon^{*}\Big|\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)\!>\!\frac{\Delta^{*}}{2}\middle|P\text{ not Bayes-realizable wrt }\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\!\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathbf{P}\Big(\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,1},S^{\prime}_{m^{*}})>\epsilon^{*}\Big|\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\Big)>\frac{\Delta^{*}}{2}\middle|P\text{ not Bayes-realizable wrt }\mathbb{G}_{b,1}\!\right).

Since (by definition) any
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
has probability greater than
\frac{3}{10}
that
P
is not Bayes-realizable with respect to
\mathbb{G}_{b,1}
,
and since the data sets
B^{b}_{i}
,
i\in I_{b}
,
are identically distributed,
we can further conclude that

\forall b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,
\forall i\in I_{b}
,

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\mathbf{P}\Big(\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,i},S^{\prime}_{m^{*}})>\epsilon^{*}\Big|\mathbb{G}_{b,i}\Big)>\frac{\Delta^{*}}{2}\right)>\frac{1}{4}.

For each
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
, letting

J_{b}=\left\{i\in I_{b}:\mathbf{P}\Big(\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,i},S^{\prime}_{m^{*}})>\epsilon^{*}\Big|\mathbb{G}_{b,i}\Big)>\frac{\Delta^{*}}{2}\right\},

since the data sets
B^{b}_{i}
,
i\in I_{b}
,
are independent,
a Chernoff bound implies that,
with probability at least
1-e^{-C_{2}n}
,
where
C_{2}=\frac{1}{64\cdot 25\cdot b^{*}}
,
it holds that
|J_{b}|\geq\frac{1}{5}|I_{b}|
.
Let
E_{5}
denote the event that
|J_{b}|\geq\frac{1}{5}|I_{b}|
holds simultaneously for all
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
.
By the union bound,
E_{5}
has probability at least
1-(b^{*}-1)e^{-C_{2}n}
.

Since the
o\!\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
rate guarantee is asymptotic in nature,
we may focus on the case
n\geq 4m^{*}
; we suppose this to be the case for the remainder of the proof.
Let
t_{0}=n/4
,
and for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor n/(4m^{*})\rfloor\}
,
let
t_{j}=jm^{*}+n/4
,
and for each such
j
, let

S_{m^{*},j}=\left\{(X_{1+t_{j-1}},Y_{1+t_{j-1}}),\ldots,(X_{t_{j}},Y_{t_{j}})\right\}
.
Note that
S_{m^{*},1},\ldots,S_{m^{*},\lfloor n/(4m^{*})\rfloor}

are independent (and independent of
S_{n}^{0}
),
each with distribution the same as that of
S^{\prime}_{m^{*}}
defined above.
Moreover, defining
\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,i},S_{m^{*},j})
as above (i.e., by taking
S^{\prime}_{m^{*}}=S_{m^{*},j}
above),
for any
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
and
i\in I_{b}
,
on the event
E_{2}
,
if
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})\neq\emptyset
, we have

\displaystyle\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})}\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(h^{\star})

\displaystyle=\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})}\frac{4}{n}\sum_{t=1+n/4}^{n/2}\left(\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{t}\neq g(X_{t})\middle|X_{t}\right)-\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{t}\neq h^{\star}(X_{t})\middle|X_{t}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})}\frac{4}{n}\sum_{t=1+n/4}^{n/2}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{t}\neq g(X_{t})\middle|X_{t}\right)>\frac{1}{2}\right]2\left(\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{t}\neq g(X_{t})\middle|X_{t}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{4}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n/(4m^{*})\rfloor}\!\min_{(y_{1+t_{j-1}},\ldots,y_{t_{j}})\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{m^{*},j})}\sum_{t=1+t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}\!\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{t}\neq y_{t}\middle|X_{t}\right)>\frac{1}{2}\right]2\left(\mathbf{P}\!\left(Y_{t}\neq y_{t}\middle|X_{t}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{4}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n/(4m^{*})\rfloor}2\epsilon^{*}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,i},S^{\prime}_{m^{*},j})>\epsilon^{*}\right].

Given
S_{n}^{0}
,
for any
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
and
i\in J_{b}
,
applying a Chernoff bound under the conditional distribution given
S_{n}^{0}
(from which
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
is derived, independent from
S_{n}^{1}
),
we have that with conditional probability at least
1-e^{-C_{3}n}
, where
C_{3}=\frac{\Delta^{*}}{128m^{*}}
,

\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n/(4m^{*})\rfloor}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\epsilon(\mathbb{G}_{b,i},S^{\prime}_{m^{*},j})>\epsilon^{*}\right]\geq\frac{\Delta^{*}}{4}\left\lfloor\frac{n}{4m^{*}}\right\rfloor.

Letting
E_{6}
denote the event that this inequality is satisfied for every
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
and
i\in J_{b}
,
the union bound and the law of total probability together imply that
E_{6}
holds with probability at least

1-(b^{*}-1)\frac{n}{4}e^{-C_{3}n}
.
Altogether, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{6}
,
each
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
and
i\in J_{b}

with
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})\neq\emptyset

satisfy

\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})}\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g)-\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(h^{\star})\geq\frac{8\epsilon^{*}}{n}\frac{\Delta^{*}}{4}\left\lfloor\frac{n}{4m^{*}}\right\rfloor\geq\frac{\epsilon^{*}\Delta^{*}}{4m^{*}}.

To complete this argument (toward the conclusion that
\hat{b}_{n}\notin\mathrm{PrbBadB}
on the above events),
we combine this with the above guarantees for
b^{*}
from (
65
).
Specifically, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}\cap E_{6}
,
for any
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
and
i\in J_{b}
with
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})\neq\emptyset
,
for any
i^{*}\in I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
, it holds that
\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i^{*}}(S_{n}^{1})\neq\emptyset
(established above) and

\displaystyle\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)+4\sqrt{\frac{b\log(n)}{n}}\geq\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})}\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(g)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\epsilon^{*}\Delta^{*}}{4m^{*}}+\overline{\mathrm{er}}_{n}^{1}(h^{\star})\geq\frac{\epsilon^{*}\Delta^{*}}{4m^{*}}+\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i^{*}}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)-4\sqrt{\frac{b^{*}\log(n)}{n}},

where the first inequality is due to
E_{4}
,
the second inequality is due to
E_{6}
,
and the last inequality is from (
65
).
Since the
o(n^{-1/2})
rate guarantee is asymptotic in nature,
we may suppose for the remainder of the proof that

n>2b^{*}\left(\frac{64m^{*}}{\epsilon^{*}\Delta^{*}}\right)^{2}\log\!\left(b^{*}\left(\frac{64m^{*}}{\epsilon^{*}\Delta^{*}}\right)^{2}\right)
,
so that
\frac{\epsilon^{*}\Delta^{*}}{4m^{*}}>16\sqrt{\frac{b^{*}\log(n)}{n}}
.
In this case, on
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}\cap E_{6}
,
the above inequality implies that,
for any
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
and
i\in J_{b}
with
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})\neq\emptyset
, for any
i^{*}\in I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}
,

\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b,i}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)>\min_{g\in\mathbb{G}_{b^{*},i^{*}}(S_{n}^{1})}\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{1}}(g)+8\sqrt{\frac{b^{*}\log(n)}{n}}.

Since we have shown above that

|I^{h^{\star}}_{b^{*}}|\geq\frac{9}{10}|I_{b^{*}}|>0
on
E_{3}
,
the above implies that on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}\cap E_{6}
,
every
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
and
i\in J_{b}

have
i\notin\mathrm{GoodI}(b)
.
Since the event
E_{5}
guarantees
every
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
has

|J_{b}|\geq\frac{1}{5}|I_{b}|
,
we conclude that, on the event
\bigcap_{j=2}^{6}E_{j}
,
every
b\in\mathrm{PrbBadB}
has
|\mathrm{GoodI}(b)|\leq\frac{4}{5}|I_{b}|<\frac{9}{10}|I_{b}|
,
and hence
\hat{b}_{n}\notin\mathrm{PrbBadB}
.
This concludes the portion of the proof concerned with the
case
\mathrm{PrbBadB}\neq\emptyset
;
for the remainder of the proof, we return to the general case,
allowing
\mathrm{PrbBadB}
either non-empty or empty (in the latter case letting
E_{5}
and
E_{6}
denote vacuous events of probability one).

Since, on the above events,
\hat{b}_{n}\leq b^{*}
and
\hat{b}_{n}\notin\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,
let us consider the complementary set. Consider any
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}
.
By definition, we have that any
i\in I_{b}
has

\mathbf{P}\!\left(P\text{ is Bayes-realizable with respect to }\mathbb{G}_{b,i}\right)\geq\frac{7}{10}
.
Let

I^{*}_{b}=\left\{i\in I_{b}:P\text{ is Bayes-realizable with respect to }\mathbb{G}_{b,i}\right\}.

By Hoeffding’s inequality,
it holds that
|I^{*}_{b}|\geq\frac{3}{5}|I_{b}|

with probability at least
1-e^{-C_{4}n}
,
where
C_{4}=\frac{1}{400\cdot b^{*}}
.
Let
E_{7}
denote the event that this occurs simultaneously for all
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}
.
By the union bound,
E_{7}
holds with probability at least
1-b^{*}\cdot e^{-C_{4}n}
.

Now note that, for each
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,
the set
I^{*}_{b}
and the associated set
\{\mathbb{G}_{b,i}:i\in I^{*}_{b}\}
are determined by
S_{n}^{0}
, and hence are independent of
S_{n}^{2}
.
Thus, given
S_{n}^{0}
,
letting
\hat{f}^{b,i}_{n/4}=\hat{f}^{\mathbb{G}_{b,i}}_{n/4}

defined in Lemma
25
,
by the guarantee from Lemma
25
,
each
i\in I^{*}_{b}
has

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}_{n/4}^{b,i}(S_{n}^{2}))-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\Bigg|S_{n}^{0}\right]\leq\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P),

(66)

where
\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)\leq c\sqrt{\frac{20b}{n}}

(for a universal constant
c
, recalling that
\mathsf{VC}(\mathbb{G}_{b,i})\leq 5b
)
and
\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)=o(n^{-1/2})

(noting that
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
is invariant to
n
, subject to
n\geq 4bi
).

Note that the values
\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)
,
i\in I^{*}_{b}
,
are conditionally i.i.d. given
I^{*}_{b}
.
Denote their common conditional expectation by
\phi(n/4;b,P)
:
namely, for any
i\in I_{b}
,

\phi(n/4;b,P):=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)\middle|i\in I^{*}_{b}\right].

We will next argue that
\phi(n/4;b,P)=o(n^{-1/2})
.
Since the above value is identical for every
i
(which is why the notation
\phi(n/4;b,P)
omits a dependence on
i
),
and since
b\notin\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,
we have (taking
i=1
for simplicity)

\phi(n/4;b,P)\leq\frac{10}{7}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,1},P)\mathbbold{1}\!\left[1\in I^{*}_{b}\right]\right].

Thus, since
n^{1/2}\cdot\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,1},P)\mathbbold{1}\!\left[1\in I^{*}_{b}\right]\leq c\sqrt{20b}
(i.e., it is
bounded
independent of
n
)
for all
n\geq 4b
,
Fatou’s lemma implies that

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}~n^{1/2}\cdot\phi(n/4;b,P)

\displaystyle~\leq~\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}~\frac{10}{7}\mathbf{E}\!\left[n^{1/2}\cdot\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,1},P)\mathbbold{1}\!\left[1\in I^{*}_{b}\right]\right]

\displaystyle~\leq~\frac{10}{7}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathop{\rm limsup}_{n\to\infty}~n^{1/2}\cdot\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,1},P)\mathbbold{1}\!\left[1\in I^{*}_{b}\right]\right]=0,

so that we indeed have
\phi(n/4;b,P)=o(n^{-1/2})
.
Moreover, defining

\phi(n/4;P)=\sum_{b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}}\phi(n/4;b,P),

this immediately implies
\phi(n/4;P)=o(n^{-1/2})
as well.

Also note that for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\hat{h}_{n}^{1}(x)\neq h^{\star}(x)\implies\frac{1}{|I_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},x)\neq h^{\star}(x)\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Additionally,
letting
\alpha=\frac{|I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}{|I_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}
,
as argued above, on the event
\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}
,
we have
\alpha\geq\frac{3}{5}
,
which has the further implication that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|I_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},x)\neq h^{\star}(x)\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{1}{|I_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},x)\neq h^{\star}(x)\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}-(1-\alpha)

\displaystyle\implies\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},x)\neq h^{\star}(x)\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\geq\frac{1}{6}.

Thus, on the event
\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}
,
for
(X,Y)\sim P
(independent of
S_{n}
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{h}_{n}^{1}(X)\neq h^{\star}(X)\right]\left(1-2\mathbf{P}(Y\neq h^{\star}(X)|X)\right)\middle|S_{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\frac{1}{|I_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},X)\neq h^{\star}(X)\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\right]\left(1-2\mathbf{P}(Y\neq h^{\star}(X)|X)\right)\middle|S_{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},X)\neq h^{\star}(X)\right]\geq\frac{1}{6}\right]\left(1-2\mathbf{P}(Y\neq h^{\star}(X)|X)\right)\middle|S_{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 6\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2},X)\neq h^{\star}(X)\right]\left(1-2\mathbf{P}(Y\neq h^{\star}(X)|X)\right)\middle|S_{n}\right]

\displaystyle=6\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}^{i}_{n/4}(S_{n}^{2}))-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right).

Altogether, since the event
\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}

depends only on
S_{n}^{0}
and
S_{n}^{1}
(i.e., it is
\sigma(S_{n}^{0},S_{n}^{1})
-measurable), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right)\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 6\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{\hat{b}_{n}}}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}_{n/4}^{i}(S_{n}^{2}))-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\Bigg|(S_{n}^{0},S_{n}^{1})\right]\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}}\right].

Since, on
\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}
, we have

\hat{b}_{n}\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,
this last expression is at most

\displaystyle 6\mathbf{E}\!\left[\sum_{b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}}\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{b}}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}_{n/4}^{b,i}(S_{n}^{2}))-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\Bigg|(S_{n}^{0},S_{n}^{1})\right]\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle=6\sum_{b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{b}}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{f}_{n/4}^{b,i}(S_{n}^{2}))-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\Bigg|S_{n}^{0}\right]\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 6\sum_{b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{b}}\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}}\right],

(67)

where the second line is based on independence of
\{\hat{f}^{b,i}_{n/4}(S_{n}^{2}):i\in I^{*}_{b}\}
from
S_{n}^{1}
,
and linearity of expectations,
and the final inequality is due to (
66
).
Since the event
\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}

implies
I^{*}_{b}\neq\emptyset
for each
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,
each such
b
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{b}}\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}}\right]\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[I^{*}_{b}\neq\emptyset\right]\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{b}}\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)\right]=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[I^{*}_{b}\!\neq\!\emptyset\right]\mathbf{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{b}}\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)\middle|I^{*}_{b}\right]\right]=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[I^{*}_{b}\!\neq\!\emptyset\right]\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{b}}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)\middle|I^{*}_{b}\right]\right]\!.

Due to the independence of the
\mathbb{G}_{b,i}
sets
(
i\in I_{b}
),
each
i\in I^{*}_{b}
has

\mathbf{E}[\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)|I^{*}_{b}]=\mathbf{E}[\phi(n/4;\mathbb{G}_{b,i},P)|i\in I^{*}_{b}]=\phi(n/4;b,P)
.
Thus, for each
b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}
,
the rightmost expression above equals

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathbbold{1}\!\left[I^{*}_{b}\neq\emptyset\right]\frac{1}{|I^{*}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in I^{*}_{b}}\phi(n/4;b,P)\right]\leq\phi(n/4;b,P).

Plugging back into (
67
),
we conclude that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right)\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}}\right]\leq 6\sum_{b\in\{1,\ldots,b^{*}\}\setminus\mathrm{PrbBadB}}\phi(n/4;b,P)=6\cdot\phi(n/4;P).

It remains to bound
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right]
in terms of
\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right)\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=2}^{7}E_{j}}\right]
.
On the event
E_{1}
, if
\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is defined and
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})
, then

\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})+\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(n)}{n}}\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(n)}{n}}\leq\hat{\mathrm{er}}_{S_{n}^{3}}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})+\sqrt{\frac{8\ln(n)}{n}},

so that the algorithm chooses
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
.
On the other hand, if
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})>\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{0})
,
then regardless of whether
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{0}
or
\hat{h}_{n}=\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
,
we have
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})
.
Thus, since we have argued above that, on the event
E_{2}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
,

\hat{h}_{n}^{1}
is always defined,
we conclude that on the event
\bigcap_{j=1}^{4}E_{j}
,
we have

\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\leq\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})
.
Since
\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\leq 1
,
this implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\inf_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{er}_{P}(h)=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n})\right]-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left(\mathrm{er}_{P}(\hat{h}_{n}^{1})-\mathrm{er}_{P}(h^{\star})\right)\mathbbold{1}_{\bigcap_{j=1}^{7}E_{j}}\right]+\left(1-\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{7}E_{j}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 6\cdot\phi(n/4;P)+\frac{4}{n}+e^{-C_{1}n}\!+\frac{1}{n}+(b^{*}-1)e^{-C_{2}n}\!+(b^{*}-1)\frac{n}{4}e^{-C_{3}n}\!+b^{*}e^{-C_{4}n}=o\!\left(n^{-1/2}\right),

where the second inequality is by the union bound.
This completes the proof.

10
Conclusions and Future Directions

We have established a complete theory of optimal universal rates for binary classification in the agnostic setting.
The main result (Theorem
3
)
establishes a fundamental trichotomy (or tetrachotomy, if we count finite classes).
Finite concept classes
\mathcal{H}
exhibit an optimal rate
e^{-n}
.
Any infinite concept class which does not shatter an infinite Littlestone tree exhibits an optimal rate
e^{-o(n)}
,
while any concept class which shatters an infinite concept class but does not shatter an infinite VCL tree exhibits an optimal rate
o(n^{-1/2})
,
and otherwise the class requires arbitrarily slow rates.
This extends the realizable-case theory of
Bousquet et al. 2021

by removing the realizability assumption on the distribution.

As the realizable-case theory of
Bousquet et al. 2021

has to-date been extended to numerous other learning settings beyond supervised binary classification

(
Kalavasis et al. 2022
;
Hanneke et al. 2022
;
Hanneke et al. 2023
;
Bousquet et al. 2023
;
Attias et al. 2024
;
Hanneke et al. 2024
;
Hanneke et al. 2025
)
,
a natural future direction for the study of universal rates is to pursue analogous extensions of the theory of agnostic universal rates to these settings.
